Table 1
Donor Agreement Review and Grant Setup Controls
1. Q: What is the purpose of a donor agreement review and grant setup SOP?
A: Show answer
The SOP establishes a controlled process for evaluating donor funding instruments before the organization commits to their terms, approving the correct agreement and budget versions, obtaining authorized signatures, configuring grant master data, and transferring enforceable obligations to operational owners. It helps ensure that legal commitments, financial assumptions, donor restrictions, reporting duties, and system configurations remain consistent. The process should prevent premature signing, acceptance of funds, expenditure commitments, and activation when required reviews or approvals remain incomplete. It should also provide traceable evidence of the decision and explain why an opportunity was approved, conditionally approved, held, deferred, or declined. The supplied SOP is explicitly a fictional demonstration: its parties, clauses, approvals, budgets, and evidence records must never be presented as genuine organizational records or authority to proceed.

Study instruction:
- "Explain the control objectives and distinguish an illustrative procedure from an approved operational SOP."
Key point:
- Agreement review and grant setup require documented authorization and evidence.
- The supplied example is fictional and must not be used as a live approval record.
2. Q: Which transactions and lifecycle events should fall within the SOP's scope?
A: Show answer
Scope should cover prospective funding opportunities, draft agreements, award letters, executed agreements, restricted donations, renewals, consortium arrangements, amendments, extensions, and other funding instruments requiring formal review or grant configuration. The scope register should identify the recipient legal entity, funding arrangement, applicable processes, responsible owner, and relevant approval route. The SOP should also identify exclusions so users do not assume that agreement review automatically authorizes journal posting, grant revenue recognition, procurement, payroll, partner management, tax filings, or donor reporting. Those activities remain governed by their respective processes. Where another procedure becomes responsible, the handoff should include the agreement version, relevant clauses, required evidence, owner, and referral reference.

Study instruction:
- "Map a new donor agreement, an amendment, a partner funding agreement, and a grant closeout to the correct process and accountable owner."
Key point:
- Scope defines the SOP's responsibilities and boundaries.
- Downstream activities require controlled handoffs to their own procedures.
3. Q: How should governing law, donor agreements, internal policies, and accounting frameworks be distinguished?
A: Show answer
Applicable legislation and regulatory requirements must be identified first because a contract or internal approval cannot override mandatory law. The executed agreement and its incorporated documents then establish the contractual commitments, subject to applicable law and interpretation. Internal policies, delegated authority, and organizational risk limits govern whether the organization may accept and perform those commitments. Accounting recognition and measurement are separate decisions based on the applicable financial reporting framework and the transaction's actual facts. When requirements conflict, the reviewer should document the conflicting provisions, identify the decision required, obtain qualified legal or technical advice, and keep the matter on hold if it prevents an authorized decision. The hierarchy must be verified for the actual entity and instrument rather than assumed from the fictional example.

Study instruction:
- "Create a source register for one funding instrument and identify which document governs each legal, operational, approval, and accounting question."
Key point:
- Contractual acceptance and accounting treatment are distinct decisions.
- Mandatory legal requirements cannot be waived through internal approval.
Source links:
- Law No. 16 of 2001 on Foundations — Indonesian Legal Information
- Law No. 28 of 2004 amending the Foundations Law — Indonesian Legal Information
4. Q: How should a new donor funding opportunity be registered and classified?
A: Show answer
The grants or agreement lead should create a unique intake ID as soon as the opportunity or instrument is received. Record the receipt date, requesting department, donor or counterparty, recipient entity, instrument type, expected value and currency, decision deadline, owner, and current document status. Identify whether the request concerns a new award, renewal, amendment, extension, consortium agreement, or another funding arrangement. Distinguish verified facts from preliminary statements and assign each missing item an owner and due date. The opportunity should then be routed to the appropriate diligence, legal, program, finance, and management reviewers. Incomplete requests should be returned or held with an explicit reason rather than treated as ready for approval.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a sample intake record and test whether another reviewer could identify the opportunity, its legal entity, its current status, and its next required action."
Key point:
- Every opportunity needs a unique identifier and accountable owner.
- Incomplete information must remain visible until resolved.
5. Q: How should draft agreements, executed agreements, budgets, annexes, and correspondence be controlled?
A: Show answer
Maintain a controlled document inventory showing each record's ID, title, document type, version, receipt date, source, status, custodian, and relationship to the agreement. The inventory should identify the proposed controlling version and confirm that every referenced annex, budget, schedule, amendment, and incorporated guideline is available. Draft, submitted, approved, executed, superseded, and archived versions must remain distinguishable. A revised document should not silently overwrite the version already approved or signed. Compare material versions and record changes, their effects on review conclusions, and any required reapproval. Where a referenced document is missing or two documents conflict, mark the review incomplete and obtain an authorized resolution before the affected commitment proceeds.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a draft and revised agreement and document version IDs, changed clauses, affected budget lines, and the approvals that must be repeated."
Key point:
- The approved and executed document sets must be identifiable and retrievable.
- A material change may invalidate previous approvals or setup checks.
6. Q: Why is donor and counterparty due diligence necessary before agreement approval?
A: Show answer
Due diligence helps the organization assess whether the proposed relationship is legitimate, within its mandate, and manageable under its legal, operational, financial, and integrity requirements. Depending on the arrangement and risk, checks may include legal identity, authority to enter the agreement, beneficial ownership where relevant, sanctions screening, conflicts of interest, fraud indicators, financial capacity, safeguarding exposure, and reputational concerns. The actual checks must follow applicable law, donor conditions, internal policy, and the risk-based review procedure. Record the source, screening date, scope, result, reviewer, and disposition. A search result is not automatically a clearance, and an inconclusive match is not equivalent to a confirmed negative result. Unresolved material concerns should remain on hold until the authorized specialist documents a decision and its basis.

Study instruction:
- "Design a due-diligence checklist that distinguishes verified identity, clear results, possible matches, unresolved questions, and formally approved exceptions."
Key point:
- Screening requires evidence and an authorized disposition.
- Unresolved material risks must not be converted into an assumed clearance.
7. Q: What should happen when donor screening produces a possible sanctions match or incomplete result?
A: Show answer
Preserve the screening result and the information needed for authorized investigation in an appropriately restricted record. The Compliance or Legal specialist should compare relevant identifiers, assess the quality and limitations of the match, obtain necessary authoritative checks, and document the final disposition. Do not describe a possible match as cleared solely because it has not been confirmed. Until required clearance is obtained, block the affected approval, transaction, or activation step according to the organization's risk procedure. Avoid unnecessary disclosure of sensitive investigation details to general grant users. If a legal reporting duty or an urgent protective action may apply, escalate through the designated process without waiting for routine agreement review to finish.

Study instruction:
- "Walk through an inconclusive screening case and specify the evidence, restricted record, decision authority, and release conditions required to clear the hold."
Key point:
- An unresolved screening result is an exception, not a clearance.
- Sensitive investigations require controlled access and authorized decision-making.
8. Q: How should the reviewer perform a clause-by-clause agreement assessment?
A: Show answer
Build a clause matrix linking each material provision to its exact document and clause reference, interpretation question, risk, responsible specialist, required action, evidence, and disposition. Review the purpose and scope of funding, permitted activities and geography, eligible expenditure, budget limits, prior approvals, indirect costs, reporting and audit rights, ownership of assets, treatment of interest and unspent balances, repayment duties, termination, dispute resolution, confidentiality, data protection, and any other provisions relevant to the instrument. These are review prompts, not a universal list of contractual requirements. Every material clause must be reviewed or marked not applicable with a documented rationale and authorized acceptance. Unresolved conflicts, missing clauses, or unacceptable terms must remain visible in the decision pack.

Study instruction:
- "Select a sample agreement and trace every material clause from its wording to the risk assessment, control, owner, and recorded decision."
Key point:
- The clause matrix connects contractual wording to practical controls.
- Not-applicable decisions require a defensible rationale.
9. Q: How should responsibilities be allocated across Program, Grants, Finance, Legal, Operations, and Management?
A: Show answer
Use a responsibility matrix to identify who prepares information, performs specialist review, independently challenges the proposed decision, approves the commitment, signs the agreement, configures the grant, verifies the configuration, and monitors performance. Program owners assess activity feasibility and deliverables. Grants personnel control the document inventory and contractual obligation register. Finance examines budget integrity, financial viability, currency, eligibility, and reporting requirements. Legal and other specialists assess issues within their expertise. Authorized management accepts risks within its delegation, and authorized signatories execute the approved instrument. Setup administrators implement approved attributes, while an independent tester verifies them. The matrix should identify deputies, escalation paths, approval thresholds, and handover expectations instead of relying on job titles or informal practice.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a RACI matrix and identify the accountable owner, reviewer, approver, implementer, and verifier for each gate."
Key point:
- Every material decision and process handoff needs a defined owner.
- Specialist responsibility cannot be replaced by general managerial approval.
10. Q: How should segregation of duties be applied during agreement approval and grant setup?
A: Show answer
Separate preparation, independent review, approval, signature, system implementation, and post-implementation verification wherever practicable. A requester should not be the sole person approving the request, accepting its own unresolved exceptions, or certifying its own grant setup. A person who changes the approved budget should not independently validate that same change without an authorized compensating control. If staffing constraints prevent full separation, document the conflict, assess the risk, obtain approval for the alternative arrangement, and introduce an independent check appropriate to the risk. Review actual system permissions as well as the written responsibility matrix because technical access can undermine formal segregation. Keep the resulting evidence with the relevant approval and configuration record.

Study instruction:
- "Test whether one user could request, approve, sign, configure, and activate the same grant without an independent control."
Key point:
- Segregation must work in actual workflows and system permissions.
- Compensating controls require authorization and evidence.
11. Q: What is the difference between a donor restriction and a donor-imposed condition for accounting purposes?
A: Show answer
A restriction limits or directs the purpose for which transferred resources may be used. A condition, under the framework described in the UN IPSAS Finance Manual, involves a performance obligation together with an enforceable return obligation if the specified requirement is not fulfilled. This distinction matters because a restriction does not automatically create the same accounting consequences as a condition. Review the actual clause, enforceability of any return obligation, related performance requirements, and the applicable accounting framework. Record the contractual restriction in the grant controls regardless of whether it changes revenue recognition. Do not classify an arrangement based only on words such as “restricted grant,” “conditional funding,” or “donation”; the specific terms and applicable reporting requirements must be assessed.

Study instruction:
- "Compare two hypothetical donor clauses and identify which features require further analysis of a restriction, an enforceable condition, or another contractual obligation."
Key point:
- Contractual restrictions and accounting conditions are not interchangeable concepts.
- The precise agreement and applicable accounting guidance determine the treatment.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Revenue from Non-Exchange Transactions
12. Q: Why must legal agreement approval be kept separate from grant revenue recognition?
A: Show answer
Agreement approval establishes that the organization has completed the required decision process and may accept the contractual terms within its authority. Accounting recognition is a separate assessment of the applicable framework, the rights obtained, control of resources, enforceable obligations, conditions, and transaction facts. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual explains that accounting for non-exchange transfers requires assessment of whether an asset or liability arises and whether conditions affect the amount recognized as revenue. The relevant framework for the actual organization must be confirmed rather than automatically applying UN-specific operational policies to another entity. Record the accounting-policy referral, preparer, reviewer, conclusion, and supporting clauses independently from the approval to sign and the technical grant setup.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one agreement from approval to the separate accounting assessment and identify which evidence supports each decision."
Key point:
- Legal acceptance does not automatically determine revenue recognition.
- The applicable accounting framework and actual transaction terms must be verified.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Revenue from Non-Exchange Transactions
13. Q: How should donor funding restrictions and eligible-cost rules be converted into practical controls?
A: Show answer
Translate each applicable clause into a documented eligibility rule identifying the allowed activity, geography, funding period, budget line, required supporting documents, approval conditions, and any donor consent needed before expenditure. Assign an accountable owner for interpreting and maintaining the rule, then communicate it to Program, Procurement, HR, Finance, and other affected teams. Cost eligibility should be tested against both the agreement and the approved budget rather than inferred from the ledger account or availability of funds. Where the agreement is silent or ambiguous, obtain an authorized interpretation before treating a material cost as eligible. The resulting matrix should support transaction review and donor reporting without replacing the specialist decision required for exceptional cases.

Study instruction:
- "Convert three sample clauses into eligibility tests and identify the evidence needed to approve a cost under each clause."
Key point:
- Eligibility depends on contractual terms, timing, activity, budget, and required approval.
- A ledger code or available balance alone does not establish eligibility.
14. Q: How should the proposed grant budget and financial viability be reviewed before acceptance?
A: Show answer
Finance should verify the budget's arithmetic, currency, funding ceiling, expenditure categories, direct and indirect costs, co-financing assumptions, cash-flow needs, delivery resources, and reporting commitments. Compare every material budget line to the agreement's eligible-cost clauses and required donor approvals. Confirm that reallocation limits, personnel or travel caps, indirect-cost rules, and budget period conditions are reflected in the review. Assess whether the organization can deliver the activities within the available resources and whether timing or reimbursement arrangements create liquidity exposure. Record the exact budget version and all changes requested during review. An approval should refer to the same version that passed the checks; any material revision requires impact assessment and appropriate reapproval.

Study instruction:
- "Recalculate a sample budget, reconcile it to the proposed award ceiling, and document how one changed budget line affects approval and eligibility."
Key point:
- Budget approval requires arithmetic, contractual eligibility, and delivery-feasibility checks.
- The approved budget version must be identifiable and controlled.
15. Q: How should grant currency, foreign-exchange assumptions, and budget conversions be controlled?
A: Show answer
Record the agreement currency, approved budget currency, conversion assumptions, exchange-rate source, applicable date, and any contractual reporting convention. Review whether the donor specifies a conversion method or whether organizational accounting and treasury policies establish the method for internal reporting. Keep the original contractual amount separate from converted reporting amounts so a change in exchange rate does not silently alter the agreement ceiling. Confirm that the calculation uses the approved convention and can be reproduced from retained inputs. Where accounting measurement, cash forecasting, and donor reporting use different conversion dates or policies, document each purpose separately. The currency and conversion settings in a grant system must reflect the actual agreement and applicable organizational policy, not a configuration borrowed automatically from another institution.

Study instruction:
- "Recalculate a sample award conversion using its documented rate, date, and rounding rules, then compare it with the recorded budget and reporting values."
Key point:
- Contractual amounts and converted reporting values serve different purposes.
- Currency settings must follow verified terms and approved policy.
16. Q: How should reporting obligations and contractual deadlines be recorded?
A: Show answer
Create an obligation register with a stable obligation ID, agreement clause reference, required deliverable, reporting period, due-date trigger, calculation rule, accountable owner, reviewer, recipient, submission method, evidence requirement, and escalation route. Distinguish donor deadlines from earlier internal deadlines needed for data collection, drafting, review, and approval. Do not infer dates from past practice when the agreement contains an unclear trigger or conflicting provisions. Obtain an authorized interpretation and record it before finalizing the calendar. Track recurring obligations explicitly so that calendar generation does not omit or duplicate periods. The register should also identify conditions that block grant activation, expenditure, or closeout until the required evidence or approval is available.

Study instruction:
- "Select reporting clauses from an agreement and derive the due dates, internal milestones, responsible owners, and supporting evidence for each obligation."
Key point:
- Every material obligation needs a verifiable source and accountable owner.
- Unclear deadlines must be resolved rather than guessed.
17. Q: How should the organization distinguish report preparation, submission, receipt, and acceptance?
A: Show answer
Define separate workflow states for each meaningful event. A report may be prepared, under review, approved, submitted, received, acknowledged, accepted, or returned for correction. These states are not interchangeable: evidence that an email was sent does not establish that the donor received the report, and receipt does not necessarily establish acceptance when the agreement requires formal approval. Store the final submitted version, submission timestamp, recipient or portal reference, receipt evidence, and any donor response. When a report is rejected or challenged, retain the original and revised versions, assign corrective actions, and update the obligation status only when the applicable completion criteria are met. The status model must match the agreement's actual requirements.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one hypothetical report from preparation to donor acceptance and list the evidence needed to advance each workflow state."
Key point:
- Submission, receipt, and acceptance are distinct states.
- A completed status must be supported by the contractually required evidence.
18. Q: What gates should be passed before a donor agreement is approved?
A: Show answer
The decision pack should demonstrate that the controlling document set is complete, required due diligence has been resolved, all material clauses have been assessed, the budget is viable, triggered specialist reviews are complete, material risks have an authorized disposition, and the correct approver has authority for the actual entity and commitment. Present the proposed decision, key assumptions, outstanding conditions, risk treatments, negotiation items, and precise agreement and budget versions. Permitted outcomes may include approval, conditional approval, hold, deferment, negotiation, or decline. A conditional approval must identify the authorized decision, each prerequisite, its owner, due date, evidence, and the action blocked until satisfaction. An approval cannot waive mandatory law or override a required specialist clearance or donor-prohibited term.

Study instruction:
- "Build a decision checklist for a sample award and identify which unresolved issues must prevent approval or remain blocking conditions."
Key point:
- Approval must rely on complete evidence and valid delegation.
- Conditional approval does not automatically authorize signing or activation.
19. Q: How should signatory authority and execution of the agreement be verified?
A: Show answer
Before execution, confirm the recipient legal entity, required signing parties, current written delegation, scope of authority, applicable limits, and any required governing-body or committee approval. A person's job title, prior signature, or email instruction is not sufficient evidence of authority on its own. Match the execution packet against the precise approved agreement and incorporated annexes. Verify that all required parties executed the correct version and preserve the signed originals or controlled authoritative copies. If an agreement is signed by an unauthorized person, contains unexpected alterations, or lacks required signatures, refer it for legal review and do not represent the execution as complete. The governing law and actual legal form of the organization must be confirmed for each instrument.

Study instruction:
- "Compare an approved agreement with the proposed signature packet and identify the documents needed to verify entity identity, authority, signatories, and version matching."
Key point:
- Signature validity depends on authority and the correct executed version.
- Approval to proceed does not automatically confer signing authority.
Source links:
- Law No. 16 of 2001 on Foundations — Indonesian Legal Information
- Law No. 28 of 2004 amending the Foundations Law — Indonesian Legal Information
20. Q: What are conditions precedent, and how should they affect agreement execution and grant activation?
A: Show answer
A condition precedent is a specified requirement that must be satisfied before a contractual or operational step may proceed, according to the actual instrument and applicable law. Examples may include a required donor approval, receipt of a formal document, completion of a specified clearance, or evidence of an agreed prerequisite. Record each condition's source clause, responsible owner, deadline, required evidence, reviewer, and consequences if unmet. Distinguish conditions to signing, conditions to effectiveness, conditions to drawdown, and conditions to expenditure or other activities because they may trigger different restrictions. The status must remain on hold for the affected action until authorized evidence establishes that the condition has been satisfied or that an authorized resolution permits another course.

Study instruction:
- "Extract all conditions precedent from a sample agreement and identify the event each condition blocks, the evidence needed, and the authority that may clear it."
Key point:
- Conditions must be translated into explicit, testable workflow gates.
- An unmet blocking condition prevents the affected action from proceeding.
21. Q: How should grant identifiers and master data be designed before system setup?
A: Show answer
Define controlled identifiers for the agreement, grant, donor, project, fund, budget, cost center, and other organizational dimensions that are actually required by the accounting and reporting systems. Each field needs a documented purpose, format, source, owner, permitted values, uniqueness rule, and maintenance control. The setup should preserve links among related grants, agreements, funding sources, projects, and reporting obligations without duplicating or confusing their meanings. Record the external reference assigned by the donor separately from internal identifiers. Confirm the agreed naming convention and system requirements before generating production records. Do not copy another organization's code types or mandatory field values unless the same configuration and rules genuinely apply to the receiving organization's environment.

Study instruction:
- "Design a grant master-data specification and test uniqueness, permitted values, external references, and links to the approved agreement and budget."
Key point:
- Grant identifiers support traceability across systems and reports.
- Master data must follow the actual organization's approved configuration.
22. Q: How should contractual dates, grant validity dates, and expenditure restrictions be configured?
A: Show answer
Maintain separate attributes for signature dates, agreement effective dates, operational start and end dates, reporting periods, financial closing dates, and any other dates the agreement distinguishes. The start or end of contractual validity does not automatically determine the accounting recognition date or the timing of every permissible transaction. Translate the actual clauses and approved interpretation into system rules for commitments, expenditure, reporting, and closeout. Test boundary dates, amendments, missing dates, and dates that conflict across the agreement and annexes. Any ambiguous effective date or operational period must be resolved by the authorized owner rather than defaulted silently. The system should prevent or flag activity outside the approved rules and retain evidence of any authorized exception.

Study instruction:
- "Test transactions immediately before, on, and after the grant start and end dates and compare system outcomes with the signed terms."
Key point:
- Different dates may serve different contractual and operational purposes.
- Boundary conditions and conflicting dates require explicit verification.
23. Q: How should grant setup be independently tested against the executed agreement and approved budget?
A: Show answer
After the implementer creates or updates the grant, an independent verifier should compare the live or test configuration with the exact executed agreement, approved budget, clause matrix, restriction schedule, and obligation register. Verify identifiers, legal entity, currency, values, dates, eligible activities, funding restrictions, budget controls, reporting attributes, access groups, and any other critical system fields. Perform positive tests to confirm authorized transactions can proceed and negative tests to confirm prohibited or premature actions are blocked or flagged. Document expected and actual results, test data, tester, date, defects, remediation, and retest evidence. A successful save operation is not evidence that the configuration is correct. Critical discrepancies must block release until corrected and independently verified.

Study instruction:
- "Create a setup-to-agreement comparison and execute negative tests for a mismatched budget, incorrect date, missing restriction, and duplicate identifier."
Key point:
- Independent verification detects differences between contract and configuration.
- Critical test failures must prevent release.
24. Q: What should prevent a grant from being activated for operational use?
A: Show answer
Activation should be blocked when the required agreement review, authorized execution, applicable conditions precedent, budget approval, master-data configuration, independent setup testing, or mandatory handover has not passed. The workflow should identify each blocking criterion and the evidence needed to release it. A user should not be able to bypass a failed gate simply by editing a status field or relabeling an exception as complete. Where a controlled exception is permissible, it must be approved by a competent authority, limited to the appropriate scope, and recorded with any remaining restrictions. Preserve the sequence of state changes so a reviewer can reconstruct who authorized activation and on what basis. The actual production release requires real evidence; fictional example records cannot authorize it.

Study instruction:
- "Test whether a grant with an unsigned agreement, unmet blocking condition, or failed setup test can be activated, and document the expected blocked outcome."
Key point:
- Activation must follow passed gates and an authorized release.
- Status changes must not override missing evidence or unresolved blockers.
25. Q: How should agreement requirements be handed over to operational and finance teams?
A: Show answer
Prepare a controlled handover pack containing the executed agreement, incorporated annexes, approved budget, funding restrictions, eligible-cost rules, unresolved risks and conditions, grant identifiers, reporting calendar, required donor approvals, and assigned obligation owners. Include instructions for procurement, payroll, asset management, partner arrangements, tax review, accounting assessment, donor reporting, records custody, and security where relevant. Each receiving owner should acknowledge responsibility for the duties assigned to them and understand the evidence required for completion. Do not assume that distributing a file constitutes a complete handover. Track missing acknowledgments, unassigned obligations, and unresolved conditions to closure. The original grant record should remain accessible as the authoritative reference for subsequent execution and monitoring.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a handover checklist for Program, Finance, Procurement, and donor reporting, and verify that each recipient acknowledges the required controls."
Key point:
- Handover converts contractual obligations into assigned operational responsibilities.
- Receiving owners must understand their duties and evidence requirements.
26. Q: How should the SOP interface with accounting journals, grant recognition, period-end close, and donor reporting?
A: Show answer
The agreement review process should transmit contractual facts and approved setup instructions to each accountable downstream procedure. Accounting must determine recognition, measurement, presentation, and journal implications under the applicable framework. The close process must perform its own reconciliation and period certification. Donor reporting must prepare and submit reports using the correct approved data, deadlines, and acceptance evidence. Procurement, HR, tax, asset management, and partner accounting must apply their own procedures to the relevant contractual terms. Maintain a cross-reference between each obligation, its receiving process, and the referral record. This prevents agreement approval from being mistaken for completion of every downstream financial or operational requirement.

Study instruction:
- "Map the handoffs from agreement approval to accounting assessment, expenditure control, donor reporting, and closeout, identifying the evidence needed at each boundary."
Key point:
- SOP 011 coordinates downstream duties but does not replace their procedures.
- Each receiving process remains responsible for its own decisions and controls.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Revenue from Non-Exchange Transactions
27. Q: How should amendments, extensions, and waivers be processed without losing the original agreement history?
A: Show answer
Register each proposed amendment or extension as a linked lifecycle event with its own ID, receipt date, requested change, affected clauses, supporting correspondence, responsible owner, and status. Compare the new proposal with the controlling agreement and identify effects on budget, eligibility, currency, dates, deliverables, reporting duties, risks, and system configuration. Determine whether donor consent, internal approval, specialist review, or formal signature is required. Preserve the original agreement and all superseded versions while identifying the currently authoritative set of terms. A waiver should never be presumed merely because a donor or staff member discussed it informally. Update the grant calendar, controls, and master data only after the appropriate authorization and effective-date requirements have been verified.

Study instruction:
- "Trace an amendment from intake through comparison, approval, execution, setup changes, retesting, and handover."
Key point:
- Amendments must preserve version lineage and authorization evidence.
- Downstream controls and records must reflect approved changes.
28. Q: How should material changes in budget, scope, dates, or eligibility affect existing approvals?
A: Show answer
Determine whether the change affects the assumptions or conditions under which the agreement and budget were originally approved. Reopen affected reviews, update the clause matrix and risk register, and obtain any donor consent or internal reapproval required by the instrument and delegation. Identify whether a new signature, amendment record, updated budget version, changed system attribute, or revised reporting calendar is necessary. Do not carry forward a prior approval simply because the grant identifier remains unchanged. After the change is authorized and effective, update the system and independently retest the impacted controls. Retain the former values and approval history so auditors and managers can reconstruct the state before and after the change.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate a material budget increase and an extension of the grant period, then identify every review, approval, system attribute, and reporting date that must be reconsidered."
Key point:
- Approval validity depends on the terms and version that were reviewed.
- Material changes require impact analysis and controlled propagation.
29. Q: How should suspension, termination, expiry, and grant closeout be controlled?
A: Show answer
Establish a lifecycle review that identifies the effective date, authority, contractual consequences, activity restrictions, communication duties, remaining funding, open commitments, report deadlines, unspent balance treatment, asset and data requirements, and any repayment obligations. Distinguish suspension from termination and normal expiry because the permitted activities and required actions may differ. Review the agreement, amendments, donor instructions, applicable law, and organizational policies before implementing the decision. Closeout should not be marked complete merely because the grant has reached its end date or new transactions have stopped. Mandatory reports, donor acceptance where required, refunds, open obligations, asset disposition, access removal, and records custody must be resolved or transferred through a valid authorized process.

Study instruction:
- "Build a closeout checklist and test a grant with an outstanding report, unspent balance, open commitment, and unresolved asset obligation."
Key point:
- Expiry does not automatically discharge outstanding responsibilities.
- Closeout requires evidence of each mandatory duty's resolution or authorized transfer.
30. Q: What should a controlled exception register contain?
A: Show answer
The register should identify the exception ID, source record, affected agreement or grant, issue type, description, severity, value where relevant, date identified, accountable owner, deadline, required decision, containment action, root cause, supporting evidence, and closure authority. Examples include missing annexes, conflicting clauses, incomplete diligence, unauthorized changes, budget mismatches, missing donor approval, overdue reports, failed setup tests, and unresolved conditions. Each issue should be assessed for its effect on signing, acceptance, expenditure, reporting, or activation. The system should distinguish an open issue, an approved temporary disposition, completed remediation, independent retest, and formally authorized closure. Moving an item to a later period or changing its status does not prove resolution.

Study instruction:
- "Create an exception register for three different scenarios and specify the immediate containment, decision owner, target date, and evidence required for closure."
Key point:
- Exceptions require ownership, risk assessment, and a documented resolution path.
- Closure requires evidence and the required authority.
31. Q: How should root-cause analysis and corrective action be applied to recurring failures?
A: Show answer
For each significant or recurring failure, document what happened, when it was detected, which control failed, the immediate cause, the underlying process weakness, the consequences, and the evidence supporting the analysis. Do not stop at statements such as “staff error” when late source data, inadequate delegation, ambiguous policy, broken system validation, or missing review may have contributed. Assign corrective actions to responsible owners with due dates and measurable completion criteria. Where the issue affects a gate or other critical control, preserve containment until an independent verifier confirms the remedy works. Analyze repeated findings across agreements to identify systemic improvements to templates, training, source documentation, approval routes, and system validation.

Study instruction:
- "Investigate a hypothetical grant activation failure and distinguish the immediate error, root cause, corrective action, preventive action, and independent retest."
Key point:
- Effective corrective action addresses the underlying cause, not only the visible error.
- Critical remedies require verification before the control is considered restored.
32. Q: How should sensitive agreement, due-diligence, safeguarding, and personal information be protected?
A: Show answer
Classify records according to sensitivity and business purpose, then restrict access to authorized users who need the information for their duties. General grant records should contain only the details required for operational tracking; sensitive investigation evidence, security reports, or personal information should remain in the designated protected repository. Record access grants, changes, reviews, and removals, and ensure consultants and other external parties receive only authorized access. Apply retention, disclosure, incident response, and secure-disposal rules under applicable law, donor commitments, and organizational policy. For an Indonesian entity where the Personal Data Protection Law applies, legal and privacy specialists should verify the relevant obligations for the actual processing activity. Do not store passwords, authentication codes, or other security credentials in grant registers.

Study instruction:
- "Classify a sample grant file inventory by sensitivity and identify access, storage, retention, and incident-escalation requirements for each category."
Key point:
- Access and disclosure must be proportionate to the user's authorized purpose.
- Sensitive evidence requires protected handling and an approved retention basis.
Source links:
- Law No. 27 of 2022 on Personal Data Protection — Indonesian Legal Information
33. Q: What audit trail should be maintained from initial agreement intake to final closeout?
A: Show answer
Maintain a retrievable history of source documents, agreement versions, diligence records, clause assessments, budget calculations, decisions, delegated approvals, signatures, system setup, independent test results, handover acknowledgments, amendments, submissions, exceptions, and closeout evidence. Every material change should record the actor, timestamp, reason, previous value, new value, and authorization reference where relevant. Avoid overwriting authoritative documents or relying on uncontrolled email attachments as the only record of a decision. Establish a verified retention schedule and apply any legal hold before disposing of records. Periodically test whether a reviewer can reconstruct a past decision from the evidence and whether the records are readable and accessible to authorized users.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a selected grant from its intake ID to the executed agreement, approved budget, setup verification, amendments, and closeout record without relying on undocumented verbal explanations."
Key point:
- The audit trail must reconstruct decisions and changes across the complete lifecycle.
- Retention and access rules must be verified, not guessed.
34. Q: How should an agreement with contradictory clauses or missing annexes be handled?
A: Show answer
Identify the specific conflict or missing document, record the affected decisions, and determine whether the matter prevents a reliable interpretation of scope, eligible costs, dates, budget, reporting, or another material obligation. Request the authoritative document or written clarification through the approved communication route. Legal or other qualified specialists should evaluate conflicts within their remit and advise which interpretation or resolution requires formal approval. Do not select whichever clause appears more convenient or assume a later informal email overrides the signed agreement. Where the conflict is material, maintain the relevant hold until the resolution is documented and any necessary agreement amendment or reapproval is completed.

Study instruction:
- "Review a hypothetical agreement with inconsistent end dates and a missing budget annex, then document the blocking issue, required clarification, approver, and conditions for resuming the workflow."
Key point:
- Material ambiguity must be resolved through an authorized process.
- Missing or conflicting evidence cannot be replaced with an unsupported assumption.
35. Q: How should a sample budget calculation be used during review without confusing an illustration with an approved rule?
A: Show answer
The supplied fictional worked case includes an illustrative budget of IDR 600,000,000 and an indirect-cost calculation of 8%, producing IDR 48,000,000. The calculation is mathematically correct for that example, but the rate and amount are not evidence of a generally applicable donor entitlement, organizational policy, or approved overhead method. Reviewers should determine the actual contractual base, permitted categories, cap, exclusions, and required approvals before adopting a calculation. The final budget should show the formula, source clause, assumptions, currency, version, preparer, reviewer, and reconciliation to the award ceiling. Use controlled calculation logic so changes to an input update the total transparently and cannot bypass approval of a revised budget.

Study instruction:
- "Recalculate the illustrative 8% overhead amount and identify the contract facts and approval evidence needed before applying any indirect-cost rate to a real grant."
Key point:
- Correct arithmetic does not establish that a rate is contractually permitted.
- Illustrative amounts must remain distinct from approved organizational data.
36. Q: How can Excel, Power Query, or VBA support agreement review and grant setup controls?
A: Show answer
Excel tables can organize agreement metadata, clause references, approval records, eligibility rules, deadlines, and master-data mappings. Power Query can standardize imported records, compare lists, detect missing fields, and produce exception reports. VBA can prepare controlled handover packs, update workflow records, create dated output files, or log specified actions when the workbook is designed and tested appropriately. Automated outputs must remain traceable to their source documents and approved versions. Validation should flag duplicate identifiers, missing annexes, invalid dates, inconsistent totals, unresolved conditions, and mismatches between signed agreements and system data. Automation cannot interpret complex legal terms or establish accounting recognition by itself, so specialist judgment and independent verification remain necessary. Technology guidance supplements rather than replaces legal, accounting, and contractual authority.

Study instruction:
- "Design a prototype that flags an unapproved budget change and missing obligation owner, and test it against manually verified expected results."
Key point:
- Automation improves consistency and exception detection but does not establish legal or accounting validity.
- Source traceability, testing, approval, and error handling must be designed into the workflow.
37. Q: Which negative tests are essential before releasing a grant setup workflow?
A: Show answer
Negative tests should deliberately introduce conditions that the control design must reject or hold. Examples include a missing mandatory annex, an obsolete agreement version, an unresolved screening match, an ineligible expenditure, a budget above the authorized ceiling, an invalid signatory, an unmet condition precedent, a duplicate grant code, an incorrect end date, a missing reporting owner, and a mismatch between the executed agreement and configuration. For each test, document the input, expected response, actual response, evidence, severity, defect owner, and retest result. A workflow should not pass merely because normal examples process successfully. Tests must also check that changes cannot bypass mandatory gates and that failed tests remain visible in the final acceptance record.

Study instruction:
- "Run a negative-test set covering document completeness, approval authority, budget limits, duplicate codes, and blocking conditions, then compare actual and expected outcomes."
Key point:
- Critical negative tests should prove that invalid states are blocked or flagged.
- Defects require remediation and retesting before release.
38. Q: How should management monitor whether the SOP is operating effectively?
A: Show answer
Management should monitor indicators that reveal both timeliness and control quality. Useful measures include time from intake to decision, percentage of agreements with complete document inventories, unresolved diligence and clause exceptions, approvals returned for correction, setup discrepancies, overdue obligations, amendment propagation failures, incomplete handovers, and closeout items outstanding. Define each indicator's numerator, denominator, source system, owner, reporting frequency, threshold, and escalation rule. A short processing time should not count as success when reviews have been skipped. Analyze recurring causes across periods and assign corrective actions to the owners who can improve the process. Independent assurance may test the design and operation of controls without taking over management's responsibility for executing them.

Study instruction:
- "Build a monthly control dashboard with explicit KPI definitions and test its results against the source register, approval evidence, and obligation calendar."
Key point:
- Performance metrics should measure control effectiveness as well as speed.
- Management review should result in documented decisions and corrective actions.
39. Q: What is required before the fictional SOP example can be adopted for a real organization?
A: Show answer
The organization must verify its legal identity, governing law, actual donor instruments, current legal and regulatory requirements, accounting framework, internal policies, delegation instruments, system configuration, and operational responsibilities. Qualified specialists must assess whether the model procedures fit the organization and identify the revisions needed for its actual funding arrangements. Real agreements, screening outcomes, approvals, signatures, budget values, and evidence records must replace fictional illustrations through controlled, authorized processes. Required workflow and system tests should be executed, significant defects resolved, and all required gates evidenced before implementation. The approved version must identify the policy owner, approver, effective date, review schedule, training requirements, and controlled repository. Until those steps are completed, the example remains a demonstration and must not be treated as an adopted SOP or permission to accept funds, sign, commit expenditure, or activate a grant.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare an adoption-readiness checklist distinguishing fictional content, unverified legal assumptions, organization-specific decisions, testing evidence, and formal approval."
Key point:
- A fictional SOP is a design reference, not operational authorization.
- Adoption requires verified requirements, controlled adaptation, testing, and formal approval.

Table 2
Grant and Donation Receipt Controls
1. Q: How should an SOP control the receipt of grantor funds and donations from initial detection through final accounting reconciliation?
A: Show answer
The process should begin by identifying an expected or actual incoming payment, then verifying the receiving legal entity, bank or payment channel, settlement status, remitter, underlying donor, purpose, agreement, amount and currency. Finance should register the receipt, preserve original evidence, investigate discrepancies, obtain independent review, document allocation instructions or a controlled holding status, and issue only an authorized acknowledgement. Accounting classification must be referred to the authorized accounting process, which determines recognition and posting under the applicable framework and approved policies. The process ends only when source records, receipt registers, actual general ledger records and relevant reconciliation evidence have been reviewed. The supplied SOP is explicitly a fictional, unapproved draft; its examples and approval references are not evidence of actual organizational authorization.
Study instruction:
- Trace one receipt through each process stage and identify the evidence required before it can advance to the next stage.
Key point:
- Receipt verification, allocation, accounting recognition, posting and reconciliation are separate controls.
- A draft procedure cannot be treated as an adopted organizational policy.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
2. Q: What is the purpose of a dedicated procedure for receiving grantor funds and donations?
A: Show answer
Its purpose is to ensure incoming resources are detected, verified, recorded and referred for appropriate accounting treatment without losing their identity, source evidence or intended purpose. The procedure establishes responsibilities, authorization requirements, evidence standards, escalation rules and interfaces with Treasury, Grants Management, Accounting, Legal and compliance functions. It also prevents unsupported donor attribution, premature acknowledgements, unauthorized allocation and duplicate accounting entries. The scope should distinguish receipt processing from grant establishment, revenue recognition, formal bank reconciliation, payment execution and final donor reporting. Those activities may depend on separate procedures and specialized approval authorities. The organization should define these boundaries clearly so that responsibility is neither duplicated nor omitted.
Study instruction:
- Map the receipt procedure against adjacent grant management, Treasury, accounting and donor-reporting procedures, identifying every handoff.
Key point:
- A procedure must define both its responsibilities and its boundaries.
- Every handoff requires an accountable owner and identifiable evidence.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger processes
3. Q: Why is the occurrence of a cash receipt not sufficient evidence that revenue should be recognized?
A: Show answer
A bank credit establishes a cash movement, but it does not independently establish the accounting nature of the transaction. The funds might represent a donation, a grant advance, an amount subject to enforceable conditions, a refundable transfer, an unidentified payment or another type of receipt. Recognition depends on the applicable reporting framework, the rights and obligations arising from the arrangement, whether the organization controls the resource, and whether any corresponding liability or other balance is required. Finance should therefore verify the receipt first and refer the accounting assessment to the authorized accounting owner. The final entry must be supported by the actual circumstances and approved accounting policy rather than by the description appearing in the bank statement.
Study instruction:
- Compare a confirmed donation, an unidentified bank credit and a conditional grant, and identify the additional evidence needed to determine their accounting treatment.
Key point:
- Cash receipt and revenue recognition are different events.
- Classification must follow the applicable accounting framework and transaction evidence.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Revenue from Non-Exchange Transactions
- IPSASB, IPSAS 47 — Revenue
4. Q: How should an organization determine which accounting framework governs donations and grant receipts?
A: Show answer
The organization should document its legal form, statutory reporting requirements, governing instruments, applicable accounting standards, donor obligations and approved accounting policies before determining recognition and measurement. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual provides useful reference material for applicable UN reporting entities, but it is not automatically binding on every independent NGO or foundation. IPSAS 47, Revenue, has an effective date of 1 January 2026 for periods beginning on or after that date, subject to the entity's applicable framework and adoption arrangements. Some legacy UN Finance Manual guidance reflects earlier standards. The preparer must establish which requirements actually apply rather than combining rules from different frameworks. Unresolved framework questions should be escalated to the appropriate accounting authority before an operational treatment is approved.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a framework applicability memo identifying the governing requirements, effective dates, reporting period and approval authority.
Key point:
- Reference guidance must not be confused with an entity's mandatory accounting framework.
- Recognition decisions require confirmation of the applicable standard and policy.
Source links:
- IPSASB, IPSAS 47 — Revenue
- IPSASB — Revenue and Transfer Expense Pronouncements and Effective Dates
5. Q: What legal and regulatory checks should be completed before an NGO collects public donations in Indonesia?
A: Show answer
Legal or compliance personnel should determine whether the proposed fundraising activity falls within Indonesian requirements governing the collection of money or goods, including relevant authorization, exemption, reporting and accountability provisions. The assessment should consider the organization conducting the activity, the geographic scope, the collection channel, the beneficiary, the nature of the campaign and any applicable exceptions. Relevant reference points include Law No. 9 of 1961, Government Regulation No. 29 of 1980 and the Minister of Social Affairs regulations governing the collection of money or goods, including amendments introduced by Minister of Social Affairs Regulation No. 8 of 2024. Their applicability and current consolidated requirements must be checked against the actual activity. A bank account or charitable organizational status does not by itself establish that every fundraising activity is legally authorized.
Study instruction:
- Ask Legal to document the applicable regulatory provisions and required permissions for each public fundraising channel used by the organization.
Key point:
- Fundraising authorization is a legal applicability question, not an accounting conclusion.
- Verify current requirements and exceptions before launching a collection activity.
Source links:
- Law No. 9 of 1961 — Collection of Money or Goods
- Government Regulation No. 29 of 1980 — Implementation of Fundraising
- Minister of Social Affairs Regulation No. 8 of 2024 — Amendment to Regulation No. 8 of 2021
6. Q: How should the organization verify the legal entity and receiving bank account before accepting incoming funds?
A: Show answer
Treasury and Legal should independently verify the beneficiary's legal identity, registration information where applicable, bank-account ownership, approved receiving currency, account mandate and authority to receive the funds. The organization's legal name should be reconciled with banking documents and approved master data. If a payment appears in an account belonging to another entity or an unapproved intermediary, it should be held for investigation rather than silently transferred or attributed to the organization. Changes to account details require independent verification and documented authorization. The procedure should also define escalation requirements for unauthorized channels, misdirected transfers and suspected impersonation. These checks protect against diversion, incorrect entity reporting and funds entering an account outside the organization's approved control environment.
Study instruction:
- Compare the legal entity register, bank mandate, approved account register and actual statement information for each receiving account.
Key point:
- Entity identity and account ownership must be established independently.
- An incoming credit does not resolve an unauthorized-account exception.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Financial transaction records
7. Q: How should responsibilities be divided among receipt preparation, verification, approval, accounting and reconciliation?
A: Show answer
The process should assign separate responsibilities for submitting donor information, registering receipts, verifying bank or provider evidence, matching remittances, approving allocations, issuing acknowledgements, determining accounting treatment, posting journals and reconciling records. Where practicable, no individual should prepare and independently approve the same transaction or certify a reconciliation without appropriate review. If staffing constraints prevent full segregation, management should approve documented compensating controls, such as independent review of evidence and subsequent reconciliation by someone who did not prepare the transaction. Access rights must match delegated authority rather than job title alone. Delegations, substitutes, approval limits and conflict-of-duty exceptions should be maintained in a controlled register and periodically reviewed.
Study instruction:
- Build a responsibility matrix covering every receipt-stage activity and test whether any user can initiate, approve and close the same transaction without independent oversight.
Key point:
- Duties should be segregated according to risk and authority.
- Compensating controls must be documented and independently evidenced.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Journal workflows and approvals
8. Q: What is the difference between a remitter, an underlying donor, an intermediary and the legal recipient of funds?
A: Show answer
The remitter is the party shown as sending or transmitting the payment. The underlying donor is the person or institution that provided the charitable funds. An intermediary, payment processor, fundraising platform or agent may collect and transfer resources without being the ultimate donor. The legal recipient is the entity whose account or other controlled channel actually receives the funds. These identities can coincide, but the process must not assume that they always do. Finance should document the relationships using bank or provider records, remittance instructions, donor communications, agreements and relevant settlement reports. Where an intermediary transmits pooled donations, the organization may know the source channel without yet knowing each donor's identity. The record must reflect that uncertainty until appropriate evidence is obtained.
Study instruction:
- Review a payment processed through an intermediary and identify the evidence that establishes the remitter, underlying donor, recipient and intended purpose separately.
Key point:
- Payment origin, donor identity and receiving entity are different attributes.
- Unverified donor attribution must remain unresolved.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Voluntary contributions
9. Q: How should the receipt register distinguish expected payments, pending credits, settled receipts and unresolved cases?
A: Show answer
Each receipt should be assigned a unique identifier and a controlled status reflecting the evidence available. A payment notice may indicate that funds are expected, but it does not establish settlement. A pending bank entry may require confirmation that it has finalized. A settled credit can proceed to donor and remittance verification, but it may still be unmatched, unallocated or awaiting accounting referral. Status transitions should be permitted only when their required evidence and approvals are recorded. For example, a receipt should not be marked reconciled merely because a bank credit exists, and it should not be marked posted merely because a journal file has been prepared. The register should preserve historical events and avoid overwriting previous statuses without a traceable correction record.
Study instruction:
- Define the permitted receipt statuses and list the mandatory evidence for each transition from pending to closure.
Key point:
- Every status must represent a verifiable condition.
- Settlement, allocation, posting and reconciliation require separate proof.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger document statuses
10. Q: What evidence demonstrates that an incoming bank or payment-provider transaction has actually settled?
A: Show answer
Evidence should come from an authoritative source appropriate to the channel, such as an official bank statement, authenticated bank transaction report or provider settlement report. The verifier should examine the receiving account, transaction identifier, currency, amount, settlement date, transaction status and relevant report filters. A donor's transfer screenshot, remittance advice, email notification or payment instruction may support the investigation but should not automatically be treated as final settlement evidence. The original source should be retained with information identifying how it was retrieved, who obtained it, when it was obtained and which population it covers. If the transaction remains pending, reversed or uncertain, the register must reflect that state and prevent unsupported completion of downstream controls.
Study instruction:
- Compare a payment notice with independent bank or provider evidence and record the exact fact each document proves.
Key point:
- Payment instructions and settled transactions are not equivalent.
- Source authenticity and settlement status must be verified separately.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and transaction controls
11. Q: How should evidence authenticity, completeness and custody be documented?
A: Show answer
Maintain an evidence record containing the source identity, retrieval method, retrieval date, reporting period, applicable filters, transaction reference, file version, custodian and reviewer. Preserve the original evidence or a controlled immutable copy and link it to the receipt record. Any transformed file, extracted table or supporting calculation should remain traceable to its original source. Reviewers should verify that the report covers the correct accounts, dates and transaction types rather than relying on totals alone. Missing pages, incomplete statements, inconsistent identifiers or unsupported screenshots should be recorded as exceptions. Corrections should be added as linked events rather than used to overwrite original evidence. These controls allow another reviewer to reproduce the investigation and assess whether the record is reliable.
Study instruction:
- Select a receipt and reconstruct its evidence history, including retrieval metadata, original source, transformations and independent review.
Key point:
- Evidence must be traceable to its origin and scope.
- A plausible total does not establish source completeness or authenticity.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger records and controls
12. Q: How should donor remittances be matched to executed agreements and receipt records?
A: Show answer
Matching should consider donor identity, remitter, agreement or award identifier, payment purpose, amount, currency, expected settlement date and approved receiving account. Finance should compare the actual receipt with executed agreements, amendments, remittance advice and other corroborating records. A similar donor name or matching amount alone is insufficient if the evidence conflicts. For example, an intermediary may remit a pooled sum that does not correspond to one individual award. The system should allow one remittance to relate to multiple supported allocation lines while retaining the trace to the original receipt. If the matching information is incomplete or inconsistent, the case should remain unmatched or on hold until the appropriate owner resolves the discrepancy.
Study instruction:
- Reconcile a sample of executed agreements and remittance instructions to actual credits, noting all discrepancies and the evidence required to resolve them.
Key point:
- Matching requires corroborating identity, amount, purpose and contractual evidence.
- Unsupported donor attribution must not be used to force a match.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Donor agreements and voluntary contributions
13. Q: Why should every incoming receipt have a unique identifier and a duplicate-screening procedure?
A: Show answer
A unique identifier links the receipt register, source evidence, allocation instructions, acknowledgement, accounting referral, actual journal entry and reconciliation record. Duplicate screening should compare more than transaction reference alone. Useful attributes include account, transaction identifier, date, currency, amount, remitter, agreement reference and provider batch. The same valid remittance may appear in a bank report, provider report and accounting extract, so identical amounts in different source systems do not automatically indicate duplicate cash. Conversely, duplicate imports can cause double counting even when the original payment occurred only once. Candidate duplicates should be investigated and either linked to a single underlying event or documented as distinct valid transactions before the records are released for processing.
Study instruction:
- Test a register with duplicate imports, repeated amounts from different donors and the same payment represented by several source-system records.
Key point:
- Duplicate detection should identify candidate duplicates without automatically deleting legitimate transactions.
- Unique identifiers improve traceability across the complete receipt lifecycle.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger records
14. Q: How should gross donations, processing fees, reserves, refunds and net bank settlements be reconciled?
A: Show answer
The reconciliation should establish how the reported gross amount leads to the actual net settlement. Calculate gross funds less evidenced fees, reserves, refunds, chargebacks and other valid adjustments, applying the contract and provider's settlement terms. Separate fees deducted before settlement from charges debited independently to the bank to avoid counting the same cost twice. The resulting amount should agree with the relevant settlement evidence, and each adjustment should have a source reference and appropriate accounting treatment. Differences should be identified by receipt or settlement batch rather than buried in an aggregate balancing figure. A zero difference supports arithmetic agreement but does not independently establish donor identity, authorization, correct recognition or complete source data.
Study instruction:
- Recalculate a provider settlement independently and identify evidence for every gross-to-net adjustment and separate bank charge.
Key point:
- Gross-to-net calculations must be reproducible and supported by source evidence.
- Arithmetic agreement does not replace other recognition and approval requirements.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and reconciliations
15. Q: How should foreign-currency donations be converted and documented?
A: Show answer
The applicable accounting policy should specify the permitted exchange-rate source, currency pair, direction, date convention, precision and rounding method for the intended accounting purpose. Retain the original foreign-currency amount, converted amount, rate evidence, rate date, formula version and approval. Do not assume that an illustrative exchange rate in a training example is an approved operating parameter. Accounting translation and the conversion used for donor reporting may have different requirements, depending on the framework and agreement. If the required rate is unavailable or its basis is uncertain, hold the affected conversion until the authorized accountant resolves the issue. The calculation can be automated, but the software cannot determine which rate convention the governing policy requires.
Study instruction:
- Recalculate a foreign-currency receipt using the approved source, rate date, currency direction and rounding convention, then compare it with the accounting record.
Key point:
- Currency conversion requires an approved and documented methodology.
- An illustrative exchange rate is not automatically an accounting policy.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and financial controls
16. Q: How should a receipt be allocated across multiple donor agreements, projects or cost centres?
A: Show answer
Allocation should follow the executed agreement, documented donor instructions, approved project master data and delegated allocation authority. A receipt may need to be split across multiple projects, but each line should identify the supporting basis, currency, amount and approver. The sum of allocation lines must equal the amount eligible for allocation after considering documented settlement adjustments and the applicable currency policy. The procedure should prohibit allocation to an unapproved project code or use of an unsupported balancing line merely to force the total to agree. If the donor purpose or split is unresolved, retain the amount in the approved holding workflow. Approved allocation instructions must remain linked to the original receipt and the version reviewed by the authorizing officer.
Study instruction:
- Recalculate an allocation across multiple projects and test the total, donor authority, valid project codes and approval version.
Key point:
- Allocation is an authorized distribution decision, not an automatic consequence of receipt.
- Every allocation must be traceable to the original amount and its approved basis.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Funding arrangements
17. Q: How should donor restrictions be distinguished from enforceable conditions attached to a donation?
A: Show answer
A restriction generally directs or limits the purpose for which resources may be used, while an enforceable condition may create additional obligations and a potential liability if the stipulated requirements are not met. The distinction depends on the applicable accounting standard, the actual agreement and the enforceability of the donor's rights. Finance should document the specified use, performance requirements, return obligations, reporting commitments and legal basis for any conclusion. The source SOP correctly warns against inferring unrestricted availability from receipt alone. However, classification and recognition must be determined by the applicable framework, not by a generic rule copied from a different reporting entity. Spending eligibility should also be checked against the donor agreement and organizational authorization, independently of the accounting conclusion.
Study instruction:
- Compare two donor agreements, one containing a purpose restriction and another with an enforceable return obligation, and explain the questions an accountant must resolve.
Key point:
- Restrictions and conditions can have different accounting consequences.
- Agreement analysis and the governing accounting framework must support the classification.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Conditions and restrictions on non-exchange transfers
- IPSASB, IPSAS 47 — Revenue
18. Q: Why must allocation approval and permission to spend be distinguished from accounting recognition?
A: Show answer
Allocation approval identifies the project, donor or purpose to which funds may be assigned under the applicable authority. Permission to spend depends on the donor agreement, budget, procurement rules, approved activities, internal authorization and other relevant conditions. Accounting recognition is a separate assessment under the reporting framework. A receipt can be verified while still awaiting an allocation decision; it can be allocated for tracking purposes while spending remains prohibited; and a recognized balance does not necessarily establish that a proposed expenditure is eligible under a donor agreement. The register should therefore record these states separately and prevent one approval from automatically releasing every downstream action. Any unresolved eligibility question should be escalated to the relevant grant or legal owner before expenditure is authorized.
Study instruction:
- Construct a workflow showing the separate evidence required for receipt verification, allocation, spending clearance and accounting treatment.
Key point:
- Allocation, spending authorization and accounting recognition are not interchangeable.
- Every decision must be supported by its own governing criteria.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Donor funding arrangements
19. Q: When may a grant commitment or donation pledge support recognition of a receivable before cash is received?
A: Show answer
The answer depends on whether the applicable accounting requirements recognize an enforceable right to receive the resources and whether the relevant recognition criteria are satisfied. A nonbinding expression of intent or fundraising target should not automatically be recorded as a receivable. Finance should examine the executed agreement, donor commitment, acceptance provisions, enforceability, conditions, payment schedule and any remaining uncertainties. The authorized accountant should document why a receivable is or is not recognized, its measurement and any required impairment assessment or disclosure. A contractual commitment that supports recognition under one framework may require different analysis under another. The receipt procedure should preserve the agreement and refer the accounting decision to the appropriate process instead of generating an entry solely from a pledge register.
Study instruction:
- Compare a signed enforceable grant agreement with a nonbinding donor promise and identify the recognition questions that remain under the organization's framework.
Key point:
- Not every pledge or expected donation creates a recognizable receivable.
- Recognition depends on enforceability, applicable criteria and supported measurement.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Contributions receivable and non-exchange revenue
- IPSASB, IPSAS 47 — Revenue
20. Q: How should donated goods and services be evaluated for accounting and operational purposes?
A: Show answer
Non-cash contributions require their own verification process because no bank credit necessarily exists. The organization should identify the donor, item or service, transfer or delivery date, intended purpose, evidence of control or entitlement, quantity, condition, valuation basis and applicable recognition requirements. Goods may require an asset or inventory assessment, while services may be subject to different recognition or disclosure rules under the applicable framework. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual contains policies for its reporting entities, including specific thresholds and measurement guidance; these must not be applied automatically to an independent NGO. Any recognized asset should be coordinated with the relevant asset or inventory register. Uncertain valuations or ownership should be referred to the competent accounting or operational specialist.
Study instruction:
