- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Receivables and Financial Balances
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Balance-Sheet and Reporting Categories
14. Q: How should budget underspend, uncommitted budget, forecast variance, and attributable cash be distinguished?
A: Show answer
Each metric answers a different question. Budget underspend is the approved budget less eligible actual costs under a defined scope. Uncommitted budget is generally the approved budget less eligible actual costs and approved open commitments. Forecast variance compares the approved budget with forecast final eligible costs. Award cash attribution tracks grant receipts less linked cash disbursements, including the approved opening attribution where relevant. None of these measures automatically equals the general-ledger liability, actual bank balance, recognized income, or refund due. The formula must specify the budget version, eligible-cost basis, commitment status, currency, and reporting date. Finance should retain the underlying values and assumptions, then reconcile the resulting measures with the appropriate accounting and operational records. A calculation can be arithmetically correct while its interpretation remains legally or accounting-wise unapproved.

Study instruction:
- Calculate all four metrics for one award and explain the distinct evidence and interpretation required for each result.
Key point:
- Budget, forecast, cash, income, and liability are different measures.
- Correct arithmetic does not itself establish a repayment obligation.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Contributions and Unspent Funds
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Statement Balances
15. Q: How should the fictional grant example be calculated and interpreted?
A: Show answer
The supplied demonstration uses an invented approved budget of IDR 100,000,000, cash receipts of IDR 100,000,000, eligible actual costs of IDR 35,000,000, cash disbursements of IDR 35,000,000, approved open commitments of IDR 12,000,000, and forecast final eligible costs of IDR 90,000,000. Under those stated assumptions, budget underspend is IDR 65,000,000, calculated as IDR 100,000,000 minus IDR 35,000,000. Uncommitted budget is IDR 53,000,000, calculated as the approved budget less actual costs and commitments. Forecast variance is IDR 10,000,000, calculated as the approved budget less forecast final costs. Award cash attribution is IDR 65,000,000, calculated as receipts less linked cash disbursements. These are synthetic calculations and do not establish bank balance, accounting income, liability, or refund amount. The example's recognition and refund conclusions properly remain undetermined pending framework and clause review.

Study instruction:
- Recalculate the four measures independently and verify that the remaining accounting and contractual conclusions stay on HOLD.
Key point:
- The example's calculations are illustrative, not actual organization records.
- The refund amount remains undetermined until the relevant decision gates are satisfied.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Contributions and Unspent Funds
16. Q: Why does an unspent grant budget not automatically create a debt to the donor?
A: Show answer
An underspend is initially a management variance. A repayment obligation requires a separate analysis of the agreement, effective amendments, relevant trigger, eligible costs, commitments, condition status, enforceable rights, applicable framework, and any authorized donor clarification. Some agreements may allow approved carryforwards or reallocations; others may require return of specified unused or unsupported amounts. A budget difference by itself does not settle which treatment applies. The finance team should first reconcile the actual and forecast cost position, then identify the contractual provision that governs the residual amount. Legal and accounting review should be obtained when needed. Until the decision is authorized, keep the amount as an open disposition issue and do not promise, pay, net, write off, or reallocate it merely to close the grant register.

Study instruction:
- Compare two fictional agreements with different residual-balance clauses and determine what additional evidence is needed before concluding that a refund is due.
Key point:
- Underspend is not synonymous with debt.
- Residual-fund disposition depends on valid terms, measured facts, and authorized review.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Return of Unused Funds
- IFRS — IAS 37 Provisions, Contingent Liabilities and Contingent Assets
17. Q: How should commitments and forecasts affect the assessment of unspent grant balances?
A: Show answer
Commitments and forecasts provide information about expected use of the approved budget but must remain separate from actual expenditure, cash disbursement, and accounting balances. Include approved commitments using a defined status and evidence standard, such as an authorized purchase order or valid contract. Exclude informal plans and unsupported estimates from measures that are intended to show approved commitments. Forecast final costs should identify their assumptions, data sources, expected delivery timing, eligibility considerations, and owner. Material changes in forecast or commitments should be documented and reviewed because they may affect operational planning, donor communication, or the timing of a disposition review. However, the forecast should never replace contractual interpretation or accounting analysis. When actual spending or commitments exceed the approved budget, escalate the matter and identify the required authorization rather than relying on an optimistic forecast to conceal the variance.

Study instruction:
- Compare actuals, approved commitments, and forecast costs for a grant, then explain which amounts are appropriate for each management measure.
Key point:
- Forecast and commitment data improve planning but do not establish accounting or repayment treatment.
- Budget exceptions require documented ownership and action.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Grant Funding and Program Activities
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting and Balances
18. Q: How should donor conditions and milestones be monitored throughout the award period?
A: Show answer
Each condition or milestone should have a unique identifier, agreement clause, effective agreement version, objective criteria, responsible program owner, due date, required evidence standard, submission date, independent reviewer, decision, and next action. The monitoring register should distinguish a pending condition from evidence submitted but not accepted, and from a condition independently confirmed as fulfilled. Spending or submitting a report does not automatically prove that a contractual milestone has been satisfied. Where donor acceptance is required, retain the actual acceptance evidence and confirm that the relevant person or entity had authority. Overdue or unsupported conditions should be escalated before they affect future tranches or reporting deadlines. If the milestone remains disputed, preserve the original evidence and maintain the blocking status until the dispute or assessment is resolved.

Study instruction:
- Select a milestone and trace its criteria, due date, supporting evidence, reviewer decision, and impact on subsequent funding.
Key point:
- Expenditure, report submission, and milestone fulfillment are separate determinations.
- Conditions should not be marked complete without evidence that matches the agreed criteria.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Contribution Conditions and Accounting
- IPSASB — Revenue and IPSAS 47
19. Q: What evidence is necessary to demonstrate that a funded activity complies with permitted-use restrictions?
A: Show answer
Evidence should link the payment to the donor-approved purpose, activity, date, location, beneficiary group where relevant, project code, and the transaction's supporting records. Depending on the agreement, this may include contracts, invoices, attendance records, delivery confirmations, approved budgets, procurement files, travel documents, program reports, and acceptance certificates. Each piece of evidence should be evaluated against the actual eligibility criteria rather than merely collected in a folder. Ineligible, unsupported, or ambiguous costs must be placed on an exception register with an assigned owner and resolution deadline. A cost can be recorded in the accounting system and still require a separate eligibility assessment for donor reporting. Conversely, a cost deemed eligible under a donor agreement does not automatically resolve every accounting recognition or measurement question. The relevant approvals, corrections, and evidence must be retained.

Study instruction:
- Trace sample program expenses from source documents through donor eligibility review, project coding, accounting entry, and reporting classification.
Key point:
- Financial recording and donor eligibility review serve different purposes.
- Evidence must substantiate the actual clause requirement, not merely the payment amount.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Funding Program and Project Activities
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Controls
20. Q: How should interest earned on unspent grant cash be assessed?
A: Show answer
The organization should inspect the agreement and applicable law or policy to determine whether interest may be earned, who is entitled to it, whether it must be returned or allocated, and whether additional reporting or approval is required. Verify the actual interest income against bank statements or investment records and identify the period and relevant cash pool. If the cash is pooled, establish a supportable allocation method rather than assuming all recorded interest belongs to one grant. The accounting recognition and presentation of interest must follow the organization's applicable framework. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual describes circumstances in which the UN's right to direct the use of interest affects its accounting treatment, but those rules cannot automatically be transplanted to an independent NGO. Unresolved contractual entitlement, allocation, or tax matters should be reviewed by the appropriate specialist before accounting or settlement decisions are finalized.

Study instruction:
- Inspect a donor clause on interest and prepare a schedule reconciling the bank interest, allocation method, donor entitlement, and accounting review.
Key point:
- Interest allocation follows the applicable agreement and framework, not an assumed universal rule.
- Actual bank evidence and the allocation basis must be retained.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Interest on Unutilized Contributions
- IFRS — IAS 21 The Effects of Changes in Foreign Exchange Rates
21. Q: How should a grant register be reconciled with the general ledger, cash records, subledgers, and donor report?
A: Show answer
The period-end reconciliation should compare the grant register with the general ledger and relevant subledgers, bank or cash records where directly traceable, receivables, income, liabilities or deferred amounts, eligible expenditure, commitments, partner advances, foreign-exchange adjustments, and donor-report totals. Use the same reporting period, source versions, currency basis, and mapping rules. Each difference should identify the amount, cause, owner, target date, supporting evidence, correction or explanation, and independent retest. Where the donor report uses a different contractual reporting basis from the financial statements, document the bridge rather than forcing the two totals to match without explanation. Unexplained material differences should block certification of the affected report or closeout. After corrections are approved, verify the updated ledger and report against the final register and preserve the reconciliation record.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a grant-to-GL reconciliation and trace each difference to a specific source record or approved adjustment.
Key point:
- A reconciliation is evidence of agreement or an explanation of differences, not simply a matching total.
- Unresolved material differences must be escalated before certification.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Receivables and Reconciliations
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Reconciliation Controls
22. Q: Why must grant cash attribution be distinguished from the actual bank balance?
A: Show answer
An organization may hold donor funds in a pooled bank account rather than a separate account for every award. A grant register can therefore calculate attributed cash from an opening allocation, linked receipts, and linked disbursements without claiming that the calculated balance is the physical cash balance of a dedicated account. The actual bank balance must be established from bank statements and bank reconciliation, while accounting cash follows the applicable accounting records. The allocation method must be documented and applied consistently so that the same receipt or payment is not assigned to multiple grants. Outstanding transfers, bank fees, foreign-exchange movements, timing differences, and shared payments may require separate analysis. Any allocation that cannot be substantiated should remain flagged rather than being treated as exact cash availability for spending or refund purposes.

Study instruction:
- Reconcile pooled bank cash to the accounting cash balance, then separately demonstrate how grant-level attribution is calculated and controlled.
Key point:
- Attributed grant cash is not necessarily the bank balance or an accounting liability.
- Pooled-cash allocations require consistent rules and traceable evidence.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Cash and Cash Equivalents
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Statement Balances
23. Q: How should foreign-exchange differences be handled in grant monitoring and closeout?
A: Show answer
The file should retain the original currency and amount, transaction date, applicable rate source, rate date, functional or reporting currency, conversion result, and adjustment reference. The correct rate and accounting treatment depend on the applicable framework and nature of the transaction. Donor reporting may also have contractually prescribed currency or rate conventions that differ from financial-statement requirements. Reconcile currency movements and identify whether a difference arises from exchange rates, timing, rounding, missing transactions, or an inconsistent rate source. Do not assume that one spreadsheet rate is appropriate for receipt recognition, period-end measurement, settlement, and every donor report. Material unexplained differences should remain open for Finance review, with an approved resolution method and documented re-performance. An exchange-rate adjustment must not be used to conceal an actual cash or expenditure discrepancy.

Study instruction:
- Recalculate a sample foreign-currency transaction under the approved accounting and donor-reporting methods, identifying where the requirements differ.
Key point:
- Accounting conversion and contractual donor-reporting conversion may serve different purposes.
- Rate sources, dates, adjustments, and approvals must be reproducible.
Source links:
- IFRS — IAS 21 The Effects of Changes in Foreign Exchange Rates
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Foreign Currency and Contributions
24. Q: What should happen when grant balances do not agree with the accounting or donor-reporting records?
A: Show answer
The Financial Controller should identify the source and amount of the difference, assess its impact on accounting and reporting, and open an exception if the cause is not immediately supported. Investigate missing transactions, duplicate entries, incorrect grant mapping, period cut-off, ineligible costs, journal timing, foreign-exchange treatment, partner advances, and different donor-report definitions. Reconcile the original data before making adjustments. Every proposed correction should record the prior value, corrected value, reason, evidence, affected accounts and reports, preparer, reviewer, and approval. A narrative explanation alone is not sufficient if the underlying accounting entry or source record is wrong. The exception should only be closed after the correction or valid reconciliation explanation has been independently tested and all affected reports have been updated or appropriately disclosed.

Study instruction:
- Investigate a hypothetical grant-register difference and document the root cause, supporting evidence, required correction, approval, and independent retest.
Key point:
- Differences need evidence-based explanations and controlled corrections.
- Repeated unexplained variances should lead to process and control improvements.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Financial Control Processes
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting
25. Q: How should unsupported, ineligible, overdue, or potentially over-spent grant balances be investigated?
A: Show answer
Create a controlled issue record containing the grant ID, affected clause, amount if known, transaction or milestone, date identified, severity, owner, deadline, immediate containment action, and current status. Preserve original documents and avoid overwriting the evidence under investigation. The program owner should provide delivery and activity evidence, Grants Finance should assess donor eligibility and contractual reporting impact, Legal should review potential breaches or disputed terms, and Accounting should determine the effect on the financial records. Suspected fraud, material breach, unauthorized payment, or data exposure must follow the relevant incident procedure rather than being treated as an ordinary spreadsheet exception. Any adjustment or recovery should follow authorized procedures. The case remains open until corrective action, approvals, revised records, and independent closure testing support resolution.

Study instruction:
- Build an exception record from a hypothetical unsupported vendor payment and identify the containment, investigation, accounting, donor, and approval steps.
Key point:
- Preserve evidence and escalate issues in proportion to their risk.
- An exception is not resolved just because its status has been changed in the register.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Financial Control Processes
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Funding Program and Project Activities
26. Q: What decision gates should be passed before a grant refund or repayment is authorized?
A: Show answer
The release decision should pass five distinct gates. First, a valid contractual basis and relevant trigger must be supported by the effective agreement and legal review when needed. Second, the amount must be reproducible from reconciled eligible costs, commitments, currency, exchange rates, and relevant adjustments. Third, Accounting must determine the appropriate recognition, measurement, and posting implications under the established framework. Fourth, the proposed decision must be approved by a person with current authority for the entity, award, amount, and currency. Fifth, the payment must follow the approved payment procedure and provide evidence that the bank transaction, general ledger, grant register, and any required donor confirmation agree. A failure at any critical gate keeps the process on HOLD. The workflow must not jump directly from a calculated underspend to a payment instruction.

Study instruction:
- Apply all five gates to a sample refund request and identify the evidence that is missing at each failed gate.
Key point:
- Contract, calculation, accounting, authority, and settlement are separate release requirements.
- An unapproved amount must not proceed to payment or be treated as a final liability.
Source links:
- IFRS — IAS 37 Provisions, Contingent Liabilities and Contingent Assets
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Journal Workflows and Approvals
27. Q: How should an approved refund or repayment be recorded and settled?
A: Show answer
The authorized decision should state the recipient, legal and contractual basis, amount, currency, payment deadline, accounting treatment, approval reference, and supporting reconciliation. The payment owner should verify these details against current payment authority and instructions before execution. After payment, retain the bank confirmation and reference, match it to the approved obligation, verify any required donor confirmation, and post or verify the approved accounting entry. Reconcile the payment to the general ledger, grant register, related liability or adjustment, bank statement, and relevant donor reporting. A payment request or bank instruction alone does not demonstrate settlement. Failed, partial, duplicated, or misdirected payments must be investigated and remain open until the correct resolution is supported. If the payment changes an earlier accounting or reporting conclusion, update the related records under version control.

Study instruction:
- Trace an approved refund from the decision memo to the bank record, journal, grant-register clearance, and donor confirmation where required.
Key point:
- Payment execution and settlement reconciliation are distinct activities.
- A balance is not cleared until the transaction and accounting records are reconciled.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Journal Controls
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Cash and Financial Balances
28. Q: How should a donor amendment, waiver, reallocation, or extension affect the grant control process?
A: Show answer
The organization should determine whether the change is legally effective, whether it was signed by authorized parties, which clauses and periods it changes, and whether other agreement provisions remain applicable. Legal or Compliance should review enforceability as appropriate, Grants Finance should update the clause and condition registers, and Accounting should reassess any affected recognition or measurement conclusion. The budget owner should update the approved budget or forecast only when the change is effective and authorized under the relevant procedures. Preserve the prior agreement version, assessment, calculation, approvals, and reports. Do not overwrite the original clause assessment or treat an informal donor email as a binding amendment without checking the required form and authority. A change may require renewed approval, new condition dates, recalculation of residual balances, or restatement of forecasts, but it cannot waive mandatory law or override the applicable accounting framework.

Study instruction:
- Review a hypothetical budget-reallocation amendment and list the legal, operational, accounting, reporting, and approval records that must be updated.
Key point:
- Only effective, authorized changes should alter the operative grant terms.
- Material amendments can invalidate earlier assessments or approvals.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Donor Agreements and Grant Accounting
29. Q: What activities must be completed before a grant is formally closed?
A: Show answer
Grant expiry should trigger a controlled review rather than automatic closure. The organization should reconcile final eligible costs, open commitments, condition evidence, receivables, cash attribution, outstanding liabilities, partner advances, assets, interest, foreign-exchange effects, donor reports, refund or repayment decisions, and unresolved disputes. Where required, confirm that the final financial report was submitted and accepted, that residual funds were refunded or reallocated under authorized terms, and that outstanding vendor or partner liabilities were resolved. Accounting should review any remaining balances under the applicable framework. An independent reviewer should verify that all critical reconciliation and disposition gates are complete before the authorized approver signs off. Open items should prevent closure or remain explicitly controlled under an approved exception process. Archive the final register, decision history, evidence index, and required future review or retention triggers.

Study instruction:
- Complete a closeout checklist and demonstrate that every critical balance and condition has evidence of resolution or an authorized open-item treatment.
Key point:
- Award expiry is a review trigger, not proof that the grant is fully settled.
- No grant should be marked closed merely because its end date has passed.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Grant Closure
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting and Balances
30. Q: How should advances paid to implementing partners be distinguished from unused grant cash held by the NGO?
A: Show answer
An advance paid to an implementing partner is an outgoing resource transfer whose accounting depends on the arrangement, the applicable framework, and the relevant recognition requirements. Under the UN manual's described process, qualifying partner advances not yet expensed at year-end are presented as assets in specified reporting circumstances, while the manual also identifies an exception for a particular reporting volume when control has transferred. An independent NGO must verify its own framework and circumstances rather than automatically adopting that specific UN treatment. The partner's certified expenditure reports, supporting documents, settlement records, unused amounts, and refund requirements should be tracked separately from the NGO's unused incoming grant cash. Reconcile partner balances with payment evidence, reports received, eligible costs accepted, and amounts returned or outstanding. The outstanding asset or other balance must not be cleared merely because the donor grant itself is approaching expiry.

Study instruction:
- Trace an outgoing partner advance from payment through expense substantiation, accounting review, settlement, and grant closeout.
Key point:
- An advance paid to a partner and unused cash received from a donor are different balances.
- Partner expenditure evidence and the applicable framework determine the proper clearance process.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Grants Out and Implementing Partners
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Receivables and Advances
31. Q: How should a donor financial report be distinguished from the organization's financial statements and accounting records?
A: Show answer
A donor report follows the agreement's required reporting format, scope, period, currency, budget categories, and eligible-cost definitions. Financial statements follow the applicable reporting framework and accounting policies. The two outputs may legitimately differ in classification or measurement, but those differences must be supported by a documented bridge rather than unexplained adjustments. Reconcile the donor report to the grant register, general ledger, underlying transactions, and any contractual schedules. Include approved amendments and explain outstanding conditions, disputed costs, or residual balances when required by the agreement. The person certifying the donor report must verify completeness and consistency with available evidence. A management summary or donor report must not be treated as proof that a statutory accounting obligation or journal has been satisfied unless the relevant evidence independently supports that conclusion.

Study instruction:
- Build a bridge from grant-level accounting balances to donor-report lines and document every difference in basis or classification.
Key point:
- Donor reporting and financial-statement reporting have different purposes and may use different bases.
- All material differences need documented explanations and approval.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Donor Funding and Grant Reporting
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Statement Presentation and Disclosure
32. Q: What documentation should be retained to support a grant decision and its audit trail?
A: Show answer
The record should connect the original agreement and amendments to the clause assessment, condition evidence, framework assessment, accounting decision, calculation, independent review, approvals, journal references, payment evidence, donor correspondence, reconciliation, and closeout decision. Each assessment should identify the applicable source versions and dates, preparer, reviewer, conclusion, unresolved questions, and reason for subsequent changes. Preserve historical versions instead of replacing evidence with a later file that no longer explains the earlier decision. Documents should have consistent identifiers and be stored in a controlled repository with appropriate access restrictions. Retention periods must be checked against applicable legal, donor, contractual, audit, and internal policy requirements. Backup procedures should be periodically tested by restoring and opening sample records. Sensitive personal or banking data should only be accessible to authorized people with a legitimate need to use it.

Study instruction:
- Select one material grant decision and verify that another reviewer can reconstruct the evidence, calculation, approval, posting, and subsequent settlement without relying on oral explanations.
Key point:
- An audit trail must preserve both the evidence and the history of decisions.
- Retention and access controls should be defined and tested.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Documentation Controls
- Undang-Undang Nomor 28 Tahun 2004 tentang Perubahan atas Undang-Undang Yayasan
33. Q: How should the organization respond when the donor's directions conflict with the signed agreement or internal accounting policy?
A: Show answer
Preserve the signed agreement, applicable amendments, correspondence, and existing assessments before seeking clarification. Identify which directions conflict, the authority of the people issuing them, whether the change requires a formal amendment, and whether mandatory law or the applicable accounting framework limits the proposed action. Grants Finance should coordinate the documented issue, Legal or Compliance should assess enforceability when needed, and Accounting should evaluate the financial-reporting consequence. Do not make an irreversible reallocation, refund, netting adjustment, write-off, or journal based solely on an informal instruction. The disputed element should remain on HOLD, with an owner, deadline, risk assessment, and escalation route. Once valid clarification or an amendment is obtained, reassess all affected conditions, balances, approvals, and reporting records while preserving the history of the original decision.

Study instruction:
- Prepare an exception memo for conflicting donor directions and identify the decision, authority, and evidence required to release the HOLD status.
Key point:
- Informal correspondence must not automatically replace effective contractual terms.
- Unresolved material conflicts require controlled escalation and preserved evidence.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Donor Agreement Accounting
34. Q: What control indicators should management monitor to evaluate the effectiveness of grant controls?
A: Show answer
Useful indicators include overdue condition evidence, unverified accounting frameworks, outstanding grant advances, aged receivables, unexplained reconciliation differences, unsupported costs, unresolved refund assessments, unincorporated amendments, forecast-versus-budget variances, and closeout delays. Each KPI should define its calculation, source, owner, frequency, target or threshold, and required action when it fails. For example, an overdue-condition count should include all conditions due within the defined reporting scope, not only items that have already been raised as exceptions. A reconciliation KPI should identify the actual unreconciled difference and aging, not merely indicate whether a spreadsheet field says “complete.” Management should review trends and root causes, prioritize issues by risk and deadline, and assign corrective actions. KPI dashboards help focus attention, but they do not replace evidence review, accounting judgment, or external reporting requirements.

Study instruction:
- Define at least five grant-control KPIs and independently verify each result against source records and the applicable period.
Key point:
- KPI definitions and source data must be consistent and reproducible.
- Management should use exceptions to trigger action, not simply produce status reports.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting and Disclosure
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Financial Control Processes
35. Q: When may a grant-related accounting journal be handed over for posting?
A: Show answer
Journal preparation should follow an approved accounting assessment that identifies the applicable framework, transaction facts, amount, currency, accounts, period, supporting documents, and rationale. The journal handoff should reference the exact assessment ID and version, relevant agreement version, reconciliation, approval, and applicable journal procedure. The journal owner should verify that the period is open, the posting route is valid, debit and credit totals agree, and the transaction is not already posted. A failed or ambiguous system response must be investigated against the ledger before retrying to prevent duplicate entries. After posting, record the journal ID and timestamp and reconcile the actual entry to the approved decision. If the source facts or agreement change materially, reassess the earlier approval before relying on it for a new or revised journal.

Study instruction:
- Follow a sample approved assessment into the journal system and verify the period, account mapping, posting confirmation, and reconciliation evidence.
Key point:
- A draft or HOLD assessment is not posting authority.
- The actual ledger entry must be confirmed and reconciled to the approved handoff.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Entries and Journal Controls
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Accounting Policies and Financial Reporting
36. Q: How should a workflow distinguish registered, under-review, approved, implemented, and unresolved grant decisions?
A: Show answer
A workflow should define explicit states, responsible roles, allowed transitions, required records, and release conditions. A registered issue may progress to review, authorized clarification or amendment, reassessment, approval, implementation, and verified closure. If a material question cannot be resolved, it should transition to a visible HOLD state with an owner, deadline, containment action, and escalation path. Closure must require evidence that the corrective action was implemented and independently retested. A user should not be able to bypass legal, accounting, approval, or settlement gates by simply changing a status field. The workflow design should identify protected actions such as approval, journal release, refund payment, and final closure. It should also retain version history so that reopened or revised decisions do not erase the earlier state and its evidence.

Study instruction:
- Draw a state-transition diagram for a disputed grant refund and test each possible success, failure, and HOLD outcome.
Key point:
- Workflow status must reflect controlled decisions and verifiable evidence.
- Critical gates must not be bypassed through manual status changes.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Workflow and Approvals
37. Q: How can Excel, Power Query, and VBA support grant monitoring without determining accounting policy automatically?
A: Show answer
Excel can hold structured grant, condition, source-document, movement, reconciliation, and exception tables. Power Query can import and normalize data from bank files, general-ledger extracts, project transactions, partner reports, and approved grant registers. Formula-driven checks can calculate approved-budget variance, outstanding commitments, aging, condition deadlines, and differences between source totals. VBA can prepare controlled reports, generate an evidence index, export approved outputs, and log workflow actions when its controls are designed and tested appropriately. However, no formula should decide that an ambiguous restriction is an accounting liability or that budget underspend is repayable without an approved framework and contractual conclusion. Sensitive approvals and accounting judgments remain under authorized human review. Changes to calculations, mappings, and status logic require version control, independent testing, and documented approval before release.

Study instruction:
- Build a prototype grant register and test its formulas against independently calculated values before designing any posting or payment automation.
Key point:
- Automation organizes evidence and calculations; it does not establish legal or accounting validity.
- Critical outputs require approved rules, independent testing, and human authorization.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Grant Accounting Principles
- Microsoft Learn — What Is Power Query?
- Microsoft Learn — Excel VBA Reference
38. Q: What data-validation and duplicate-detection controls should be built into the grant register?
A: Show answer
Use controlled identifiers for grants, agreements, versions, conditions, transactions, evidence, reconciliations, and exceptions. Data-validation lists can restrict fields such as status, currency, condition type, decision outcome, and authorized owner to approved values. Duplicate checks should use a combination of source ID, transaction reference, date, amount, currency, grant ID, and document type rather than one field alone. Different transactions can legitimately share a number, and the same transaction can appear in multiple systems with different references. Power Query should retain source counts and totals and produce exception records when mappings, mandatory fields, or reference relationships fail. Candidate duplicates require review instead of automatic deletion. Every transformation must preserve traceability to the original data and identify the version used in reporting or approved decisions.

Study instruction:
- Test the register using duplicate transactions, missing grant IDs, invalid statuses, and conflicting agreement versions; verify that exceptions are detected without removing valid records.
Key point:
- Controlled fields reduce inconsistent inputs and broken mappings.
- Duplicate candidates must be reviewed before records are excluded.
Source links:
- Microsoft Learn — Use Power Query to Transform Data
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Financial Data Controls
39. Q: What testing is required before a restricted-funds workbook or automated workflow is released?
A: Show answer
Testing should cover source loading, unique IDs, reference resolution, date validity, framework HOLD rules, source applicability, classification separation, balance arithmetic, condition evidence, journal handoffs, reconciliation, foreign-exchange controls, refund release gates, amendments, approval segregation, exception closure, audit trails, and closeout restrictions. Critical negative tests should verify that missing rows, invalid references, failed evidence gates, or unauthorized state transitions are rejected. Calculations should use a suitable decimal-safe approach and be compared with independently prepared results. The saved DOCX or workbook package must be inspected to confirm that required content and workflow identifiers actually persisted. Where the deliverable is a Word document, opening, saving, reopening, and rendering should be tested separately; a successful text extraction does not prove visual or schema-level correctness. Record each test's expected result, actual result, severity, evidence, defect owner, and retest status before release approval.

Study instruction:
- Create a regression-test register and demonstrate that at least one failed-evidence case, one arithmetic case, and one prohibited workflow transition are detected.
Key point:
- Successful generation is not proof of complete functional or visual acceptance.
- Failed critical tests must prevent release until resolution and retest.
Source links:
- Microsoft Learn — Excel VBA Reference
40. Q: How should the SOP be adopted, communicated, and reviewed after implementation?
A: Show answer
Adoption requires confirmation of the legal entity, applicable framework, executed agreement requirements, current internal procedures, approval authority, and source applicability. The policy owner should reconcile the final document with the approved canonical specification, controlled forms, register fields, and workflow procedures. Independent reviewers should verify critical decisions and testing results before the authorized governing body or delegated approver approves the SOP. Training should explain roles, evidence standards, HOLD conditions, escalation procedures, use of the registers, and the difference between accounting judgments and operational indicators. Assign an effective date and next-review date, distribute the approved version to affected users, and preserve obsolete versions under the retention policy. Review should also be triggered by changes to legislation, accounting standards, donor agreements, systems, internal policy, or recurring control failures. Approval does not eliminate the need to monitor whether the procedures actually operate as intended.

Study instruction:
- Prepare an adoption checklist identifying the final version, approver, effective date, training evidence, controlled repository, and triggers for periodic review.
Key point:
- An SOP becomes operative only through valid organizational approval and controlled release.
- Ongoing review is needed when authorities, agreements, systems, or processes change.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting and Accounting Policies
- IAI — Indonesian Financial Accounting Standards Effective from 1 January 2025
