Table 5
Donor Receivables and Grant Billing Controls
1. Q: What is the purpose of a donor receivables, grant billing, and cost recovery SOP in an international NGO?
A: Show answer
A donor receivables SOP establishes the controlled process for identifying potential claims, verifying contractual entitlement, preparing grant invoices or reimbursement requests, obtaining approval, submitting claims, monitoring outstanding balances, collecting funds, investigating disputes, evaluating recoverability, and reconciling accounting records. It connects programme delivery, grant management, finance, treasury, accounting, and donor reporting without allowing one process to make decisions reserved for another. A submitted claim is not automatically an accounting receivable, approved revenue, or received cash. Each status requires its own evidence and, where appropriate, a separate accounting decision. The supplied SOP 015 is explicitly a fictional demonstration, including its organization, amounts, agreements, dates, approval records, and recoverability assumptions. An actual NGO must select its accounting framework, verify applicable law, review executed donor agreements, approve delegated authority, and validate system configuration before adopting any procedure.
Study instruction:
- "Map the entire donor receivables lifecycle and distinguish operational claim processing from accounting recognition and cash receipt."
Key point:
- Claim preparation, revenue recognition, receivable recording, and cash collection are distinct activities.
- A fictional SOP is a design example, not evidence of an organization's approved policy.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
2. Q: What activities should be included in the scope of the donor receivables SOP?
A: Show answer
The scope should cover claim intake and classification, donor agreement verification, milestone acceptance, reimbursement eligibility, cost recovery, calculations, supporting evidence, review, approval, version control, submission, acknowledgment, receivable registration, aging, collection follow-up, disputes, partial receipts, recoverability assessment, adjustments, reconciliation, reporting, and controlled closure. It should also define interfaces with grant setup, programme delivery, accounts payable, tax, treasury, financial reporting, legal review, records management, and information security. Activities outside the SOP should be referred to the designated controlling process rather than absorbed informally. For example, donor receivables staff may identify a possible revenue-recognition issue, but the designated accounting authority determines the accounting treatment under the applicable framework. The SOP should identify mandatory inputs, expected outputs, responsible roles, evidence requirements, and blocking conditions for each interface.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the SOP scope with the full claim lifecycle and identify activities that require a formal handoff to another procedure."
Key point:
- Clear boundaries prevent omitted responsibilities and unauthorized decisions.
- Each process interface needs an accountable owner and documented evidence.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
3. Q: Why must the applicable accounting framework be verified before establishing donor receivable recognition and impairment rules?
A: Show answer
Recognition, measurement, impairment, presentation, and derecognition depend on the financial reporting framework applicable to the reporting entity. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual describes accounting policies and Umoja procedures for the United Nations; it is a useful reference for an NGO, but its specific rules and system procedures do not automatically become mandatory for an independent Indonesian foundation or international NGO. The organization must establish which accounting standards, approved accounting policies, donor conditions, contractual provisions, and mandatory local requirements apply. The policy owner should document the authority hierarchy, the specific requirements adopted, and any unresolved interpretations. If the framework or applicable method remains unverified, the SOP should hold the affected recognition, impairment, or write-off decision rather than inventing a default. This prevents a technically consistent spreadsheet from implementing an accounting rule that is inappropriate for the entity.
Study instruction:
- "Build an accounting-authority register that maps each receivable decision to the applicable framework, policy, agreement, and approving authority."
Key point:
- Guidance must be applicable to the reporting entity before it governs accounting treatment.
- Unresolved authority questions must remain visible and block affected decisions.
Source links:
- United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual — Accounting Manual chapters
4. Q: How should donor agreements and amendments be verified before a grant claim is prepared?
A: Show answer
Grant Management should maintain a controlled record of the executed agreement and every effective amendment. Verification should cover the donor and recipient entities, agreement identifier, funding purpose, approved budget, eligible expenditure, reporting periods, milestones, billing triggers, reimbursement conditions, permitted indirect costs, recovery rates and caps, currency, payment terms, submission channel, documentation requirements, and dispute provisions. The preparer must use the version effective for the claim period rather than a draft, expired agreement, prior budget, or informal summary. Grant Management should link the approved agreement version to the claim register and preserve the signed document and amendment history. If two documents conflict, the team must identify the controlling contractual terms and obtain an authorized decision before proceeding. Portal settings, historical practice, or a verbal assurance should not substitute for the executed agreement and formal approvals required by the organization.
Study instruction:
- "Select one proposed claim and trace each material billing parameter to the executed agreement and applicable amendment."
Key point:
- Claim entitlement and calculation rules must be based on the correct agreement version.
- Conflicting or incomplete contractual evidence requires resolution before submission.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Revenue from Non-Exchange Transactions
5. Q: How should a donor-related item be classified before it enters the claim preparation workflow?
A: Show answer
Classify the item according to its economic and contractual status. Relevant categories include scheduled instalments, milestone-based requests, reimbursement claims, cost-recovery claims, prospective or unbilled amounts, submitted claims, recognized receivables, disputed amounts, corrections, and receipt-allocation exceptions. Each category should have its own eligibility criteria, evidence, approval path, and prohibited inferences. A forecast of future funding should not automatically become a receivable. A scheduled instalment may require confirmation that its due date and contractual conditions have been satisfied. A reimbursement request requires eligible expenditure and supporting records. A disputed claim needs a separate dispute status and review. Classification should happen at intake, before calculations or status changes, and the classification decision should be recorded with the responsible owner and evidence reference.
Study instruction:
- "Classify sample grant transactions by claim type and document the evidence and prerequisites required for each classification."
Key point:
- Classification determines the correct workflow and mandatory controls.
- Similar-looking funding items can have different billing and accounting consequences.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
6. Q: What evidence is required to establish that a donor claim is contractually eligible?
A: Show answer
The evidence depends on the claim type and the donor agreement. A reimbursement claim may require invoices, proof of delivery, expenditure records, payment evidence when required, approved budgets, activity records, project coding, and confirmation that costs fall within the eligible period. A milestone claim may require deliverables, acceptance certificates, programme certification, and the agreement's specified billing trigger. Cost recovery requires an executed rate or allocation basis, a verified eligible cost base, and evidence that the claimed amount remains within contractual limits. The preparer should maintain a line-level evidence index linking each claim amount to its source. Finance review should test completeness, authenticity, period, eligibility, and consistency, not merely confirm that files are attached. Missing evidence should trigger a specific exception and corrective action, rather than an assumption that unsupported expenditure is recoverable.
Study instruction:
- "Create an evidence matrix for one reimbursement claim and test whether every material claim line is supported by the relevant contractual and operational records."
Key point:
- Evidence must substantiate the actual entitlement, not just the existence of expenditure.
- Unsupported claim lines require resolution or exclusion under an approved process.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Revenue from Non-Exchange Transactions
7. Q: How should scheduled donor instalments be distinguished from instalments that are already due and billable?
A: Show answer
A payment schedule indicates contractual timing but does not, by itself, prove that every instalment is currently billable or that all recognition conditions have been met. Grant Management should verify the executed payment schedule, the applicable agreement version, the relevant due date, any milestone or performance prerequisite, and any donor-specific documentary requirement. The claim register should distinguish future instalments, currently due instalments, submitted requests, accepted claims, overdue receivables, and amounts subject to unresolved conditions. The due-date calculation must be traceable to the contractual clause and any applicable business-day convention. Do not accelerate a future instalment into the current billing population merely because the donor has historically paid early. Likewise, do not treat a scheduled amount as revenue or a receivable without a separate determination under the applicable accounting framework.
Study instruction:
- "Reconcile a multi-instalment agreement to the claim register and verify which instalments are future, currently billable, submitted, and recognized."
Key point:
- The payment schedule, billing trigger, recognition decision, and actual receipt are separate facts.
- Future instalments must not be misrepresented as currently due claims.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Revenue from Non-Exchange Transactions
8. Q: How should a milestone-based payment request be supported before billing?
A: Show answer
The programme owner should identify the contractual milestone and provide verifiable evidence that the required deliverable, service, or performance condition has been achieved. The evidence may include an acceptance certificate, approved completion report, deliverable register, inspection record, or donor acknowledgment, depending on the agreement. Finance and Grant Management should compare the evidence to the exact milestone definition and confirm that any required certification has been given by an authorized person. A project team's internal statement that work is complete is not necessarily equivalent to contractual acceptance. If acceptance is conditional, disputed, or missing, the claim should remain on hold unless the agreement and authorized policy explicitly permit an alternative route. The accounting team must separately determine whether the evidence affects revenue or receivable recognition under the applicable framework.
Study instruction:
- "Trace one milestone claim from the contractual criterion to delivery evidence, authorized acceptance, billing approval, and the separate accounting decision."
Key point:
- Milestone achievement must be evidenced against the actual contract.
- A claim workflow must not bypass unresolved acceptance requirements.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Revenue from Non-Exchange Transactions
9. Q: How should reimbursement claims be assembled from transaction-level expenditure records?
A: Show answer
The preparer should import the authorized expenditure population, identify the applicable agreement and claim period, filter eligible transaction lines, and link each amount to a source record. Checks should cover project and cost-centre coding, expenditure dates, budget limits, supporting invoices, evidence of delivery, tax treatment, foreign-exchange requirements, allocation rules, and previous claims. Costs that are ineligible, duplicated, outside the period, insufficiently supported, or already funded by another source should be excluded or investigated according to the donor terms. The claim total should be recalculated independently from the approved line population and reconciled to the supporting schedule. Every exclusion or adjustment should remain traceable, with reasons and reviewer evidence. This produces a defensible claim and reduces the risk of claiming costs that cannot legitimately be recovered.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a sample reimbursement claim from transaction-level records and independently recalculate the total after testing eligibility and duplicate flags."
Key point:
- The claim total must reconcile to a defined, evidence-supported population.
- Eligibility screening and duplicate checks must occur before approval.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
10. Q: How should a cost-recovery claim be calculated when the donor agreement specifies rates, allocation bases, exclusions, or caps?
A: Show answer
The preparer should first identify the executed agreement version and establish the permitted cost-recovery rate, calculation base, eligible indirect cost categories, exclusions, cap, and rounding convention. The calculation base should be built from verified eligible expenditure after applying contractual exclusions and relevant adjustments. The formula should calculate the permitted recovery and compare it with any applicable ceiling; the amount claimed must not exceed the contractual entitlement. Prior recoveries and overlapping claims must also be checked so that the same cost base is not recovered twice. Finance should independently recalculate the amount and verify the source of each parameter. A percentage from a previous grant, another donor's policy, or the sample SOP must never become a universal default. Any unclear rate or base should trigger an authority hold before submission.
Study instruction:
- "Recalculate a cost-recovery claim from the executed agreement, eligible base, exclusions, prior recoveries, applicable cap, and approved rounding rule."
Key point:
- Rates and caps must be sourced from the applicable agreement rather than assumed.
- Prior recovery and overlap checks protect against duplicate or excessive claims.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
11. Q: How should double funding and duplicate expenditure claims be detected?
A: Show answer
Duplicate detection should compare the proposed claim with previous submissions, accepted claims, recognized expenditure, donor-specific funding records, other project claims, and relevant co-funding records. Matching may use the source transaction identifier, invoice number, supplier, date, currency, amount, project, funding source, and service description. A simple invoice-number match is insufficient because different suppliers may use identical numbers, and one underlying transaction may appear under different references across systems. Automated screening should identify candidates for investigation, not automatically remove every similar record. Reviewers should assess legitimate split allocations, partial claims, reversals, credit notes, and transactions supported by multiple permissible funding sources. Each resolved duplicate candidate should carry a disposition, reviewer, evidence, and link to the affected claims so that the same issue cannot reappear unnoticed.
Study instruction:
- "Test duplicate detection against previously submitted claims, alternate invoice references, split allocations, and valid credit-note adjustments."
Key point:
- Duplicate screening must consider both source-level identity and prior claim history.
- Automated matches require review before excluding a transaction or finalizing a claim.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
12. Q: What is the difference between a potential claim, a submitted claim, an accepted claim, and a recognized receivable?
A: Show answer
A potential claim is an expected or conditional funding amount that has not yet passed the relevant claim or accounting gates. A submitted claim has been dispatched through an approved channel, but submission alone does not establish donor acceptance. An accepted claim has evidence that the donor accepted the request according to the applicable process; the precise contractual and accounting implications must still be evaluated. A recognized receivable is an asset recorded after the applicable accounting recognition criteria have been met. These statuses may be related but are not interchangeable. The organization should maintain separate operational and accounting status fields and define the evidence required for each transition. A portal status or claim acknowledgment must not automatically post revenue, create a receivable, or clear an existing balance without the relevant authorized accounting decision.
Study instruction:
- "Construct a status matrix showing the required evidence and permitted actions for potential, submitted, accepted, and recognized claim amounts."
Key point:
- Operational progress does not automatically establish an accounting asset.
- Each status transition requires its own criteria and verifiable evidence.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
13. Q: How does a donor receivable differ from grant revenue or a forecast of future funding?
A: Show answer
A receivable represents an asset when the applicable accounting recognition criteria for a right to receive resources have been satisfied. Revenue represents a recognized increase in economic resources or service potential, subject to the applicable framework and any associated liabilities. A forecast represents an expected future amount and does not, by itself, create either recognized revenue or a receivable. Under the UN's non-exchange revenue policy, asset recognition and any liability associated with donor conditions affect the amount of revenue recognized; other entities must apply their own governing framework and policies. A signed agreement, performance condition, enforceability, donor restriction, or payment schedule may affect the conclusion depending on the facts. Staff should document the underlying right, conditions, amount, timing, and accounting basis rather than classify all anticipated donor funding as an asset.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a forecast, an unsubmitted claim, an accepted claim, and a recognized receivable and identify the accounting evidence needed for each."
Key point:
- Expected funding is not automatically a recognized receivable or revenue.
- Revenue and receivable decisions must follow the applicable accounting framework.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Revenue from Non-Exchange Transactions
14. Q: How should donor conditions and restrictions be evaluated before the accounting treatment is approved?
A: Show answer
The organization should examine the exact stipulation in the executed agreement and establish whether it limits how resources may be used, creates a performance obligation, requires the return of resources if a specified obligation is not met, or has another consequence under the applicable framework. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual explains a distinction between conditions and restrictions in the context of non-exchange revenue. Its detailed thresholds and treatments should not be transferred automatically to another organization. Grant Management should summarize the contractual facts and evidence, while the designated accounting authority determines the applicable treatment. The resulting decision should identify whether a receivable, revenue, liability, disclosure, or another accounting consequence is appropriate. A donor's use restriction should not automatically be described as a liability without analyzing the governing standard and the specific agreement.
Study instruction:
- "Review a grant agreement's stipulations and document the distinction between restrictions, enforceable conditions, payment terms, and accounting consequences."
Key point:
- The wording and enforceability of the agreement matter more than informal labels.
- Entity-specific accounting treatment must be verified before posting.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Revenue from Non-Exchange Transactions
15. Q: How should the donor claim approval process separate preparation, review, authorization, and submission?
A: Show answer
The preparer assembles the claim and maintains supporting evidence. An independent finance reviewer checks the agreement version, eligibility, calculations, duplicate screening, tax and foreign-exchange inputs, prior billings, and exceptions. An authorized approver then makes the decision within the applicable delegation, taking account of the amount, currency, donor, entity, and claim scope. An authorized submitter dispatches only the approved version and retains proof of dispatch. Where risk warrants, accounting recognition, receipt allocation, reconciliation, and write-off approval should also be assigned to separate responsible roles. If staffing limitations prevent full segregation, an approved compensating control must provide independent review and evidence. The system should block self-approval and require the approval reference to match the exact version submitted.
Study instruction:
- "Build a responsibility matrix for a claim and test whether the same person can prepare, approve, submit, allocate receipt, and reconcile it without independent oversight."
Key point:
- Approval must be performed by an authorized person independent of claim preparation as required by the control design.
- The approved version must be the exact version submitted to the donor.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
16. Q: What controls are required to preserve the approved claim version and prevent unauthorized changes?
A: Show answer
Every claim should have a unique identifier, version number, preparation date, preparer, reviewer, approval reference, and controlled evidence location. After approval, the claim content should be locked or otherwise protected against uncontrolled changes. If an amount, transaction population, formula, agreement parameter, or supporting evidence changes materially, the system should invalidate or suspend the prior approval and require a documented impact assessment and renewed review or approval as prescribed by policy. The previously approved version should remain retrievable rather than being overwritten. The final submitted package must be linked to the approved version, and any subsequent correction must have its own version and audit trail. Access controls, logging, and backup procedures should support the integrity of the record without relying solely on a manually typed status label.
Study instruction:
- "Simulate a material change after approval and verify that the old approval cannot authorize submission of the revised claim."
Key point:
- Approval is specific to the content and version that was reviewed.
- Material changes require controlled reapproval and a preserved history.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
17. Q: How should donor claim submission and acknowledgment be documented?
A: Show answer
The submitter should retain the approved package, submission channel, dispatch timestamp, recipient or portal reference, confirmation returned by the channel, and the version sent. Submission acknowledgment should be recorded separately from acceptance, approval, payment promises, and actual receipt. If the donor requests clarification, the correspondence should be linked to the claim and assigned to an owner with a response deadline. When the donor confirms acceptance or rejects the claim, the evidence and resulting status should be recorded according to the actual contractual process. The organization should preserve the original submission and any later revised package so that a reviewer can reconstruct what was sent and when. A status marked "submitted" without a dispatch reference or equivalent evidence should remain an exception rather than being treated as completed.
Study instruction:
- "Trace a claim from its approved version to dispatch evidence, donor acknowledgment, subsequent correspondence, and the final recorded status."
Key point:
- Dispatch and acceptance are different milestones and require different evidence.
- Each claim status must be supported by a retrievable source record.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
18. Q: What should finance do when a submission attempt has an unknown outcome?
A: Show answer
An unknown outcome occurs when the team cannot establish whether a portal or other channel received a claim, for example because the session timed out or the confirmation page failed to load. The submitter should preserve the attempt log, timestamp, package version, system message, and incident or support reference. Before retrying, an authorized person must check the submission channel and available donor confirmation to establish whether the original attempt was dispatched. If it was submitted, link the evidence to the original claim and do not create a duplicate. If it was not submitted, document the finding and follow the approved retry procedure. If status cannot be verified, keep the claim in an uncertainty status and escalate. Automatically retrying an unknown transaction can create duplicate claims, conflicting versions, and avoidable donor disputes.
Study instruction:
- "Run a test scenario with an ambiguous submission response and verify that the workflow checks prior status before authorizing a retry."
Key point:
- Unknown submission status is a controlled exception, not proof of failure.
- Duplicate prevention requires status verification before retrying.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
19. Q: How should a recognized donor receivable be entered into the receivables register?
A: Show answer
The register should identify the donor, grant or agreement, claim identifier, recognition reference, recognized gross amount, currency, contractual due date, due-date basis, reporting currency where relevant, open balance, and responsible collection owner. It should also link to the accounting decision, supporting documentation, approved claim version, and general ledger control account. The receivable should be recorded only after the authorized accounting owner determines that the applicable recognition criteria have been met. The register must distinguish the recognized amount from forecast funding, unsubmitted claims, disputed amounts, and unallocated receipts. Controlled field definitions and unique identifiers make it possible to reconcile the subledger with the general ledger and to trace each balance to its source. Subsequent movements should be recorded with references rather than overwriting the original recognized amount or history.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect a sample receivables-register entry and confirm that the balance links to a recognition decision, source claim, due-date basis, and GL reference."
Key point:
- Register inclusion must follow an authorized recognition decision.
- Each balance needs a stable identifier and traceable accounting reference.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
20. Q: How should the contractual due date and aging cutoff be determined for donor receivables?
A: Show answer
The due date should be derived from the executed agreement, approved invoice terms, valid amendment, and applicable date convention. The register should retain the source clause, calculation method, date basis, and any approved interpretation needed where the contract states a period rather than a calendar date. The aging cutoff should be set for the reporting period and applied consistently. The SOP must distinguish the date a claim is submitted, the date it is accepted, the contractual due date, the accounting recognition date, the date cash is received, and the date the accounting records are closed. These dates answer different questions. If the due-date convention or cutoff is unresolved, the affected aging report should not be labelled approved. A fictional bucket structure in a sample SOP is not a default for an actual entity.
Study instruction:
- "Recalculate due dates for several agreement clauses and compare aging results under the approved cutoff convention."
Key point:
- Aging depends on the documented contractual due date and reporting cutoff.
- Submission or recognition dates must not be substituted automatically for due dates.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
21. Q: How should an NGO calculate and report receivable aging without misclassifying open balances?
A: Show answer
Aging should be calculated from each qualifying open receivable using its verified due date and the approved reporting cutoff. The calculation should identify balances that are not yet due and those that fall into the organization's approved overdue buckets. Partial payments, credit notes, valid adjustments, disputes, and allocation corrections must be incorporated only when supported and posted or otherwise recorded under the approved process. The aging schedule should reconcile by donor and claim to the receivables subledger and GL control balance. Management should be able to distinguish gross recognized receivables, amounts already settled, unresolved disputes, and cash received but not allocated. Bucket boundaries and treatment of due dates falling on non-business days should be documented so that results remain consistent from period to period. No sample aging convention should be adopted without approval.
Study instruction:
- "Recalculate aging by donor and claim, test bucket boundaries, and reconcile gross, settled, and open balances to the register."
Key point:
- Aging must be repeatable, consistent, and tied to verified dates and balances.
- Unapplied receipts and unresolved adjustments require separate visibility.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
22. Q: What collection actions should be taken when a donor receivable becomes overdue?
A: Show answer
The receivables owner should first confirm the outstanding balance, contractual due date, submission and acknowledgment history, donor queries, and any evidence of a payment already received. Follow-up should then use the agreed donor communication channel and a documented schedule consistent with the agreement and internal policy. Each contact record should include the date, method, factual summary, response, promised next step, owner, and deadline. The owner should prioritize material, older, disputed, or higher-risk balances and escalate according to authorized thresholds. Contact activity must remain professional and should not bypass legal, safeguarding, fraud, sanctions, or privacy referrals when applicable. Management reporting should distinguish routine follow-up from unresolved disputes and actual credit-risk concerns. A reminder sent is evidence of an action, not proof that the debt has become collectible.
Study instruction:
- "Review an overdue receivable and prepare a collection action plan with evidence, owner, target date, and escalation criteria."
Key point:
- Follow-up must be documented and prioritized according to contract, risk, and materiality.
- Collection activity does not replace the separate assessment of recoverability.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
23. Q: Why must a donor's promise to pay be distinguished from an actual cash receipt?
A: Show answer
A promise to pay records a donor commitment or communication about expected settlement. It is not evidence that funds have entered the organization's bank account, and it should not reduce the receivable balance as though cash had been received. The receivables owner should record the promised amount, date, contact reference, expected settlement date, and follow-up responsibility. If the promise is missed, the account should return to the appropriate collection status and the missed commitment should be escalated according to policy. The cash function should reduce the receivable only after verifying the actual receipt and establishing the allocation. If a promise reflects a legal modification, disputed settlement, or waiver, the accounting and legal implications must be considered separately rather than inferred from collection notes alone.
Study instruction:
- "Trace a promised payment through the collection log and verify that the receivable remains open until actual settlement is independently evidenced."
Key point:
- A payment promise is not cash and does not by itself clear a receivable.
- Settlement commitments and accounting movements need separate evidence.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
24. Q: How should donor queries, rejected claims, and disputed amounts be managed?
A: Show answer
Record the donor communication, claim identifier, issue category, affected amount, response deadline, responsible owner, supporting evidence, decision history, and current status in a controlled dispute register. Separate the affected amount from undisputed balances so management can assess the open position without double counting or hiding the dispute. Grant Management should establish what contractual or documentary issue is being challenged, while finance assesses the accounting consequences and legal or specialist teams advise on matters outside ordinary processing. A rejected claim should not be silently removed from the records if an asset has already been recognized or if a correction, appeal, or other action remains possible. Responses and resubmissions should use a versioned package and appropriate approval. Every closure should state the outcome and preserve evidence of acceptance, withdrawal, correction, settlement, or another authorized disposition.
Study instruction:
- "Select a disputed claim and trace the donor query, affected balance, response deadline, decision authority, revised submission, and accounting consequences."
Key point:
- Disputes need explicit ownership, deadlines, and documented outcomes.
- A rejected or disputed claim may require an accounting review rather than a routine status change.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
25. Q: How should partial donor payments and short payments be investigated?
A: Show answer
Verify the amount received from independent bank evidence and compare it with the relevant remittance advice, donor reference, claim, currency, value date, and any agreed deductions. Determine whether the donor made an authorized partial payment, applied a permitted contractual deduction, withheld an amount under dispute, paid bank charges, or sent funds that cannot yet be identified. Allocate only the supported portion to the relevant receivable. Keep any difference visible as an open balance, a disputed deduction, a bank-charge matter, or another appropriately classified item under the approved policy. Do not close the receivable because the incoming cash appears reasonable or because a donor's email says payment is complete. Reconciliation and any required accounting adjustment should be independently reviewed before final closure.
Study instruction:
- "Reconcile a short payment from bank evidence through remittance advice, claim allocation, remaining balance, and approved adjustment if applicable."
Key point:
- Partial cash receipt reduces a claim only by the verified amount properly allocated.
- Unexplained differences remain open exceptions until resolved or formally accounted for.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
26. Q: How should unidentified or unapplied donor cash be recorded and controlled?
A: Show answer
The cash team should independently verify the bank receipt, including amount, currency, value date, account, and bank reference. If the donor or claim cannot yet be identified, retain the cash in the organization's approved unapplied-receipt or suspense process, with a responsible owner, investigation date, supporting evidence, and follow-up deadline. Do not arbitrarily allocate the amount to the oldest receivable merely to clear aging or force the subledger to reconcile. When remittance information becomes available, match the payment to the correct claim or claims and retain the allocation review. If an amount spans multiple invoices, the allocation should follow authoritative remittance instructions and applicable policy. Unresolved cash must be reported separately from receivables, and material or aged items should be escalated rather than repeatedly rolled forward without explanation.
Study instruction:
- "Trace an unidentified bank receipt from verification to suspense, donor identification, approved allocation, and reconciliation."
Key point:
- Unallocated cash is not the same as a settled, correctly allocated receivable.
- Suspense items require ownership, aging, investigation, and documented resolution.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
27. Q: How should receivable impairment and expected-credit-loss assessments be performed?
A: Show answer
The accounting owner must first establish the measurement and impairment requirements of the applicable reporting framework. The assessment should consider the nature of the receivable, counterparty, contractual rights, aging, payment history, disputes, donor correspondence, subsequent receipts, known financial or operational developments, and other relevant evidence at the reporting date. Material or individually higher-risk balances may require specific assessment, while an approved framework may also require collective assessment of similar exposures. Methods, assumptions, data sources, calculation dates, review steps, and approvals should be documented. The organization should distinguish an accounting allowance from the operational process of pursuing collection. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual contains United Nations-specific guidance on receivables impairment, but its historical percentages or system procedures must not be copied into another entity's books without confirming applicability and current policy.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a sample recoverability workpaper that identifies the applicable framework, source evidence, assessment method, assumptions, and independent review."
Key point:
- Impairment must follow the applicable accounting framework and reporting-date evidence.
- Aging informs assessment but does not replace judgment or an approved methodology.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
28. Q: What is the difference between an individual impairment assessment and a collective assessment of receivables?
A: Show answer
An individual assessment focuses on a particular exposure when its circumstances, size, or risk profile warrant separate analysis under the applicable framework. A collective assessment considers a group of receivables with similar credit-risk characteristics using an approved method and relevant historical or current evidence. The organization must follow the requirements of its applicable accounting policy rather than assume every receivable belongs exclusively in one approach. A control is required to prevent an exposure already assessed and provided for individually from being counted again in a collective calculation when the framework or method prohibits that overlap. The workpaper should identify the receivables included, excluded items, grouping criteria, assumptions, calculation, and reviewer approval. Age, missed promises, donor disputes, or known financial difficulties may trigger investigation but do not independently dictate a universal allowance percentage.
Study instruction:
- "Reconcile the receivables included in individual and collective assessments and test for overlap or omission under the approved methodology."
Key point:
- Individual and collective assessments must follow the applicable framework and method.
- Double counting and unsupported allowance percentages must be prevented.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
29. Q: Why must hypothetical recoverability percentages in an example SOP never be posted directly as an accounting allowance?
A: Show answer
A demonstration percentage illustrates a calculation or sensitivity; it does not establish an organization's approved impairment model. Before an allowance is recognized, the accounting team must verify the applicable framework, scope of receivables, recognition and measurement rules, relevant evidence, calculation method, assumptions, reporting date, and required approvals. A rate shown in fictional documentation may be intentionally illustrative or may belong to another entity's policy. Copying it into a live ledger can cause a material misstatement even if the arithmetic is correct. The workpaper should distinguish scenario analysis from the approved accounting calculation and mark unverified parameters as non-bookable. Changes to the method, assumptions, or data population should be versioned and independently reviewed before the accounting entry is authorized.
Study instruction:
- "Review a hypothetical allowance worksheet and identify every item that must be verified before it can support a real journal entry."
Key point:
- Illustrative sensitivity rates are not automatically valid accounting estimates.
- No allowance should be posted without an approved method and sufficient supporting evidence.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
30. Q: What information should the accountant consider when a previously reliable donor becomes slow to pay or disputes a claim?
A: Show answer
The accountant should reassess the exposure using updated evidence rather than relying only on past payment behavior. Relevant factors may include aging, overdue commitments, the donor's reasons for withholding payment, changes in the grant's status, formal rejection or disallowance, remaining contractual remedies, subsequent receipts, and any evidence that changes the probability or expected amount of recovery. Grant Management should provide the agreement and correspondence; the collection owner should provide the contact history; and accounting should document its separate recoverability conclusion. The organization must avoid assuming that every disputed balance is entirely irrecoverable or that every donor balance is fully recoverable because the donor is reputable. Any allowance adjustment should follow the approved framework, materiality threshold, review, and journal authorization requirements.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the evidence for a current donor balance with the evidence available after a missed payment commitment or formal claim dispute."
Key point:
- Updated facts can change recoverability even when the original claim was correctly recognized.
- A collection escalation and an impairment decision are related but distinct controls.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
31. Q: How should foreign currency and exchange differences be managed for donor claims and receipts?
A: Show answer
The team should preserve the original transaction currency and amount, agreement currency, approved exchange-rate source, applicable date convention, translated amount, reporting currency, rounding rule, and relevant supporting reference. The correct date and rate may depend on the agreement, transaction, accounting framework, and type of calculation. Claim calculation, donor billing, bank receipt allocation, and period-end financial reporting can require different but documented steps. Treasury or the designated rate owner should maintain approved rate inputs, while accounting should determine the treatment of exchange differences and foreign-currency balances. Reviewers should test translated totals, partial receipts, currency mismatches, and rate changes independently. No rate should be copied from a previous claim merely because the donor or currency is the same; the governing convention and reporting date must be verified for the specific transaction.
Study instruction:
- "Reperform a foreign-currency claim and partial receipt using the documented agreement, approved rate source, date basis, and reporting-currency reconciliation."
Key point:
- Foreign-currency calculations need traceable rates and date conventions.
- Billing calculations and financial-reporting translation must follow their applicable rules.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
32. Q: How should taxes and tax documentation be considered when preparing a donor claim or cost-recovery request?
A: Show answer
The preparer should determine whether the source expenditure includes VAT or another tax, whether any tax is recoverable, whether the donor agreement permits the cost to be claimed, and whether transaction-specific tax requirements affect the amount or supporting documentation. An NGO's nonprofit status does not automatically establish a tax exemption, and an expense that is valid in the accounting ledger is not automatically eligible under the donor agreement. Tax specialists should assess local law, transaction facts, exemption evidence, and required tax documents; Grant Management should determine donor eligibility; accounting should determine the appropriate ledger treatment. The claim calculation should distinguish gross source expenditure, recoverable tax, non-recoverable tax, and any amount excluded under donor terms. Unsupported assumptions must be escalated before the claim is finalized.
Study instruction:
- "Select a tax-sensitive expenditure line and trace the tax analysis, donor eligibility decision, calculation, and reviewer approval."
Key point:
- Tax treatment and donor recoverability are separate decisions.
- Exemptions and eligible claim amounts require applicable evidence and authority.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
33. Q: How should credit notes, concessions, settlements, and write-offs be controlled?
A: Show answer
These actions can change the organization's contractual rights, recognized balance, donor relationship, and financial statements, so they require distinct reasons and authorization rather than a routine status update. The request should identify the affected claim, amount, original recognition reference, reason, remaining rights, contractual basis, donor communication, proposed accounting effect, tax implications if relevant, and delegated authority. Legal or specialist review may be required where the decision changes rights, resolves a dispute, or involves settlement terms. Accounting should determine the appropriate treatment under the applicable framework, and an independent reviewer should verify that the proposed action matches the approval. A write-off should not be used merely to remove an old or inconvenient balance from an aging report. The original record and complete approval history must remain retrievable after the adjustment is posted.
Study instruction:
- "Trace one proposed credit or write-off from the reason and supporting authority through independent review, approval, journal, and updated reconciliation."
Key point:
- Credit notes, concessions, settlements, and write-offs are controlled decisions with different consequences.
- An unapproved adjustment must not change the recognized balance.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
34. Q: How should donor receivables be reconciled between the claim register, receivables subledger, general ledger, and bank records?
A: Show answer
Establish a periodic reconciliation that compares the claim register's recognized gross amount, valid adjustments, verified applied receipts, unapplied cash, and closing open balance with the receivables subledger and GL control accounts. Differences should be analyzed by claim, donor, period, currency, posting reference, and transaction status. Common causes include an omitted journal, duplicate invoice, incorrect payment allocation, unposted credit, foreign-exchange movement, cutoff difference, or an amount incorrectly treated as both receivable and suspense cash. The preparer should document each variance, the corrective action, the supporting entry or evidence, and the resolution date. A reviewer independent of the preparation should confirm that material differences are resolved or formally escalated before close sign-off. Reconciliation should not force totals to agree by means of unsupported journal adjustments.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a month-end reconciliation from the claim register to subledger, GL, bank receipts, and unapplied cash, and resolve each material difference."
Key point:
- Reconciliation must explain the movement and composition of balances, not just match totals.
- Unsupported adjustments must never be used to conceal unresolved differences.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
35. Q: What should the month-end donor receivables close include?
A: Show answer
The close should establish the cutoff date and reconcile opening balances, current-period recognitions, invoices or approved claims, cash receipts, allocations, credits, adjustments, foreign-exchange movements, allowance entries, and closing balances. The team should review overdue items, unsubmitted or rejected claims, pending donor queries, unidentified cash, stale suspense items, and any missing source documents. Accounting must confirm that recognition, impairment, derecognition, and presentation follow the approved framework, while Grant Management confirms the claim and agreement records. The period-end package should retain the aging schedule, reconciliation, exception register, material estimates, approvals, supporting journals, and evidence of independent review. Items still open at close need owners, target dates, and documented disposition; they should not disappear merely because the reporting deadline has arrived.
Study instruction:
- "Use a month-end checklist to trace all opening balances and movements to the closing receivables schedule, GL, evidence, and approval records."
Key point:
- Month-end close combines claim monitoring, accounting, cash allocation, and reconciliation controls.
- Outstanding exceptions require a recorded decision and accountable follow-up.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
36. Q: What information should a donor receivables management report contain?
A: Show answer
The report should show opening and closing recognized balances, new claims, amounts submitted and accepted, cash applied, remaining open balances, aging by approved buckets, significant disputed amounts, collection commitments, unapplied receipts, expected next actions, and any approved impairment or adjustment information relevant to the reporting framework. Each total should reconcile to the register and GL or have a documented explanation. The report should distinguish recognized receivables from prospective claims and should not combine accepted-but-unpaid claims with unsubmitted forecasts without clear definitions. Management needs visibility into material overdue amounts, recurring donor queries, concentration of exposure, approaching contractual deadlines, and decisions that require escalation. Every status and KPI needs a defined source, cutoff, calculation method, owner, and review. A dashboard is an oversight tool, not proof that underlying claims or balances are valid.
Study instruction:
- "Design a management report and verify that each balance, aging indicator, and exception metric can be reproduced from the underlying registers and reconciliations."
Key point:
- Management reports must be reconcilable and use clearly defined statuses.
- Forecasts, submitted claims, recognized receivables, and receipts must remain distinguishable.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
37. Q: How can Excel and Power Query support donor claim preparation and receivables monitoring without replacing accounting judgment?
A: Show answer
Power Query can import authorized expenditure files, claim registers, agreement metadata, receipt records, and approved exchange-rate tables into structured datasets. Transformations can standardize columns, check required fields, identify possible duplicates, apply controlled mappings, and prepare exception lists. Excel tables and formulas can support repeatable claim calculations, aging schedules, and reconciliations. However, the accounting framework, agreement terms, rate parameters, eligibility rules, and recognition decisions must come from verified sources and authorized owners. The workbook should log source filenames, extraction dates, row counts, amount totals, transformation versions, exceptions, and refresh status. Test calculations against independent samples and retain approved reference results. Do not let a successful refresh or formula output automatically authorize submission, recognize revenue, post an allowance, or clear a receipt.
Study instruction:
- "Build a test dataset containing missing fields, duplicate candidates, revised claims, and partial receipts, then compare automated outputs against independently calculated results."
Key point:
- Automation improves consistency and exception detection but does not establish entitlement or accounting authority.
- Input completeness, transformations, calculations, and output status must be independently verifiable.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
38. Q: What data-validation and master-data controls are necessary for an automated donor receivables workbook?
A: Show answer
Maintain controlled master lists for donor identifiers, grant IDs, agreement versions, eligible cost categories, project codes, currencies, claim types, status values, responsible roles, and approved parameter references. Use unique identifiers and explicit effective dates so that historical records are not silently recalculated under new terms. Validate required fields, data types, date ranges, valid combinations, negative amounts, duplicates, and references to missing records before any claim or report is produced. Changes to donor terms, cost-recovery parameters, due dates, or approval limits should be logged with the old value, new value, source, effective date, owner, approver, and impacted claims. The workbook must produce actionable exception messages rather than substituting defaults for missing information. Data-validation lists support controlled entry, but reviewer checks and reconciliation are still needed to confirm that the source facts are correct.
Study instruction:
- "Test the master-data controls by entering invalid donor IDs, inactive agreement versions, unsupported currencies, and missing mandatory fields."
Key point:
- Valid formats do not prove that transaction data or contractual parameters are substantively correct.
- Master-data changes require authorization, effective dating, and impact assessment.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
39. Q: How should exceptions and corrective actions be controlled throughout the donor receivables lifecycle?
A: Show answer
Each exception should have a unique reference, category, date detected, affected claim and amount, severity, root cause where established, accountable owner, required decision, next action, deadline, and closure evidence. Examples include missing agreement amendments, unsupported expenditure, unresolved duplicate candidates, invalid calculations, unknown submission outcomes, overdue donor responses, unallocated receipts, unexplained reconciliation differences, or disputed accounting authority. A blocker should prevent the affected workflow from advancing past a mandatory gate. Escalation must follow the approved severity and delegation rules. Closing an exception requires a documented disposition, sufficient evidence, any necessary correction, an appropriate retest, and approval by the authorized closure role. Moving an item to the next reporting period is not resolution. Repeated exceptions should also feed process improvement and management review.
Study instruction:
- "Review a sample exception log and verify that every closed critical item has a root-cause or disposition record, evidence, required retesting, and authorized closure."
Key point:
- Exceptions need owners, deadlines, evidence, and an explicit closure decision.
- Critical blockers must not be bypassed merely to complete a claim or reporting cycle.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
40. Q: What controls are required for records retention, privacy, access security, and audit trails in donor receivables processing?
A: Show answer
The organization should retain executed agreements, amendments, claim versions, source expenditure records, approvals, donor correspondence, submission evidence, collection records, receipt allocations, accounting workpapers, reconciliation, and closure decisions in a controlled repository. Access should follow role-based need and least privilege, with approval and periodic review of privileged users, consultants, and users whose roles have changed. Audit logs should record relevant changes to claim amounts, agreement parameters, approval states, accounting decisions, and allocation records. Records must be protected against unauthorized alteration or deletion, and retention schedules should reflect applicable law, donor requirements, contracts, privacy rules, and organizational policy. A backup is not sufficient unless restoration is periodically tested. Potential fraud, sanctions, legal matters, or privacy incidents should be routed to the appropriate specialist process, with access restrictions and evidence preservation as required.
Study instruction:
- "Trace one claim through the repository and test permission controls, version history, change logs, retention requirements, and recovery of supporting evidence."
Key point:
- Auditability depends on preserving both transaction evidence and the history of decisions.
- Retention, access, security, and legal holds must follow verified requirements.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
41. Q: How should a complete end-to-end walkthrough test whether the donor receivables SOP works in practice?
A: Show answer
Select a completed claim and follow the evidence from the executed agreement and source transactions through claim classification, calculation, review, approval, version lock, submission, donor acknowledgment, accounting recognition, receivable registration, collection, receipt allocation, reconciliation, and closure. Record the responsible role, dates, references, system used, evidence observed, and the decision made at each stage. Compare the transaction count and monetary totals between steps, and include cases such as a partial receipt, query, amended submission, or unresolved item when available. Distinguish documents actually inspected from explanations received verbally. Where the observed process differs from the SOP, record the gap, risk, required policy or control change, decision authority, and implementation target. A walkthrough is only evidence of the steps tested; it does not by itself prove that the control operated effectively in every period.
Study instruction:
- "Perform an end-to-end walkthrough of a completed claim and document every missing handoff, unsupported status change, reconciliation difference, and control gap."
Key point:
- Process design must be compared with actual evidence and observed execution.
- A walkthrough identifies design and operating issues but does not automatically provide assurance over all transactions.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
42. Q: How should management evaluate whether donor receivables controls remain effective over time?
A: Show answer
Management should monitor indicators such as on-time claim submission, percentage of claims returned for correction, unsupported or ineligible amounts identified, overdue balances by approved aging bucket, time to resolve donor queries, unapplied cash aging, unreconciled differences, repeat exceptions, and time required to close the period. Each indicator needs a stable definition, data source, cutoff, calculation method, accountable owner, target where approved, and escalation rule. Trends should be assessed alongside materiality and underlying causes rather than interpreted in isolation. For example, fewer overdue claims may be caused by a change in claim population or classification rather than improved collection performance. The report should distinguish actual results from estimates and preserve evidence that critical exceptions have been addressed. Management should approve corrective actions and assess whether changes to agreements, systems, staffing, training, or policies are required.
Study instruction:
- "Define a set of receivables-control indicators and independently verify the source population and calculation for each metric."
Key point:
- KPIs need controlled definitions and evidence that the source data is complete.
- Management should use trends to investigate root causes and improve controls, not merely report percentages.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
43. Q: What should happen when a donor receivables process encounters a suspected fraud, sanctions concern, suspicious remittance, or privacy incident?
A: Show answer
The ordinary billing or collections workflow should stop or be restricted to the extent needed to protect the investigation, evidence, people, and legal obligations. Staff should follow the organization's authorized referral process and notify the designated legal, compliance, security, safeguarding, or privacy role as appropriate. Preserve relevant records, communication, timestamps, transaction references, access logs, and supporting documents under the applicable retention or legal-hold rules. Do not make unsupported accusations, delete evidence, change the accounting record to hide the issue, or allow ordinary collection activity to compromise the review. The specialist owner should determine the required investigation, reporting, access restrictions, and next steps. Accounting treatment of the underlying balance remains a separate decision made by the authorized accounting owner using the facts and applicable requirements.
Study instruction:
- "Review the specialist-referral procedure and identify which actions, evidence-preservation steps, and communications are authorized when an integrity or privacy concern is raised."
Key point:
- Sensitive incidents require the appropriate specialist route rather than routine claim processing alone.
- Evidence preservation and controlled access must accompany the referral.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
44. Q: How should an NGO distinguish accounting receivables from contingent assets or other uncertain potential recoveries?
A: Show answer
The accounting team should first establish the nature and enforceability of the right, the applicable recognition criteria, the amount and timing of expected resources, and any unresolved conditions or uncertainties. A possible recovery, disputed entitlement, unaccepted proposal, or prospective reimbursement may require monitoring and disclosure analysis without necessarily qualifying for recognition as a receivable. The conclusion depends on the relevant framework and specific facts; the organization should not assume that every potential asset should be recognized merely because the amount can be estimated. Maintain a separate register for uncertain opportunities or claims that have not passed the recognition gate, and link the item to its owner, trigger, expected decision date, and evidence. When new information changes the assessment, accounting should document whether recognition, disclosure, or no accounting entry is appropriate.
Study instruction:
- "Compare an established contractual receivable with an uncertain potential recovery and document the framework-specific recognition or disclosure conclusion."
Key point:
- Potential recoveries require analysis before being recognized as accounting assets.
- Monitoring an unrecognized claim must not create a recognized balance by default.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Revenue from Non-Exchange Transactions
45. Q: What documentation is needed to approve the adoption of a donor receivables SOP for an actual NGO?
A: Show answer
Adoption requires more than a completed document. The organization should identify the policy owner, independent reviewer, authorized approver, effective date, next review date, document version, applicable jurisdiction and framework, donor-agreement requirements, delegated authorities, system owners, and staff training responsibilities. Every unresolved parameter, including recognition criteria, impairment methodology, due-date conventions, aging buckets, foreign-exchange policy, cost-recovery terms, retention rules, and approval limits, should have an owner and a resolution path. Actual claims, sample calculations, workflow transitions, and authorization controls should be tested before release. The approved version should be distinguishable from drafts and from fictional examples, and staff should receive practical training on the required evidence and escalation rules. The organization should retain formal approval and release evidence and periodically reassess the SOP when policy, law, agreements, or systems change.
Study instruction:
- "Complete an adoption checklist and identify the authority, parameter, system, test, approval, and training evidence required before operational release."
Key point:
- A controlled draft is not an approved organizational policy.
- Adoption requires verified authority, tested workflows, authorized approval, and documented release.
Source links:
- United Nations IPSAS Finance Manual — Accounting policy reference
