Table 6
Project Expenditure Coding and Controls
1. Q: What is the purpose of a project expenditure-coding SOP in an international NGO?
A: Show answer
A project expenditure-coding SOP establishes how every expenditure is classified, assigned to the correct project and funding source, approved, recorded, reconciled, and reported. Its objective is to ensure that financial transactions reflect their actual economic nature, the activity that benefited from the expenditure, the applicable budget, and any relevant donor restrictions. A coding procedure should define the chart of accounts, project and grant identifiers, cost centers, activity codes, decision rules, required source documents, approval responsibilities, exception handling, and reporting controls. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual provides a useful technical reference for transaction coding and general-ledger processing, although its Umoja-specific structures must not be assumed to apply directly to every NGO. The supplied DOCX could not be independently retrieved in this session; this resource therefore develops the topic using the document title and verified official reference material rather than claiming to reproduce the document's contents.

Study instruction:
- "Map one expenditure from its originating document through project coding, general-ledger posting, approval, reconciliation, and donor reporting."

Key point:
- Coding connects accounting classification with project accountability.
- The SOP must reflect approved organizational policies, grant terms, and applicable accounting requirements.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 2 — Umoja Coding Block
2. Q: What is project expenditure coding, and how does it differ from ordinary general-ledger coding?
A: Show answer
General-ledger coding identifies the accounting account used to classify a transaction, such as travel expense, professional services, salaries, or equipment. Project expenditure coding adds dimensions that identify which project, grant, donor, department, activity, or cost center is associated with the transaction. A consultancy invoice, for example, may be recorded as professional services while also being assigned to a specific project and grant. Both dimensions are necessary: the general-ledger account supports financial-statement classification, while the project dimensions support budget monitoring, funding accountability, and operational reporting. A correct project code cannot compensate for an incorrect expense account, and a correct expense account cannot compensate for charging the wrong project. The organization should maintain separate definitions and validation rules for each coding dimension.

Study instruction:
- "Classify three example transactions by general-ledger account, project, grant, and activity, explaining the purpose of each field."

Key point:
- General-ledger coding identifies the nature of the transaction.
- Project coding identifies the funding and operational context.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 2 — Coding Block
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
3. Q: Which coding dimensions should an NGO consider when designing its project expenditure structure?
A: Show answer
Depending on the organization's reporting needs, a coding structure may include general-ledger account, legal entity, fund, donor, grant agreement, project, activity, budget line, department, cost center, location, currency, and fiscal period. Each dimension should serve a defined purpose and have an assigned owner. Avoid adding overlapping fields that produce conflicting interpretations or unnecessary data-entry complexity. The organization must distinguish mandatory dimensions from optional ones and define which combinations are valid for each transaction type. A project expense might require a project code and an expense account, while a shared administrative cost may require a cost center and an approved allocation reference. The appropriate structure depends on the accounting system, donor reporting obligations, organizational policies, and the granularity needed for reliable financial analysis. UN coding-block guidance illustrates how multiple dimensions can support different financial reporting views, but its precise configuration is specific to the UN environment.

Study instruction:
- "Create a coding-dimension matrix showing each field's definition, permitted values, owner, mandatory status, and reporting purpose."

Key point:
- Every coding dimension should have a clearly defined purpose.
- Valid combinations matter as much as valid individual codes.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 2 — Coding Block Elements
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual — Glossary
4. Q: How should the chart of accounts be distinguished from the project and grant master lists?
A: Show answer
The chart of accounts defines the accounting classifications available for recording assets, liabilities, net assets or equity as applicable, revenue, and expenses. Project and grant master lists identify individual funded activities, agreements, and related operational attributes. These lists should be related through approved mappings rather than combined into one uncontrolled list of account names and project descriptions. For example, a project may legitimately use several expense accounts, but an equipment expense should not be posted to a travel account merely because the project budget has a travel line available. Conversely, the presence of a valid expense account does not authorize charging every project. A controlled mapping should establish which accounts and expenditure categories are permitted for each project where restrictions apply. Accounting accounts must remain consistent with the applicable financial reporting framework.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the chart of accounts with a sample project master list and identify which combinations require restrictions or additional approval."

Key point:
- Account classification and project identification serve different functions.
- Controlled mappings prevent technically valid but inappropriate combinations.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 2 — Coding Block and Account Structure
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Master Data
5. Q: What information should be maintained in a project master-data register?
A: Show answer
A project master-data register should contain a unique project identifier, project title, responsible manager, implementing location, start and end dates, approved funding sources, donor or grant references, budget version, permitted expenditure categories, relevant cost centers, reporting currency, and applicable approval status. Additional fields may include project restrictions, reporting frequency, financial close requirements, and links to the approved agreement and budget. The register should distinguish approved, pending, closed, and suspended projects and establish the transactions permitted under each status. A closed project should not automatically remain available for new expenditure coding without an authorized reopening decision. Historical identifiers should remain traceable so that previous transactions can still be understood after project names or management structures change. Sensitive agreement details should be access-controlled.

Study instruction:
- "Inspect five project records and test whether each can be linked to an approved budget, funding agreement, accountable manager, and valid operating period."

Key point:
- Master data supports consistent coding and reporting.
- Status and effective dates prevent inappropriate transactions against inactive projects.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Master Data
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 2 — Coding Block
6. Q: How should an NGO determine whether an expenditure can be charged to a particular donor-funded project?
A: Show answer
The review must consider the nature of the expenditure, the activity that benefited, the approved project budget, the donor agreement, applicable cost eligibility rules, and the organization's financial policies. A cost should not be charged to a donor merely because sufficient budget remains or because the project appears to be related to the transaction. The finance team should verify the purpose, timing, beneficiary, supporting evidence, allocation basis where relevant, and any restrictions on the type of expenditure or recovery of costs. Special attention is required for prohibited expenditure, costs outside the grant period, costs already reimbursed elsewhere, and expenditure subject to prior approval. Where the agreement or local rules do not provide a clear answer, the transaction should be escalated to the authorized grant or finance specialist rather than resolved through an unsupported assumption.

Study instruction:
- "Choose three proposed expenses and assess eligibility using the agreement, approved budget, transaction evidence, and applicable organizational policy."

Key point:
- Budget availability does not automatically establish donor eligibility.
- Eligibility conclusions require documented agreement and policy checks.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Revenue and Funding Agreements
7. Q: What is the difference between budget availability, expenditure recognition, and donor cost eligibility?
A: Show answer
Budget availability indicates the amount of approved spending capacity remaining under the relevant budget controls. Expenditure recognition concerns when and how the transaction should be recorded under the applicable accounting framework. Donor cost eligibility concerns whether the cost can be charged to the particular funding arrangement. These are separate tests. A valid invoice may represent a recognized expense even though the donor will not reimburse it. An eligible planned purchase may still be a commitment rather than a recognized expense if the applicable accounting recognition requirements have not been met. A transaction can also be correctly recognized in the accounts but incorrectly coded to a grant. The finance process should therefore verify these conditions independently and document any differences between accounting expense, budget consumption, and expenditure accepted for donor reporting.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a three-column decision analysis for a budgeted purchase, a received invoice, and a donor-disallowed cost."

Key point:
- Accounting recognition, budget control, and donor eligibility are not interchangeable.
- Each test requires its own evidence and conclusion.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses and Fund Commitments
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Statements and Disclosures
8. Q: How should commitments be distinguished from actual project expenditure?
A: Show answer
A commitment generally represents a spending obligation or reserved budget arising from an approved procurement or other arrangement. Actual expenditure represents a transaction recognized under the applicable accounting requirements when the relevant recognition criteria are satisfied. The exact timing depends on the transaction and supporting facts. For example, an approved purchase order may consume available budget without immediately creating the same expense as a completed delivery and accepted supplier invoice. The budget-control process should therefore track commitments and actual expenditure separately, while the accounting process records the appropriate assets, liabilities, expenses, or other balances. Reports must define whether they display commitments, actuals, or a combination. Otherwise, managers may interpret a combined amount as recognized expense even though some items have not yet been recognized in the general ledger.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a purchase from budget reservation through purchase order, delivery, invoice recognition, and payment, identifying the balance reported at each stage."

Key point:
- Commitments help control future spending; actuals reflect recognized transactions.
- Reporting definitions must prevent double counting commitments and actual expenses.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses and Fund Commitments
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Processing
9. Q: At which stage should project expenditure coding be assigned and verified?
A: Show answer
Coding should begin as early as the transaction's purpose and funding source can be established reliably, often when a requisition, purchase request, travel authorization, or project activity is initiated. The initial code should then be verified against the purchase order or authorization, supporting invoice, receipt or service evidence, and final accounting entry. Early coding helps procurement and project managers assess budget availability before committing funds, while subsequent verification prevents an originally reasonable estimate from becoming an incorrect accounting classification. Some transactions may require a coding decision at invoice receipt because their final nature or beneficiary was not known earlier. The SOP should define each point at which coding is entered, validated, approved, and finalized, along with the required evidence and responsibility for correcting errors.

Study instruction:
- "Map the coding control points for procurement, payroll, travel, and supplier invoices, and explain what information is available at each stage."

Key point:
- Early coding supports authorization and budget control.
- Final posting must be checked against the actual transaction and evidence.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expense Processing
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Processes
10. Q: What supporting documents should be required before project expenditure is posted?
A: Show answer
The evidence depends on the transaction type but may include an approved requisition, purchase order or contract, supplier invoice, delivery note, service acceptance record, travel authorization, attendance record, payroll calculation, timesheet, expense claim, proof of receipt, or evidence of project activity. The file should also establish the business purpose, amount, transaction date, supplier or beneficiary, appropriate approval, and intended project or activity. Where a donor requires additional documentation, such as prior approval or specific procurement evidence, those requirements must be incorporated into the checklist. A missing document should result in a clearly defined hold, exception, or authorized alternative-evidence procedure. Finance should not treat a complete coding field as a substitute for evidence that the underlying transaction occurred and is properly authorized.

Study instruction:
- "Build an evidence matrix for supplier invoices, travel, payroll, consultancy, equipment, and shared costs."

Key point:
- Documentation supports occurrence, authorization, classification, and eligibility.
- Missing evidence requires resolution or documented escalation.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expense Documentation and Processing
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Controls
11. Q: How should direct project costs be identified and coded?
A: Show answer
Direct costs are costs that can be specifically attributed to a project or activity using a reasonable and supportable relationship. Examples may include project-specific consultant services, field travel, materials delivered to a project site, and personnel costs for staff working exclusively on the project. The organization should identify the cost's actual purpose and beneficiary, then select the correct natural expense account, project, grant, activity, and any other required dimensions. The direct-cost designation must be supported by evidence, such as a contract, purchase order, travel authorization, service record, or timesheet where relevant. Costs that benefit multiple projects should not be assigned entirely to one project merely because it is administratively convenient. The appropriate treatment must also comply with donor agreements and internal cost-allocation policy.

Study instruction:
- "Review a sample of direct-cost postings and test whether the supporting documents justify the project assignment."

Key point:
- Direct coding requires a demonstrable connection to the benefiting project.
- Cost allocation must not be replaced by arbitrary charging.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 2 — Coding Block
12. Q: How should shared costs and indirect costs be allocated across projects?
A: Show answer
Shared costs benefit more than one project, department, or activity and cannot always be attributed directly to one recipient. An allocation should use an approved, reasonable, consistently applied basis that reflects the consumption or benefit of the cost, such as documented staff time, usage, headcount, occupied space, or another demonstrably appropriate driver. The correct driver depends on the nature of the cost, available evidence, donor restrictions, and organizational policy. The allocation schedule should show the original cost, allocation pool, eligible recipients, driver data, calculation, resulting amounts, review, and approval. Where donor rules prohibit recovery or impose special limits on indirect costs, those restrictions take precedence over a purely mathematical allocation. An allocation percentage must not be selected simply because it makes the project budget balance.

Study instruction:
- "Calculate a sample shared-cost allocation and justify why the selected driver reasonably reflects the benefit received by each project."

Key point:
- Allocation methods need a defensible economic basis.
- The full allocation must reconcile to the original cost without duplication.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Cost Allocations
13. Q: How can finance prevent the same cost from being charged twice to different projects or donors?
A: Show answer
Use a unique transaction reference together with supplier, invoice number, date, amount, currency, purchase order, expense category, project, and funding source. Duplicate detection should consider both exact duplicates and likely duplicates that differ slightly in reference formatting or posting date. For shared costs, maintain a separate allocation schedule that reconciles the portions charged to each project against the original transaction. A cost charged partly to two projects is not necessarily a duplicate if the allocation is authorized and the total does not exceed the underlying cost. The reviewer should distinguish legitimate allocation from duplicate recovery, check prior reimbursement where relevant, and preserve the reasons for accepting or rejecting a suspected duplicate. Automated flags should create review cases rather than automatically deleting valid transactions.

Study instruction:
- "Test a transaction register containing duplicate invoices, split allocations, and repeated references, and document how each case should be resolved."

Key point:
- Multiple coding lines may represent one legitimate cost or an improper duplicate charge.
- Reconcile total project allocations to the original transaction.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Reconciliation
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 2 — Coding Structure
14. Q: How should donor restrictions be reflected in project expenditure coding?
A: Show answer
Donor restrictions should be documented in the project or grant master data and translated into practical controls over eligible expenditure categories, funding periods, locations, beneficiaries, allocation methods, and approval requirements where applicable. A transaction may be correctly recognized as an accounting expense but still be ineligible for a particular grant. For example, a donor may require pre-approval for equipment purchases or may prohibit certain administrative expenses. The coding structure should permit the finance team to identify restricted amounts, test spending against applicable conditions, and report exceptions. It should not automatically change an accounting classification merely because the donor uses a different budget label. The organization must evaluate the grant agreement and relevant accounting rules independently and keep evidence of any exception or approved budget revision.

Study instruction:
- "Translate a sample donor agreement into a restriction register and test how the restrictions would be applied to proposed expenses."

Key point:
- Donor restrictions govern permissible funding use, not necessarily the accounting nature of an expense.
- Restriction checks should occur before charging and during reporting.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Revenue and Funding Agreements
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses
15. Q: How should project coding handle transactions that relate to more than one grant or activity?
A: Show answer
The organization should establish whether the transaction represents several separately identifiable costs or one shared cost that needs allocation. Separate identifiable costs should be coded according to their own substance and support. A shared transaction should be allocated using an approved method, documented evidence, and the applicable funding restrictions. The coding lines should reference the original transaction so reviewers can reconstruct the total amount and allocation. Where the system supports split coding, the sum of the coding lines must reconcile to the source amount after any separately documented tax or accounting components. A user should not manually split an expense to use remaining budgets without establishing the underlying benefit and permission under the funding agreements. Unusual or disputed allocations should be approved by an authorized reviewer and retained with the allocation methodology.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a split-coding worksheet for a shared consultancy invoice and reconcile the allocated amounts to the source invoice."

Key point:
- Split coding must represent a supported allocation of one underlying transaction.
- Grant restrictions apply to each allocated portion.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 2 — Coding Block
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Cost Allocations
16. Q: How should personnel costs be assigned to projects accurately?
A: Show answer
Personnel costs should be assigned using the employee's work, contractual arrangement, approved project staffing plan, applicable payroll records, and any required timesheet or activity-allocation evidence. Staff working exclusively on one project may have a straightforward direct assignment, while staff serving multiple projects may require a supportable allocation. Salary, benefits, employer contributions, leave, and other payroll components should follow the organization's approved methodology and applicable donor rules. Finance should reconcile allocated personnel costs to the total payroll and ensure the same salary amount is not charged in full to multiple funding sources. Changes in project assignment, temporary deployment, or employment status require review and updated effective dates. Personal payroll information must be restricted to authorized personnel.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile the project allocation of a multi-project employee's payroll to the approved payroll register and supporting time records."

Key point:
- Personnel allocations must reflect actual work or an approved, supportable basis.
- Project charges must reconcile to the underlying payroll cost.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 2 — Coding Block
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses
17. Q: How should travel expenditure be coded when one trip supports several projects?
A: Show answer
Review the approved travel authorization, purpose of the trip, itinerary, activity schedule, settlement documents, receipts, and any records showing which projects benefited. Allocate the costs using a documented method appropriate to the trip, such as direct assignment of identifiable activities or an approved proportional basis supported by evidence. Airfare, accommodation, per diem, local transport, and incidental items may require different treatment under organizational or donor policies. The travel advance itself should not be confused with the final expenditure: the coding of the settled costs should reflect the supported expenses, while unused funds or additional amounts due should be handled through the settlement process. Unresolved documentation or allocation issues should remain open until reviewed and resolved.

Study instruction:
- "Review a multi-project travel settlement and separate the advance, eligible travel costs, allocation, unused balance, and reimbursement."

Key point:
- Travel coding follows the activity and approved allocation basis.
- Advance settlement and expense classification are separate controls.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Travel Expenses and Expense Processing
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Processing
18. Q: How should procurement documents and supplier invoices be linked to project codes?
A: Show answer
The requisition, purchase order or contract, receipt confirmation, invoice, approval, and accounting entry should carry consistent references to the relevant project or approved cost center. Procurement should verify that the intended purchase is authorized and within the appropriate spending controls, while finance should confirm the account classification, project code, grant restrictions, period, and evidence of delivery before posting. A purchase order's coding may require adjustment if the actual goods or services differ from the original request. Such changes should be documented, approved, and reflected in the accounting record. Finance should not rely solely on the supplier's description or an old invoice code to determine project allocation. The workflow should retain enough cross-references to trace the transaction from purchasing through payment and project reporting.

Study instruction:
- "Select a supplier invoice and trace its coding across the requisition, purchase order, delivery evidence, approval, general ledger, and project report."

Key point:
- Procurement references and accounting references should connect the same transaction.
- Coding changes must be supported and approved.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expense and Procurement Processing
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
19. Q: How should an NGO decide whether a purchase should be recorded as an expense or an asset?
A: Show answer
The decision should follow the applicable accounting framework and approved capitalization policy, considering the nature of the item, control, expected use, useful life, materiality, unit of account, and any relevant recognition criteria. The funding source alone does not determine whether a purchase is an expense or an asset. For example, equipment purchased using a grant may still require asset recognition if the applicable criteria are met, while low-value items may be expensed or tracked under a separate stewardship policy depending on the approved rules. Project coding must follow the accounting classification and preserve the grant attribution. The SOP should define escalation for unusual purchases, bundled items, construction costs, equipment donated to beneficiaries, and items whose ownership or control is unclear.

Study instruction:
- "Assess several purchases against the approved capitalization policy and identify the accounting code, project code, and evidence required for each."

Key point:
- Asset-versus-expense decisions depend on accounting criteria and policy.
- Grant funding does not automatically determine the accounting treatment.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Project Assets and Asset-Related Processing
20. Q: How should project expenditure coding deal with donor-funded equipment or other project assets?
A: Show answer
The coding process should distinguish the funding source, purpose, accounting classification, responsible project, location, and control or ownership status of the asset. Relevant documentation may include the donor agreement, purchase evidence, delivery and acceptance records, asset handover forms, asset-register entry, and evidence of disposal or transfer. The organization must assess whether it controls the asset and whether the applicable accounting framework requires recognition, stewardship tracking, or another treatment. A donor-funded purchase should not automatically be expensed or capitalized solely because of its funding source. Project coding must also avoid treating the acquisition as an eligible project expense and separately charging the same acquisition again through depreciation or another allocation without a proper accounting basis. Applicable donor and accounting requirements must be checked.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a donor-funded equipment purchase from grant approval through delivery, project coding, asset-register recording, and subsequent reporting."

Key point:
- Project coding and asset accounting must remain consistent.
- Funding source and asset control are related but distinct considerations.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Project Assets
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Processing
21. Q: How should expenditure be assigned to the correct accounting period and project period?
A: Show answer
The accounting period should be determined using the applicable recognition and cut-off requirements, not solely the payment date or the date a staff member enters the transaction. The project period and donor eligibility period may create additional checks but must not replace the accounting rule. Review services received before period-end but invoiced later, goods delivered near the closing date, advance payments, accruals, credit notes, and invoices covering multiple periods. The evidence should establish when the relevant goods or services were received and what period they relate to. If an expense is recognized in one accounting period but may not be eligible under the grant period, document and escalate that separate funding issue. Maintain distinct fields for document date, service or receipt date, posting date, and project eligibility period where needed.

Study instruction:
- "Test transactions immediately before and after month-end and identify the appropriate accounting period and donor-eligibility implications."

Key point:
- Accounting cut-off and grant eligibility dates are different tests.
- Period decisions must be supported by transaction evidence and the applicable rules.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Processing
22. Q: How should accruals and prepayments be coded to projects?
A: Show answer
An accrual records an expense or liability when the applicable recognition criteria have been met even though the invoice or payment may occur later. A prepayment represents a payment made before the related benefit has been consumed and is generally released to expense over the applicable benefit period when required by the accounting framework. Project coding should preserve the project connection through recognition, reversal, amortization, and final settlement. A month-end accrual schedule should identify the underlying transaction, estimated or supported amount, project, account, service period, rationale, reviewer, and reversal or settlement plan. A prepayment schedule should identify the original payment, benefit period, project, monthly release, and closing balance. Estimates must be supported and reviewed rather than generated merely to make project actuals match a budget.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare one project-related accrual and one prepayment schedule, including journal references and period-by-period reconciliation."

Key point:
- Timing and project assignment must be controlled together.
- Accruals and prepayments need documented release, reversal, and settlement procedures.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Journal Processing
23. Q: How should an incorrectly coded project expense be corrected?
A: Show answer
First establish what was recorded, what the correct treatment should be, why the error occurred, and which reports or approvals may have been affected. Verify whether the issue involves the general-ledger account, project, grant, cost center, period, funding restriction, or more than one dimension. Corrections should use the organization's approved journal or reclassification process and reference the original transaction. Record the reason, amount, original and corrected codes, supporting evidence, preparer, reviewer, and approval. If donor reports have already been submitted, assess whether they require correction under the applicable agreement or reporting procedure. Preserve the original posting and correction history rather than silently replacing the historical record. A correction should not be used to transfer unsupported expenditure into a project with unused budget.

Study instruction:
- "Document a sample project-code correction from error identification through approved journal, updated reconciliation, and assessment of reporting effects."

Key point:
- Reclassification corrects a documented error; it does not justify unsupported expenditure.
- Original and corrected entries must remain traceable.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Journal Processing
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting
24. Q: What controls should govern project transfers and cross-project cost reallocations?
A: Show answer
Transfers between projects should be permitted only when the underlying facts, agreements, approved allocation methodology, and delegated authority support the transfer. The reviewer should verify that the original project charge was incorrect or that a legitimate reallocation is required, and should establish whether the receiving project is authorized to bear the cost. The journal or allocation document must reference the original transaction, both project codes, the reason, supporting evidence, the amount, approval, and effects on donor or management reporting. The transfer should reconcile across all affected project balances. Where a donor's consent is required, the organization should obtain and retain that approval before proceeding. Artificial transfers designed to conceal overspending, resolve budget variances without evidence, or bypass donor conditions are not appropriate.

Study instruction:
- "Review a cross-project reallocation and verify its basis, receiving-project eligibility, approval, journal trail, and donor-reporting implications."

Key point:
- A transfer requires a legitimate basis and appropriate approval.
- Both the sending and receiving project must be checked.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Processing
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses
25. Q: How should project budgets and actual expenditure be compared and monitored?
A: Show answer
A budget-versus-actual report should compare the approved budget version with actual recognized expenditure for the same project, period, currency basis, and classification. If commitments or forecasts are also included, they should be shown separately and their calculation clearly explained. Variances should be analyzed by account, activity, funding source, and period where relevant. A negative variance may result from overspending, timing, incorrect coding, late accruals, missing budget revisions, or incomplete data. A positive remaining balance does not prove that a project has sufficient eligible spending capacity for every proposed transaction. Management thresholds should trigger investigation and, where necessary, formal reforecasting or budget revision. Every revision must retain an effective date, approval reference, and historical version so the organization can reproduce previously issued reports.

Study instruction:
- "Build a budget-versus-actual analysis and investigate material variances using transaction-level detail and the approved budget version."

Key point:
- Budget monitoring must use consistent periods and classifications.
- A variance report should lead to documented investigation or action.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses and Fund Commitments
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Statement of Comparison of Budget and Actual Amounts
26. Q: How should maker-checker and segregation-of-duties controls operate in project coding?
A: Show answer
Separate transaction preparation, coding review, budget or eligibility review where required, approval, posting, and subsequent reconciliation according to the organization's size and risk. The person entering a code should not be the sole authority to approve an unusual allocation, change restricted project master data, and certify the resulting report. System permissions should enforce authorized roles where feasible. If staffing limitations prevent complete separation, define compensating controls such as independent review of all material or unusual transactions, periodic access review, and management approval of sensitive changes. The control matrix should state who performs each step, what evidence demonstrates completion, and what happens if the assigned reviewer is unavailable. Approval should apply to the actual transaction version and coding presented for review.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a responsibility matrix for project-code creation, transaction entry, review, approval, posting, and reconciliation, then identify conflicts."

Key point:
- Coding and approval authority must be clearly separated where practicable.
- Compensating controls require evidence of actual operation.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Processing and Controls
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 2 — Coding Block
27. Q: How should project-code creation, changes, suspension, and closure be controlled?
A: Show answer
Establish a formal request and approval process for adding or changing project codes. Each request should identify the authorized project, funding agreement, purpose, accountable manager, budget, effective dates, permitted coding dimensions, reporting requirements, and relevant restrictions. The master-data owner should check for duplicates, conflicting descriptions, invalid dates, and overlap with existing codes. Changes should be versioned and approved by an authorized person independent of the requester when practicable. Closing a project should include controls over outstanding commitments, invoices, accruals, advances, assets, donor reporting, and unresolved exceptions. Closed codes should be blocked from inappropriate new transactions without destroying historical access. Reopening should require an evidenced decision and clear effective date. Each change must be traceable to a request, approval, implementation, and validation record.

Study instruction:
- "Test the lifecycle of a project code from creation through modification and closure, checking all required approvals and system changes."

Key point:
- Master-data changes can affect many transactions and reports.
- Effective dates and change history must be preserved.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Master Data
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 2 — Coding Block Elements
28. Q: How can Excel data validation, Power Query, and VBA strengthen project expenditure coding?
A: Show answer
Excel validation lists can restrict data-entry values to approved project, grant, account, activity, and status codes. Power Query can combine transaction exports, normalize formats, map codes to controlled master lists, identify unmatched records, and prepare exceptions for review. VBA can support repeatable workflows such as generating review sheets, exporting reports, recording processing metadata, and producing approved journal templates. These tools should not independently decide complex accounting or donor-eligibility questions unless a valid, approved rule and review process support that decision. Input data, mapping tables, rule versions, query transformations, and outputs should be controlled and tested. Automated processing should record row counts and control totals before and after transformation, and exceptions should remain visible until resolved. Technology improves consistency but does not establish the accounting validity of a transaction.

Study instruction:
- "Build a small test register with invalid project codes, duplicate transactions, missing fields, and shared costs, then assess how automation identifies each case."

Key point:
- Automation should enforce approved rules and expose exceptions.
- Accounting decisions remain governed by applicable policies and accounting requirements.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 2 — Coding Block
- Microsoft Learn — What Is Power Query?
29. Q: What data-quality checks should be performed before project expenditure data is posted or reported?
A: Show answer
Validate that required fields are populated, codes exist in the approved master lists, the transaction date falls within an appropriate period, the currency and amount are valid, and the combination of project, grant, account, and activity is permitted. Check for duplicate invoices, invalid supplier references, closed projects, unapproved allocation percentages, negative or unusual amounts, and missing document references. Compare transaction counts and control totals with the original source before accepting the transformed data. A failed check should generate an identifiable exception with an owner and resolution status. Data should not be altered simply to remove a validation failure without documenting the cause and authorized correction. Retain both the source snapshot and the validated version so reviewers can reproduce the processing results.

Study instruction:
- "Run a complete data-quality checklist on a sample transaction population and reconcile the source and validated totals."

Key point:
- Completeness, validity, duplicate detection, and reconciliation are distinct checks.
- Exceptions must be resolved or formally held before final reporting.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Reporting and Controls
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 2 — Coding Block
30. Q: How should project expenditure be reconciled with the general ledger and supporting subledgers?
A: Show answer
Compare project expenditure reports with the general-ledger transactions and relevant subsidiary records for the same accounting period, account scope, currency, and posting status. Establish how the reports handle reversals, accruals, reclassifications, credit notes, and journal adjustments. Differences should be identified at transaction level and classified by cause, such as cut-off, account mapping, project mapping, missing postings, duplicated records, or differing report filters. The reconciliation should show the source totals, report totals, differences, explanations, corrections, reviewer, and closure evidence. A balanced grand total does not necessarily prove that costs are assigned to the correct projects; offsetting coding errors can conceal misclassification. Accordingly, reconciliations should include both total-value checks and targeted review of project-level movements and unusual entries.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile a project report to general-ledger transactions and document how each material difference was investigated and resolved."

Key point:
- Total reconciliation and coding accuracy are complementary controls.
- Every material difference requires an explanation and supporting evidence.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Reconciliation
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting
31. Q: What should a project expenditure report contain to support management and donor decisions?
A: Show answer
The report should identify the project, donor or funding agreement where relevant, reporting period, approved budget version, actual expenditure, commitments if separately reported, remaining budget, material variances, and outstanding exceptions. Expenditure should be summarized by relevant classifications such as account, activity, location, or funding category while retaining transaction-level drill-down. Reports should clearly define their currency basis, cut-off, treatment of reversals, allocation methodology, and status of unposted or unverified entries. Donor-specific reports may require different eligible-cost groupings or additional disclosures, which must be mapped to the organization's controlled accounting data. A management report should also highlight actions requiring approval or follow-up instead of merely presenting totals. The final output must reconcile to the approved source records and use a clearly identified version.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a management report with a donor report and identify differences in classification, eligibility, presentation, and required supporting detail."

Key point:
- One controlled dataset can support different reporting views without changing the underlying facts.
- Every report should state its scope, period, classification, and reconciliation status.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Statements and Disclosures
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Reporting
32. Q: How should foreign-currency project expenditure be coded and reported?
A: Show answer
Preserve the original transaction currency and amount, the accounting currency amount, and the exchange-rate reference required by the applicable accounting and organizational policies. Where a donor agreement requires reporting in a particular currency or using a specified conversion method, record that reporting basis separately from the original accounting data. The appropriate rate and timing depend on the transaction and applicable rules; a single spreadsheet rate should not be applied indiscriminately to every situation. Reconciliations should explain differences arising from currency conversion, settlement, or revaluation where relevant. The reporting process should identify the rate source, effective date, calculation method, and approval of rate-table changes. Unsupported rates or unexplained differences should be investigated before finalization.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one foreign-currency expense from the original invoice through accounting conversion, settlement, and donor reporting."

Key point:
- Original transaction data and reporting-currency amounts must remain traceable.
- Exchange-rate methods must follow the applicable policy and requirements.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting
33. Q: What controls are needed when a project is nearing completion or has already closed?
A: Show answer
Project closure should trigger a documented review of unrecorded expenses, outstanding invoices, commitments, accruals, prepayments, staff or partner advances, supplier balances, assets, donor restrictions, and reporting obligations. Finance should establish the closing date, confirm the approved budget and final expenditure population, reconcile the general ledger with project reports, and identify outstanding claims or other matters requiring follow-up. New transactions should be blocked or routed through an approved reopening process according to policy. A project should not be marked fully closed merely because the final activity has ended or the remaining budget is zero. The closure checklist should show each required task, accountable owner, status, evidence, unresolved issues, and approval. Any continuing asset or liability must retain an appropriate accounting and monitoring path after the project code is closed.

Study instruction:
- "Complete a mock project-closure review and identify all unresolved balances, commitments, documentation, and reporting obligations."

Key point:
- Operational completion and financial closure are not the same event.
- Open assets, liabilities, and donor obligations require an approved resolution plan.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Closing Processes
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting
34. Q: How should an NGO maintain a reliable audit trail for project expenditure coding?
A: Show answer
The audit trail should connect the original source document, transaction reference, coding decision, supporting evidence, approvals, posted journal, subsequent corrections, project report, and final donor or management reporting. Record the relevant dates, responsible roles, rule or master-data versions, and reason for every material change. Preserve the original posted entry and link corrections to the original transaction instead of silently overwriting information. Access to sensitive documents should be limited to authorized users, while retention and disposal follow the applicable legal, donor, contractual, and organizational requirements. The organization should also test that archived records can be retrieved and that links to supporting evidence remain valid. An audit trail is effective only when it shows what happened, who authorized it, and how the resulting accounting treatment was verified.

Study instruction:
- "Select one expenditure and reconstruct its complete audit trail, including any coding changes, reviewer decisions, and final reporting."

Key point:
- Transaction history must remain complete and reproducible.
- Retention periods and access requirements must be based on verified obligations.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Documents and Reporting
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Statements and Disclosures
35. Q: How can management detect project expenditure coding errors and recurring control weaknesses?
A: Show answer
Establish indicators such as the percentage of transactions using invalid or missing codes, the number and value of reclassifications, unmatched project-report balances, duplicate-payment or duplicate-charge exceptions, unapproved allocation changes, late adjustments, expenditure posted to closed projects, and unresolved exceptions by age. Each indicator needs a precise definition, source, calculation method, reporting frequency, accountable owner, and escalation threshold. Trends should be reviewed over several reporting periods to distinguish isolated mistakes from systematic problems in training, master data, budget setup, or system configuration. An increase in corrections may indicate stronger detection rather than deterioration, so management should analyze the reason behind each trend. Dashboard status should be reconciled with actual evidence and must not automatically classify an unresolved item as cleared.

Study instruction:
- "Design a coding-control dashboard and verify a sample of its indicators against the transaction register and correction log."

Key point:
- Useful indicators identify causes and support corrective action.
- Dashboard results require validation against transaction-level evidence.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Reporting and Controls
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting
36. Q: What should happen when the project code is missing, inactive, invalid, or inconsistent with the supporting documents?
A: Show answer
The transaction should enter a defined exception workflow. Finance should identify the missing or conflicting information, record the transaction reference and value, assign an accountable owner, set a resolution deadline, and request the necessary documentation or master-data correction. If the accounting system allows a transaction to be held safely without losing its audit trail, use that mechanism according to policy. Do not invent a code or select an unrelated project merely to complete the posting. Where a temporary or suspense treatment is authorized, document the permitted use, approval, review frequency, and resolution deadline. The final coding should be verified against the underlying purpose, funding terms, and approved project record. An exception may be closed only when the correction and its impact on reporting have been evidenced.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate an invalid project-code submission and document the hold, escalation, authorized correction, retest, and closure evidence."

Key point:
- Invalid coding should generate controlled exceptions, not unsupported assumptions.
- A temporary holding mechanism must not become an indefinite substitute for correct coding.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 2 — Coding Block
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Controls
37. Q: How should finance respond when a donor report and the accounting system show different expenditure amounts?
A: Show answer
Begin by confirming that the two reports cover the same period, project, transaction population, currency basis, and reporting version. Identify differences arising from eligibility adjustments, donor-specific classifications, exchange-rate methods, accruals, cutoff, reclassifications, missing transactions, and duplicated entries. Separate genuine accounting errors from legitimate differences between accounting expenditure and donor-reported eligible costs. Reconcile every material difference to the underlying transaction and document the reason, calculation, approval, and reporting impact. Do not force the donor report to equal the general ledger by posting unsupported journals. If the agreement or applicable reporting rules are ambiguous, obtain an authorized interpretation and retain it with the reconciliation. Any correction to a previously submitted report should follow the formal donor reporting process.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a reconciliation between a general-ledger project report and a donor expenditure report, categorizing and explaining each variance."

Key point:
- Donor-reported eligible costs may differ from accounting expenditure for valid reasons.
- Differences must be explained rather than hidden through unsupported adjustments.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting and Disclosures
38. Q: How should project expenditure coding rules be updated when grant conditions, accounting policies, or reporting requirements change?
A: Show answer
Changes should begin with an identified trigger, such as an amended grant agreement, approved budget revision, accounting-policy change, new project structure, or revised reporting requirement. The responsible owner should assess affected accounts, project codes, validation lists, allocation methodologies, reports, and open transactions. The proposed change should include an effective date, source documentation, impact assessment, authorized approval, test results, and communication plan. Where historical transactions are affected, determine whether retrospective correction or prospective application is appropriate under the governing requirements. Preserve the previous rule version and identify which periods were processed under it. Do not change a code definition or formula without testing whether it affects prior reporting, existing mappings, or transaction history.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a proposed grant-budget revision from source approval through master-data change, rule testing, communication, and reporting impact assessment."

Key point:
- Changes require documented authority, an effective date, and controlled implementation.
- Historical reporting must remain reproducible under the rules applicable at the time.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 2 — Coding Block
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Master Data and Processing
39. Q: How should a project expenditure-coding SOP be implemented and tested before formal adoption?
A: Show answer
Implementation should begin with the approved chart of accounts, project master list, grant agreements, budget structures, financial-system configuration, delegated authorities, and reporting requirements. Map the actual transaction workflow, identify where coding is entered and reviewed, and define the forms, exception routes, and reconciliation controls needed at each stage. Test representative cases, including routine supplier costs, shared expenses, invalid codes, closed projects, cutoff issues, and authorized reclassifications. Compare the proposed procedure with actual system behavior and confirm that responsible employees can perform the controls with the available evidence and permissions. Record gaps, owners, deadlines, training needs, test results, and approval status. A draft SOP should not be described as formally adopted until the organization's authorized approval requirements have been completed.

Study instruction:
- "Run a pilot using representative transactions and document design gaps, control failures, corrective actions, and formal approval requirements."

Key point:
- The SOP must be tested against actual processes and systems.
- Adoption requires authorized approval and effective communication.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Processes
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 2 — Coding Block
40. Q: How can an NGO determine whether its project expenditure-coding controls are operating effectively?
A: Show answer
Effectiveness should be assessed using both control-design review and evidence from actual operation. Select a representative sample of posted transactions and verify coding accuracy, source-document completeness, authorization, funding eligibility where applicable, master-data validity, and reconciliation to project reports. Include unusual transactions, manual journals, shared-cost allocations, corrections, and transactions close to period-end. Confirm that review controls were performed by authorized personnel and that detected exceptions were resolved with evidence. Expand the sample or investigate the root cause when repeated errors indicate a broader failure. Report findings by severity, financial or donor impact, responsible owner, and corrective-action deadline. A control should be considered effective only when its operation is supported by evidence, not merely because the SOP describes the intended procedure.

Study instruction:
- "Perform a risk-based sample test of project expenditure postings and prepare a findings register with evidence, root causes, owners, and retest results."

Key point:
- A documented procedure is not proof that its controls operate effectively.
- Testing must verify real transactions, exceptions, corrective actions, and reporting impacts.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Processes and Reporting
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Statements and Disclosures

Table 7
Implementing Partner and Subgrant Management Controls
1. Q: What is the purpose of an implementing-partner and subgrant management SOP?
A: Show answer
The SOP establishes a controlled process for selecting, assessing, contracting, funding, monitoring, and closing out implementing partners or subrecipients. It connects programme objectives with financial accountability by requiring documented decisions, approved agreements, controlled disbursements, expenditure verification, reporting, recovery, and reconciliation. The objective is not simply to transfer funds and collect a report, but to establish an auditable chain from the original funding decision to the final accepted expenditure and closure of outstanding obligations. Each significant activity should have an accountable owner, defined decision authority, required evidence, a completion criterion, and a documented response when a control fails. The supplied DOCX is a fictional demonstration, not an adopted organizational policy; its examples must be replaced with verified organizational requirements before implementation.

Study instruction:
- Map the complete partner lifecycle and identify the evidence required at each approval and closure gate.
Key point:
- Accountability extends from partner selection through final reconciliation and closeout.
- A fictional template must not be treated as evidence of an approved policy.
Source links:
- Fictional Implementing Partner and Subgrant Management SOP example
2. Q: Which activities should fall within the SOP, and which require referral to another procedure?
A: Show answer
The SOP should cover relationship classification, due diligence, selection, agreement management, partner setup, budget control, disbursement readiness, reporting, liquidation, expenditure eligibility, monitoring, recovery, and closeout. It should not replace specialist procedures for procurement, treasury execution, donor-agreement approval, safeguarding investigations, tax filings, legal advice, or the organization's accounting close. Instead, it should define the handoff to the responsible process, including the referral owner, required evidence, approval status, and resulting decision. For example, a proposed arrangement that may constitute procurement should not proceed through the subgrant route until the authorized owner resolves the classification. Likewise, a safeguarding allegation must be referred through the protected process rather than investigated informally by ordinary partner-management staff. The exact boundaries must follow approved internal policies, law, contracts, and donor requirements.

Study instruction:
- Create an interface matrix showing the responsible procedure, referral owner, handoff evidence, and blocked actions for each boundary.
Key point:
- A referral does not replace the receiving process's decision or control requirements.
- Unresolved classification or authorization issues must block affected commitments.
Source links:
- Fictional SOP example — Scope, process interfaces, and referral matrix
3. Q: How should an organization distinguish an implementing partner from a supplier or contractor?
A: Show answer
Classification should follow the substance of the arrangement rather than its title, payment method, or the fact that the counterparty is a nonprofit organization. Assess who is responsible for delivering programme activities, who controls implementation decisions, what deliverables are required, how performance is measured, what risks each party bears, and what the actual donor agreement and applicable rules require. A supplier relationship generally follows the approved procurement and contracting process, while an implementing-partner relationship may require programme accountability, due diligence, subgrant conditions, liquidation, and expenditure verification. These characteristics are indicators, not a universal legal test. A contract labelled a subgrant does not automatically establish partner status, and a previous relationship does not determine the classification of a new arrangement. Record the analysis, supporting documents, reviewer, decision authority, and any procurement or legal referral before commitment.

Study instruction:
- Compare a proposed programme subgrant with a purchase of services and document the facts that determine the appropriate process route.
Key point:
- Substance and applicable requirements determine classification, not the label alone.
- An unresolved classification should trigger a documented hold.
Source links:
- Fictional SOP example — Relationship classification and procurement interface
4. Q: Which legal, donor, accounting, and organizational requirements should govern partner management?
A: Show answer
Establish a controlled hierarchy of requirements before applying the SOP. Relevant sources may include applicable legislation, the executed donor agreement, approved partner policy, delegation of authority, the signed partner agreement, the organization's accounting framework, procurement and safeguarding policies, and records or privacy requirements. Determine which provisions are binding, which are organizational procedures, and which are illustrative guidance. Where donor terms or laws conflict with an internal procedure, the issue must be referred to the appropriate legal, compliance, donor-management, or policy owner rather than resolved through an undocumented operational assumption. Maintain a source register recording the source ID, exact URL or internal reference, version, jurisdiction, effective period, applicability assessment, reviewer, verification date, interpretation, and affected controls. The existence of a source does not prove that it applies to every entity or transaction.

Study instruction:
- Build a source register for one partner agreement and identify which requirements govern each important decision gate.
Key point:
- Applicability must be established before a rule is implemented.
- Legal obligations, contractual terms, accounting rules, and internal controls serve different purposes.
Source links:
- Fictional SOP example — Governing hierarchy and controlled source register
5. Q: How should roles, responsibilities, and segregation of duties be designed?
A: Show answer
Assign responsibility for relationship classification, programme design, due diligence, risk review, selection, agreement approval, partner master data, bank verification, payment preparation, payment approval, report review, eligibility verification, recovery, ledger reconciliation, and records custody. Use a RACI matrix or equivalent responsibility schedule to distinguish the person performing a task, the accountable decision-maker, consulted specialists, and recipients of information. Where practical, a preparer should not approve their own work or independently verify the transaction they initiated. Smaller organizations may not have enough staff for complete separation, so approved compensating controls may include independent review of bank evidence, retrospective transaction checks, or additional management approval. Actual system permissions must match the authority matrix. Consultant involvement does not automatically transfer the organization's accountability for its own decisions, approvals, and records.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a responsibility matrix for the full lifecycle and test whether one person can bypass a critical approval or verification step.
Key point:
- Responsibilities, authority, and access rights must be aligned.
- Where duties cannot be separated, documented compensating controls are necessary.
Source links:
- Fictional SOP example — Roles, authority, and segregation of duties
6. Q: What should partner due diligence establish before an award is approved?
A: Show answer
Due diligence should establish whether the prospective partner is eligible for consideration, has the legal and operational capacity required for the proposed activity, and can manage funds and satisfy the applicable agreement terms. Depending on the arrangement and risk, evidence may cover registration and identity, authorized signatories, governance, financial controls, budget management, reporting capacity, bank-account ownership, relevant sanctions or debarment screening, conflicts of interest, safeguarding, fraud prevention, and programme delivery capability. Each requirement needs a source, date checked, result, limitations, reviewer, and follow-up where appropriate. Due diligence is a time-bound assessment, not a guarantee of future performance and not approval to award or pay. Mandatory checks and unresolved critical findings must be handled using the applicable approval criteria. Sensitive personal, integrity, and safeguarding information should remain in appropriately restricted records.

Study instruction:
- Select a prospective partner and prepare an evidence checklist distinguishing mandatory requirements, risk-based checks, and unresolved exceptions.
Key point:
- Conclusions must be supported by current and relevant evidence.
- Due diligence, award approval, agreement effectiveness, and payment authorization are separate decisions.
Source links:
- Fictional SOP example — Partner eligibility and due diligence
7. Q: How should partner risk assessments be performed and refreshed?
A: Show answer
Define a risk methodology that identifies relevant factors, specifies how evidence is evaluated, and explains how ratings influence approval authority, verification intensity, payment conditions, and monitoring frequency. Risk factors may include financial control weaknesses, complex delivery arrangements, limited reporting capacity, significant cash exposure, prior unresolved findings, safeguarding risks, and dependence on onward transfers. A numerical score must not automatically cancel a critical issue: a serious unresolved legal, integrity, safeguarding, or eligibility concern may require a hold regardless of the overall average. Record inherent risk, mitigating controls, residual risk, the responsible owner, authorized acceptance of risk, and required actions. Reassessment should occur at scheduled intervals and when material events arise, such as a change in leadership, bank details, operating location, agreement scope, reported fraud concern, or repeated reporting failure. The approved policy must define rating thresholds and who can accept residual risk.

Study instruction:
- Rate a hypothetical partner, identify critical overrides, and demonstrate how a material change triggers reassessment.
Key point:
- Risk ratings should drive proportionate controls and monitoring.
- Critical blockers must not disappear through arithmetic averaging.
Source links:
- Fictional SOP example — Risk methodology, critical overrides, and reassessment
8. Q: How should partner selection and conflicts of interest be controlled?
A: Show answer
Document the approved selection criteria before evaluating candidates. Assess programme fit, eligibility, technical capacity, financial and control capability, delivery risks, safeguarding requirements, budget reasonableness, and applicable donor restrictions. Retain the eligible candidate population, assessment evidence, evaluation results, alternatives considered, decision rationale, and approvals. Each participant in the decision should declare relevant interests and relationships. A person with a conflict should be excluded from affected evaluation or approval activity according to the applicable policy, and the recusal and handling of the conflict should be recorded. Donor nomination, an existing relationship, urgency, or an apparently strong proposal should not bypass required checks. Where a restricted or single-source selection is permitted, document the legal or policy basis, authorized exception, justification, and required independent review before commitment.

Study instruction:
- Review a fictional award decision and trace the criteria, alternatives, conflict declarations, recusals, and approval evidence.
Key point:
- A defensible selection requires evidence and an authorized decision.
- Conflicts must be managed before they compromise selection or approval.
Source links:
- Fictional SOP example — Selection, conflict-of-interest, and award controls
9. Q: What should a subgrant or implementing-partner agreement contain?
A: Show answer
The agreement should describe the parties, approved purpose, activities, deliverables, geographic coverage, implementation period, funding amount and currency, approved budget, reporting schedule, liquidation requirements, payment arrangements, and applicable performance measures. It should also address expenditure eligibility, recordkeeping and audit access, procurement or asset requirements where applicable, onward transfers, safeguarding, fraud reporting, data protection, tax responsibilities, changes to scope or budget, suspension, termination, unspent funds, recovery, and closeout. Clauses must be checked against the actual donor agreement and relevant law rather than copied blindly from a generic template. Maintain a clause matrix linking each requirement to its source, owner, evidence, and monitoring or reporting control. Record the agreement version and any required donor consent before approval. Avoid implying that illustrative clauses are universally mandatory or legally sufficient.

Study instruction:
- Compare an executed agreement with the approved clause checklist and trace each material obligation to an operational control.
Key point:
- Contract clauses need corresponding owners, evidence, and control procedures.
- Applicable donor and legal requirements must be verified for the actual arrangement.
Source links:
- Fictional SOP example — Agreement clauses and control matrix
10. Q: When is a partner agreement ready to support implementation and disbursement?
A: Show answer
Readiness depends on the executed agreement, verified counterparties and signatories, approved budget, satisfied conditions precedent, required donor consent, completed due diligence, appropriate risk approval, and established operational controls. The specific requirements depend on the agreement and applicable policy. Maintain a condition register with a unique condition ID, required evidence, owner, status, reviewer, approval, and date resolved. A condition must not be marked complete merely because an email was received or a draft document exists; the evidence must satisfy the defined requirement. If a condition is not applicable, record the rationale and obtain authorized review where required. An unmet mandatory condition must block the activity or payment that it governs unless an authorized and lawful exception is established. Agreement signature, effectiveness, readiness, and payment execution must remain distinguishable states.

Study instruction:
- Test a proposed payment against the executed agreement and every applicable condition-precedent record.
Key point:
- Readiness is demonstrated by evidence, not by an assumed status.
- Exceptions require documented authority and must not override non-waivable requirements.
Source links:
- Fictional SOP example — Agreement effectiveness and conditions precedent
11. Q: How should partner master data and agreement records be set up?
A: Show answer
Create unique identifiers for the partner, agreement, grant, approved budget, payment tranche, reporting period, and significant evidence records. Link those identifiers through controlled master data so transactions can be traced across agreements, payments, partner reports, expenditure decisions, ledger entries, and closeout. Required data may include legal name, registration reference, agreement version, approved currency, authorized signatories, verified bank reference, project and donor dimensions, reporting dates, and status. Use controlled values and validation rules instead of inconsistent free text. Changes to a partner's identity, banking details, budget dimensions, or authorized representatives should require appropriate evidence, approval, and a recorded effective date. Prevent duplicate partner records, cross-linking to another partner's agreement, and unauthorized changes to completed or submitted records.

Study instruction:
- Trace one partner identifier through agreement, budget, payment, report, and ledger references and test duplicate prevention.
Key point:
- Unique linked identifiers improve completeness and traceability.
- Master-data changes require authorization and history.
Source links:
- Fictional SOP example — Master data and partner setup
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 2 — Umoja overview and grant master data
12. Q: How should the approved budget and later budget changes be controlled?
A: Show answer
Retain the approved budget by line, period, currency, project or activity dimension, and agreement version. Check that the proposed commitment or payment is within the currently authorized amount and permitted budget categories. The control should identify budget availability, prior commitments where relevant, approved revisions, and restrictions on transfers between budget lines. A proposed reallocation must follow the partner agreement and applicable donor approval requirements; it must not become effective merely because a spreadsheet has been updated. Preserve both the old and new versions, change rationale, impact analysis, requester, reviewer, approver, donor consent where required, and effective date. Reconcile budget changes to the partner setup and any downstream forecasts or reports. If the required approval is missing, hold the affected commitment or disbursement.

Study instruction:
- Reconcile a proposed re-budgeting request to the current agreement, approved budget, delegated authority, and required donor consent.
Key point:
- Only an authorized budget version should govern commitments and payments.
- Approved changes must be traceable across all affected records.
Source links:
- Fictional SOP example — Budget mapping and change control
13. Q: How should partner reporting calendars and internal deadlines be established?
A: Show answer
Translate agreement obligations into a reporting calendar that identifies the reporting period, submission deadline, required report format, liquidation documentation, reviewer, clarification window, approval target, payment dependencies, and escalation point. Distinguish contractual or donor deadlines from internal service targets. Assign responsibility for issuing reminders, checking completeness, tracking overdue submissions, and escalating material delays. Deadlines must come from the applicable agreement and approved policy, not a fictional example or another partner's timetable. Where dates or requirements are uncertain, the relationship owner should obtain an authorized interpretation and record the source. Link the calendar to disbursement decisions so outstanding reports or liquidation requirements can be checked before a further tranche is released, subject to the agreement's actual terms and authorized exceptions.

Study instruction:
- Build a calendar for one partner and test whether every contractual reporting milestone has an owner, reminder, and escalation route.
Key point:
- Contractual due dates and internal target dates are different controls.
- Outstanding reports should be visible before further funding decisions.
