- Fictional SOP example — Reporting calendar and liquidation deadlines
14. Q: What should be verified before authorizing a partner disbursement?
A: Show answer
Verify the agreement version and effectiveness, the payment's authorized purpose, approved amount and currency, available budget, satisfaction of applicable conditions, current partner risk status, prior reporting or liquidation requirements, bank verification, correct coding, duplicate-payment checks, and required approvals. Retain the payment request, calculation, source references, review, approval, and release evidence. Record the request, readiness decision, approval, bank submission, settlement, partner receipt where required, ledger posting, and reconciliation as separate stages. Approval of a request does not prove that a bank transfer succeeded. If a mandatory prerequisite is unresolved, the transaction should be held or rejected through the defined process rather than passed through on an informal promise to complete the documentation later.

Study instruction:
- Apply the pre-disbursement checklist to a sample tranche and document each prerequisite and its evidence.
Key point:
- Payment authorization must precede execution and be supported by current evidence.
- A successful payment and a reconciled obligation are separate completion states.

15. Q: How should partner bank-account verification and banking changes be controlled?
A: Show answer
Verify the payee identity and bank details using evidence and communication channels approved by the organization. A change request should be independently confirmed rather than accepted solely on the basis of an email, invoice, or message containing new details. Record the previous and new bank references, request source, verification method, verifier, reviewer, approval, effective date, and relevant evidence. Restrict access to complete banking information and avoid placing sensitive details in general management registers. Before each payment, confirm that the beneficiary details match the approved current record. If details are inconsistent, recently changed without adequate evidence, or associated with a suspicious request, hold the payment and escalate it through the established financial-security process. Payment urgency does not justify bypassing independent verification.

Study instruction:
- Simulate a last-minute bank-account change and test the verification, approval, access, and payment-hold controls.
Key point:
- Bank changes require independent verification and an auditable approval trail.
- Unverified changes must not be used for payment.

16. Q: How should advance payments to implementing partners be monitored and accounted for?
A: Show answer
First determine the accounting treatment applicable to the reporting entity and its approved framework. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual discusses advances to implementing partners and the recognition of expenses supported by partner reporting, while also describing different treatment for certain UN reporting volumes. Consequently, an NGO must not assume that a particular UN journal entry applies automatically to its own financial statements. Operationally, maintain a partner-advance roll-forward that identifies opening balance, authorized transfers, accepted expenditure or other permitted settlements, refunds, approved adjustments, and closing balance. Link each movement to the agreement, payment evidence, submitted report, acceptance decision, and ledger entry. Do not treat money transferred, expenditure reported, expenditure accepted, and an advance cleared as interchangeable states. Unexplained balances should remain open for investigation and follow-up.

Study instruction:
- Reconcile a partner's advance from payment through report acceptance, refund or other authorized settlement, and final ledger balance.
Key point:
- The accounting treatment depends on the applicable reporting framework and policy.
- An advance is not automatically cleared when the partner submits a report.
Source links:

17. Q: What should be checked when a partner submits a financial report or liquidation statement?
A: Show answer
Confirm that the submission covers the correct agreement and reporting period, uses the required format and currency, identifies opening balances and transactions, contains the required certifications and supporting-evidence index, and reconciles to its stated totals. Check arithmetic, completeness, transaction populations, budget categories, exchange-rate treatment where applicable, and explanations of material variances. Record each submission version and whether it is received, incomplete, under review, returned, accepted, or rejected. A report that is complete in appearance may still contain ineligible, unsupported, duplicated, or incorrectly classified costs. Requests for clarification should identify the affected lines, missing evidence, partner response deadline, responsible reviewer, and resolution. Submission does not imply acceptance; accepted expenditure must be explicitly determined under the applicable agreement and policy.

Study instruction:
- Review one partner report from receipt to final disposition and trace each material adjustment to evidence and an authorized decision.
Key point:
- Completeness review and expenditure eligibility review are distinct activities.
- Reported expenditure is not automatically accepted expenditure.

- Fictional SOP example — Partner report, liquidation, and acceptance controls
18. Q: How should expenditure eligibility be determined under a partner agreement?
A: Show answer
Establish an eligibility matrix based on the actual signed agreement, applicable donor rules, approved budget, relevant organizational policy, and any governing law. For each cost category, specify the permitted purpose, period, necessary evidence, approval requirements, allocation method, applicable limits, and exception route. Review whether the cost relates to the agreed activities, falls within the permitted period, is adequately supported, and satisfies applicable contractual conditions. Where relevant, consider proof of delivery, payroll records, procurement files, contracts, receipts, payment evidence, asset records, and allocation calculations. A general accounting category does not establish donor eligibility, and a cost may be recorded in the partner's books without being accepted for reimbursement or grant liquidation. Record a separate decision and rationale for each material line or defined group of lines.

Study instruction:
- Build a cost eligibility matrix from a sample agreement and apply it to different types of partner expenditure.
Key point:
- Eligibility is agreement-, policy-, and fact-specific.
- Accounting recognition and donor eligibility must not be conflated.
Source links:
- Fictional SOP example — Expenditure eligibility and evidence matrix
19. Q: How should expenditure verification and sampling be designed?
A: Show answer
Establish a verification plan based on the reporting population, applicable requirements, partner risk, transaction characteristics, and the purpose of the review. Document the population source, completeness checks, selection method, sample size or coverage rationale, tested records, criteria, evidence inspected, limitations, findings, and reviewer conclusion. Risk-based selection may concentrate on unusual amounts, related parties, cash transactions, procurement, assets, repeat exceptions, or other elevated-risk areas, while still considering whether the overall reporting population has been reconciled. A sample does not automatically prove that all untested costs are valid. Extrapolation, where contemplated, requires a defensible method and appropriate authority. Record each tested line separately as accepted, clarified, questioned, disputed, or determined ineligible according to the actual decision rules.

Study instruction:
- Select a sample from a reconciled population and document the rationale, evidence, exceptions, and limits of the conclusion.
Key point:
- Sampling must be based on a known population and a documented method.
- Findings should not be generalized beyond what the evidence supports.
Source links:
- Fictional SOP example — Verification plans, sampling, and eligibility decisions
20. Q: How should foreign-currency partner transactions and exchange differences be handled?
A: Show answer
Retain the original currency and amount, transaction date, applicable agreement currency, exchange-rate source, rate date, conversion direction, calculation, rounding method, and resulting reporting currency. Apply the exchange-rate policy required by the organization's accounting framework and the actual agreement; do not substitute a convenient spreadsheet rate without assessing its applicability. Distinguish partner reporting currency from the INGO's functional or presentation currency where relevant. The partner's reported exchange loss or gain should be reviewed against supporting records and contract terms before deciding whether it is accepted for grant purposes. Monetary calculations should use explicit decimal precision and currency-aware reconciliation rather than uncontrolled floating-point approximations. Preserve rate versions and approvals so an independent reviewer can reproduce the amounts and investigate differences.

Study instruction:
- Recalculate one foreign-currency report line and reconcile the partner amount, applied rate, and reporting-currency result.
Key point:
- Exchange-rate methods must follow applicable accounting and contractual requirements.
- Currency, rate, date, and rounding must be traceable and reproducible.

- Fictional SOP example — Currency, FX, and expenditure verification

21. Q: How should outstanding advances, accepted costs, and unspent funds be reconciled?
A: Show answer
Prepare a roll-forward that separately identifies opening advance, transfers during the period, reported expenditure, accepted eligible expenditure, questioned amounts, refunds, authorized adjustments, and closing balance. The schedule should reconcile to the payment records, partner submissions, formal acceptance decisions, and ledger. A questioned amount must not silently be treated as accepted, and a recovery claim must not automatically be treated as cash received. Unspent funds should be assessed against the agreement's requirements and any approved continuation, refund, or other permitted treatment. Assign unresolved differences to a named owner with a target date and escalation route. A zero closing balance alone is not proof of a correct reconciliation; compensating errors, duplicate entries, omitted transactions, or unsupported offsets can conceal an unresolved problem.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a partner advance roll-forward and trace all movements to payment evidence, expenditure decisions, refund evidence, and the ledger.
Key point:
- Every movement requires a documented basis and an appropriate status.
- The closing balance must be explained by the complete transaction population.

- Fictional SOP example — Advance roll-forward and accounting reconciliation
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Advances to implementing partners
22. Q: How should programme and financial monitoring be integrated?
A: Show answer
Monitoring should assess both whether activities are progressing as agreed and whether funds are being used and reported in accordance with the applicable requirements. The plan may include financial trend analysis, progress against deliverables, budget variance, reporting timeliness, procurement or asset checks, site visits, remote verification, safeguarding controls, and follow-up on prior findings. Record the monitoring objective, period, methodology, participants by role, sample or evidence reviewed, limitations, findings, severity, responsible owner, and due date. Programme progress does not prove that reported expenditure is eligible, while a financially complete report does not prove that the intended outputs were achieved. Integrate the two streams to understand exceptions, but retain distinct evidence and conclusions for programme performance and financial acceptance.

Study instruction:
- Design a monitoring plan that connects delivery milestones, financial evidence, risk indicators, and follow-up actions.
Key point:
- Programme performance and financial eligibility require related but distinct assessments.
- Monitoring findings should lead to documented decisions and follow-up.
Source links:
- Fictional SOP example — Monitoring and corrective-action controls
23. Q: How should corrective actions from monitoring or verification findings be closed?
A: Show answer
Each finding should have a unique identifier, clear description, evidence, severity, root-cause assessment, risk or financial impact, responsible owner, agreed corrective action, due date, and required completion evidence. The partner may propose the corrective action, but the organization must decide whether it sufficiently addresses the issue. Closure should require an independent check where specified by policy, particularly for material financial control or safeguarding findings. A completed training session or revised procedure is not necessarily proof that an underlying weakness has been corrected; the control may need to be retested. Overdue or ineffective actions should be escalated and may trigger reassessment, enhanced monitoring, or other measures permitted under the agreement and policy. Retain the original finding and the closure decision so the history remains auditable.

Study instruction:
- Take a repeated reporting-control finding and document the root cause, corrective action, retest, and independent closure evidence.
Key point:
- Action completion and effective remediation are different things.
- Every material finding needs an owner, deadline, and closure criterion.
Source links:
- Fictional SOP example — Monitoring findings and corrective actions
24. Q: What is the difference between a questioned cost, a disputed cost, an ineligible cost, and a recovery claim?
A: Show answer
A questioned cost is an amount awaiting clarification or evidence assessment. A disputed cost is subject to disagreement or an unresolved decision under the applicable process. An ineligible cost is an amount determined not to satisfy the relevant eligibility requirements by the authorized decision-maker on adequate evidence. A recovery claim is a formal demand or request for repayment or settlement with a documented basis and calculation. These statuses must not be used interchangeably. A questionable transaction may ultimately be accepted after clarification, while a recovery claim can remain unpaid even after it is approved. Record the evidence, decision criteria, amount, rationale, partner response, reviewer, approval, and next step for each item. The particular remedies, appeal or dispute mechanism, and deadlines depend on the agreement and applicable policy or law.

Study instruction:
- Classify four hypothetical costs and identify the evidence needed before changing each item's status.
Key point:
- An unresolved concern is not automatically a final finding of ineligibility.
- A recovery claim is not equivalent to actual recovered funds.
Source links:
- Fictional SOP example — Eligibility decisions, disputes, and recoveries
25. Q: How should recoveries, refunds, offsets, waivers, and write-offs be authorized?
A: Show answer
Begin with the relevant agreement, eligibility decision, calculation, correspondence, and authorized assessment of the circumstances. Determine which remedy is permitted: a refund, an approved offset, another contractually valid settlement, or a waiver or write-off where legally and organizationally authorized. The decision-maker must consider contractual rights, donor restrictions, applicable law, financial impact, and any required consultation or consent. Segregate the authorization of a recovery claim from the receipt of funds, the posting of a settlement, and the final ledger reconciliation. Record claim amount, partner response, settlement method, approval, actual receipt or permitted settlement evidence, residual balance, and closure decision. Never record a recovery as completed merely because a claim letter was sent or an offset was proposed. Waiver and write-off authority must not be inferred from operational convenience.

Study instruction:
- Trace a sample recovery from the supporting eligibility decision through approval, partner communication, settlement, and ledger clearance.
Key point:
- Claims, approvals, actual settlement, and accounting clearance are separate events.
- Any waiver, offset, or write-off requires appropriate authority and evidence.
Source links:
- Fictional SOP example — Recovery, refund, offset, waiver, and write-off controls
26. Q: How should sub-partners and onward transfers be controlled?
A: Show answer
First establish whether onward funding or subcontracting is permitted by the signed agreement and relevant donor requirements. Where authorization is required, obtain and retain it before the affected transfer or commitment. Identify the downstream organization, purpose, approved amount, duration, risk, reporting responsibilities, and controls that must flow down from the principal partner's agreement. Define who verifies downstream eligibility, monitors the sub-partner, reviews its expenditure, and resolves findings. The principal partner's report alone may not provide enough evidence that downstream funds were used appropriately. Record onward transfers separately so they can be reconciled to the principal partner's funding, supported reports, accepted expenditure, and any outstanding balances. Requirements should be proportionate to the arrangement but must never dilute mandatory donor, legal, or safeguarding obligations.

Study instruction:
- Review a proposed onward transfer and identify the authorizations, downstream evidence, and reconciliation links required before release.
Key point:
- A downstream transfer creates additional visibility and accountability requirements.
- Flow-down obligations must be checked against the actual agreement and donor terms.
Source links:
- Fictional SOP example — Sub-partner and onward-transfer controls
27. Q: How should partner procurement, equipment, and tax obligations be handled within the subgrant process?
A: Show answer
The SOP should identify the relevant interfaces rather than inventing universal rules for each specialist subject. Procurement costs must be assessed against the applicable partner agreement, donor requirements, procurement policy, and supporting competition or exception evidence. Equipment or other assets may require controls over delivery, custody, tagging, use, safeguarding, disposal, transfer, or return, depending on ownership and contractual terms. Tax treatment depends on the transaction, parties, jurisdiction, applicable law, and the organization's tax obligations; it should be determined by the authorized tax owner. Assign each interface a responsible process, required evidence, decision authority, and escalation route. Partner reports should distinguish transactions reported by the partner from costs accepted by the INGO. Unresolved specialist issues should remain visible rather than being cleared by a general finance review without the required expertise.

Study instruction:
- Take one procurement cost, one equipment purchase, and one tax-related transaction and map their required source evidence and decision owners.
Key point:
- Specialist controls remain with their authorized process owners.
- Partner reporting does not by itself establish procurement compliance, asset ownership, or tax correctness.
Source links:
- Fictional SOP example — Procurement, asset, tax, and other process interfaces
28. Q: How should safeguarding concerns, suspected fraud, and other integrity issues be handled?
A: Show answer
Provide a protected referral route that permits staff to report concerns to the authorized safeguarding, security, fraud, or compliance owner. Ordinary partner-management staff should not attempt an unauthorized investigation or disseminate sensitive narratives in routine financial registers. Record only the restricted referral identifier, minimum facts needed for triage, immediate control measures, referral date, authorized receiving role, and evidence that the referral was accepted. Protect affected individuals, preserve relevant evidence, and apply the organization's approved confidentiality, anti-retaliation, incident response, and escalation procedures. A concern or allegation must not be treated automatically as proof of guilt. At the same time, a credible unresolved concern must not be dismissed simply because a final investigation finding is unavailable. Decisions to continue, restrict, suspend, or terminate a relationship must follow authorized risk and contractual processes.

Study instruction:
- Trace a hypothetical safeguarding or integrity concern through the restricted referral pathway without exposing sensitive case details in the general partner register.
Key point:
- Protected referrals require confidentiality and authorized handling.
- Allegations, verified findings, and management decisions are distinct states.
Source links:
- Fictional SOP example — Safeguarding, integrity, and protected referrals
29. Q: How should accepted partner expenditure be reconciled to the organization's accounting records?
A: Show answer
Define the accounting treatment using the applicable reporting framework and approved organizational accounting policy. The reconciliation should link agreement and budget references, disbursements, submitted reports, line-level acceptance decisions, refunds or permitted settlements, relevant foreign-exchange calculations, and the general ledger. Separate the partner's own accounting recognition from the INGO's recognition of a transfer, advance, expense, receivable, or recovery; the partner's books do not automatically determine the INGO's treatment. Reconcile complete transaction populations, identify differences, and record the evidence and approval for every correction. Use the correct accounting period and appropriate project, donor, and account dimensions. Material unresolved accounting-policy questions should be referred to the responsible accounting authority before final reporting. A clean-looking total does not replace a line-level explanation of reconciling items.

Study instruction:
- Reconcile one partner's payment and liquidation history from source records through accepted costs and the INGO ledger.
Key point:
- Partner records and the INGO ledger serve different reporting purposes.
- Approved accounting treatment and complete supporting evidence are essential.

30. Q: How should donor reporting remain consistent with partner records, accepted costs, and ledger balances?
A: Show answer
Define the reporting basis, donor requirements, reporting currency, approved budget version, expenditure eligibility criteria, and relevant cut-off rules for the actual agreement. Reconcile amounts in donor reports to accepted expenditure, the ledger, and applicable supporting evidence. Differences may arise from timing, eligibility decisions, foreign exchange, classification, or other permitted adjustments and should be explained rather than concealed by an unexplained adjustment. Maintain a traceable record of report preparation, independent review, approval, submission, and any subsequent correction. A partner's submission should not be copied directly into a donor report without the required verification. Where donor formats use categories that differ from the organization's ledger, document the mapping and test that the conversion neither omits nor double-counts amounts. Unresolved material differences must follow the applicable escalation and approval process.

Study instruction:
- Trace selected donor-report totals to the approved budget, ledger, partner reports, acceptance decisions, and supporting transactions.
Key point:
- Donor reporting requires a documented reconciliation basis.
- Mapping differences must be explainable and reproducible.

31. Q: How should partner suspension, termination, and transition be managed?
A: Show answer
Suspension or termination should follow the applicable agreement, approved delegation of authority, legal advice where necessary, and relevant donor notification or consent requirements. Document the triggering event, evidence, risk assessment, decision authority, effective date, restrictions imposed, communication, and conditions for any resumption. Assess outstanding payments, advances, partner access to systems or data, uncompleted deliverables, report submissions, disputed expenditure, assets, onward transfers, and potential recoveries. Ensure continuity arrangements protect programme participants and preserve required records without permitting unauthorized new commitments. A suspension is not automatically a final determination of wrongdoing, and termination does not automatically resolve financial balances. Assign an owner for each residual obligation and maintain oversight until every required transfer, refund, record handover, and formal closeout action is completed.

Study instruction:
- Simulate a material agreement breach and prepare the decision, communication, funding, records, and continuity actions.
Key point:
- Relationship changes require authorized decisions and controlled execution.
- Suspension or termination does not eliminate outstanding financial or safeguarding obligations.

32. Q: What must be completed before partner closeout is approved?
A: Show answer
Closeout should reconcile funding transferred, expenditure reported and accepted, closing advances, unspent funds, refunds, recovery claims, assets, outstanding findings, and any amounts remaining under dispute. Confirm that required financial and programme reports are submitted and reviewed, donor obligations are completed or otherwise handled under an authorized arrangement, relevant assets are accounted for, and records are archived under verified retention and access rules. Review whether any residual legal, safeguarding, tax, contractual, or audit obligations continue after the implementation period. Obtain independent confirmation that each mandatory closeout item is satisfied. Residual items should have a documented legal or policy basis, responsible owner, deadline, and monitoring plan rather than being hidden by changing the partner's status to closed. Preserve the final reconciliation, approval, and closure evidence.

Study instruction:
- Complete a closeout checklist for a sample agreement and reconcile the final financial position to supporting evidence.
Key point:
- Closeout means obligations are resolved or appropriately controlled, not merely that the project end date has passed.
- Final approval requires evidence for all mandatory closure gates.

33. Q: How should records, privacy, access, and retention be controlled throughout the partner lifecycle?
A: Show answer
Maintain an indexed record set linking selection, due diligence, agreements, amendments, budgets, bank verification, disbursements, reports, expenditure tests, monitoring, recoveries, reconciliation, and closeout. Define the repository, record owner, custodian, access classification, authorized roles, retention source, legal hold status where relevant, access-review interval, and secure disposal approval. Do not invent a retention period without confirming applicable law, donor or contractual requirements, and approved organizational policy. Sensitive identity, banking, safeguarding, and integrity information should be restricted to users with a legitimate need to access it. Preserve original submitted or executed records and use controlled revisions rather than overwriting evidence. Test whether records can be retrieved and opened, and verify that backups and access controls work as intended.

34. Q: How should exceptions and HOLD states operate in the partner-management workflow?
A: Show answer
Define controlled statuses for missing evidence, unverified information, pending review, rejected submissions, unresolved eligibility, blocked payment, disputed costs, open recovery claims, and incomplete closeout. Each HOLD should identify the affected action, reason, accountable owner, required correction, deadline, escalation route, reviewer, and evidence necessary to release the hold. Do not use zero to represent an unknown amount, mark missing data as not applicable without justification, or convert an unresolved case to closed merely because it is overdue. A status change should preserve the prior state, actor, time, reason, supporting evidence, and required approval. Automated status transitions should follow validated decision rules and must not bypass legal, contractual, safeguarding, or financial approvals. A hold should be released only when its defined conditions are met and the appropriate authority confirms closure.

Study instruction:
- Test the status-transition rules using missing documents, overdue reports, unresolved risks, and failed payment scenarios.
Key point:
- Unknown, pending, not applicable, rejected, and zero are distinct states.
- Every hold needs an owner, resolution criteria, and closure evidence.

35. Q: How should payment failures, duplicate transfers, or ambiguous bank settlements be handled?
A: Show answer
Identify the payment instruction, bank reference, amount, currency, beneficiary, submission date, and current settlement status. Check the authoritative bank or payment-system evidence before concluding whether funds were transmitted, rejected, reversed, duplicated, or remain unresolved. An uncertain status should not trigger an automatic retry because the original transfer might still settle. Escalate the case to the authorized treasury owner, investigate the bank evidence, and determine the safe next action under the payment procedure. Once the outcome is established, update the payment register, partner balance, ledger, and reconciliation without deleting the original instruction or failed attempt. Record any replacement payment approval and independently verify that the same obligation has not been paid twice. Keep the incident open until the cash and accounting records agree.

Study instruction:
- Review a failed or ambiguous transfer and document the evidence required before retrying, reversing, or clearing it.
Key point:
- A submitted payment instruction is not proof of settlement.
- Never repeat an uncertain transfer without establishing the original transaction's status.
Source links:
- Fictional SOP example — Payment execution and failed-payment controls
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and reconciliation controls
36. Q: How should information be handled when partner reports, bank evidence, and accounting records disagree?
A: Show answer
Preserve each source and determine which assertion is inconsistent: payment execution, partner receipt, reported expenditure, eligibility, accounting recognition, or ledger posting. Compare transaction references, dates, amounts, currency, agreement, reporting period, and supporting documents. Investigate timing differences, duplicate entries, omitted transactions, incorrect currency conversion, classification errors, or unsupported adjustments. Assign each difference an amount where known, risk, owner, required evidence, proposed treatment, reviewer, and target resolution date. Do not force the records to agree by posting an unsupported adjustment or changing the partner's report without a documented basis. Where evidence remains inconclusive, maintain the appropriate open or hold state and escalate under the approved policy. Close the issue only after the explanation, correction if needed, and reconciliation are independently supported.

Study instruction:
- Investigate a sample discrepancy from its first detection to the documented conclusion and corrected records, if required.
Key point:
- Reconciliation explains differences; it must not conceal them.
- A correction needs a documented basis, approval, and retest.

37. Q: Which indicators should management use to evaluate the effectiveness of partner and subgrant controls?
A: Show answer
Useful indicators may include the percentage of active partners with current required due diligence, agreements with complete approval evidence, disbursements that passed all required prerequisites, reports submitted on time, reports reviewed within target, unresolved questioned expenditure, overdue advances, outstanding corrective actions, aged reconciliation differences, recovery claims versus actual settlements, and closeouts completed with required evidence. Define each indicator's numerator, denominator, reporting period, data source, owner, threshold, and escalation rule. For example, reporting timeliness should include the full population of reports due, not only those received. Distinguish counts, amounts, and percentages; a small number of high-value exceptions may be more material than many low-value delays. Validate automated metrics against underlying records, and use trends to prompt investigation rather than treating a dashboard score as proof of compliance.

Study instruction:
- Create a partner-control dashboard and independently recalculate several indicators from the underlying registers.
Key point:
- Each indicator needs a reproducible definition and reliable source data.
- Management metrics support decisions but do not replace evidence-based review.

38. Q: How can Excel, Power Query, and VBA support partner and subgrant controls?
A: Show answer
Use structured Excel tables for partner, agreement, budget, payment, report, eligibility, monitoring, recovery, and reconciliation records. Power Query can standardize source files, combine reporting populations, map controlled identifiers, identify missing fields, and flag possible duplicates. Excel formulas can calculate agreed roll-forwards and variance checks when their logic is reviewed and tested. VBA may prepare reports, update controlled workflow fields, create document outputs, and record authorized processing events. Automation must not determine an unverified legal classification, accept a disputed cost, approve a high-risk partner, or release a payment without the required authorized decision. Protect formulas and configuration, validate imported records, preserve source versions, log exceptions, and test changes before release. Users should be able to reproduce important outputs from controlled input versions and approved rules.

Study instruction:
- Prototype a report-import and reconciliation workflow and test missing fields, duplicate candidates, invalid statuses, and changed source versions.
Key point:
- Automation improves repeatability only when its rules and inputs are controlled.
- Professional judgment and formal approvals remain necessary.

39. Q: How should the organization test the SOP before putting it into operation?
A: Show answer
Test the process using both normal transactions and negative cases. Normal cases should cover classification, due diligence, award, agreement execution, setup, disbursement, reporting, expenditure acceptance, reconciliation, and closeout. Negative cases should include missing mandatory evidence, expired due diligence, conflict of interest, an unapproved budget change, mismatched bank details, duplicate payments, unsupported expenditure, incomplete reporting, a protected referral, and a failed reconciliation. For each test, define the initial conditions, expected decision, blocked actions, responsible owner, evidence, and closure criteria. Confirm stable IDs and references across controls, procedures, forms, and tests. Inspect the generated documents and data structures, verify that required records are editable, and independently check calculations and status logic. Record test results and defects; a document's existence does not establish operational effectiveness or compliance with the actual agreement.

Study instruction:
- Build a test matrix for ordinary and failure scenarios and record expected versus actual outcomes before adoption.
Key point:
- Negative testing verifies whether important controls genuinely prevent unsafe progression.
- A completed draft is not proof of successful operational testing.

40. Q: How should changes to partner policy, donor terms, systems, or law be incorporated into the SOP?
A: Show answer
Establish an authorized owner responsible for monitoring relevant sources and assessing the effect of changes on classifications, agreement clauses, eligibility criteria, approvals, calculations, reporting, and records. Log the source and version, date identified, effective date, impacted procedures, risk assessment, implementation owner, testing, approvals, communication, and next review. Do not revise a control solely on the basis of an unofficial summary or an informal instruction. Determine whether current partners, pending payments, reporting periods, or ongoing disputes require a transition arrangement. Preserve the superseded version and establish which version applies to each decision or period. Required training, user acknowledgment, system configuration changes, and post-release monitoring should be completed before the revised procedure is treated as operational, in accordance with authorized release criteria.

41. Q: How should staff training and handover support consistent application of the SOP?

Staff who review agreements need different competencies from those who prepare payments, verify expenditure, manage protected referrals, or approve closeout. Maintain the training-material version, delivery date, audience, attendance or acknowledgment where required, competency checks, remediation for incomplete training, and release authorization. Document the location of current procedures, forms, source registers, escalation contacts, and system instructions. Handover should include open partner advances, reporting deadlines, overdue actions, disputes, bank-change requests, pending approvals, and residual obligations, with restricted details handled through authorized channels. Do not assume attendance alone demonstrates competence or that an incoming owner has accepted every open issue. The authorized manager should confirm responsibility transfer and evidence of completion.

Donor Financial Reporting and Grant Closeout
1. Q: What is the purpose of an integrated donor financial reporting and grant closure SOP?
A: Show answer
The SOP establishes a controlled process for preparing, reviewing, approving, submitting, correcting, reconciling, and closing donor financial reports and grants. It connects executed agreements, approved budgets, accounting transactions, eligibility decisions, supporting evidence, donor submissions, partner balances, grant-funded assets, final claims, refunds, and closure approvals. A report that balances, a successful upload, or the end of programme activities does not by itself prove that all costs are eligible, the donor has accepted the report, or the grant is financially closed. Each stage must have a responsible owner, defined evidence, appropriate authorization, and a documented outcome. The supplied SOP is explicitly a fictional demonstration, not an adopted organizational procedure. Actual implementation requires verification of the entity, applicable accounting framework, current laws, signed donor agreement, organizational policies, and authorized approvals.

Study instruction:
- "Map the complete reporting and closure lifecycle and distinguish the illustrative procedure from an approved organizational SOP."
Key point:
- Financial reporting, donor acceptance, settlement, and closure are separate milestones.
- An example SOP becomes operational only after applicability, authority, and approval are verified.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
2. Q: How should the organization determine which accounting framework and donor requirements govern a grant?
A: Show answer
Begin by establishing the legal entity, jurisdiction, applicable statutory reporting requirements, organizational accounting policy, and approved financial reporting framework. Then identify the executed donor agreement, amendments, approved budget, reporting instructions, and any incorporated conditions. These requirements serve different purposes: accounting standards govern applicable accounting treatment, legislation governs legal obligations, and the donor agreement establishes contractual requirements within its lawful scope. Donor reporting rules must not be assumed to replace statutory accounting requirements, nor should an international accounting framework be assumed applicable merely because it offers useful guidance. The supplied example identifies Indonesia and an illustrative SAK-based candidate, while leaving actual applicability unverified. For that reason, determine whether relevant Indonesian standards or another framework applies before adopting any recognition, measurement, presentation, or disclosure conclusion. Record the conclusion, supporting authority, reviewer, and approval.

Study instruction:
- "Create an applicability matrix identifying the legal entity, reporting framework, statutory requirements, agreement obligations, and unresolved questions."
Key point:
- The applicable framework must be established before the SOP is adopted.
- Accounting standards, law, organizational policy, and donor conditions must be distinguished.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual
3. Q: What documents establish the reporting obligations for each donor grant?
A: Show answer
The controlling record normally includes the signed agreement, effective amendments, approved budget, donor-specific reporting instructions, required templates, submission-channel requirements, and documented correspondence that changes or clarifies an obligation. For each requirement, capture its source, clause or section, effective date, applicability, report type, deadline, responsible role, and required evidence. Maintain a hierarchy for resolving conflicting documents and refer unclear or contradictory provisions to the authorized grants, finance, or legal reviewer. Draft agreements and unsigned amendments must not automatically be treated as binding authority. Where a requirement cannot be established, mark the affected decision as on hold rather than inventing a deadline, threshold, or interpretation. Preserve the earlier documents and the approval trail so reviewers can reconstruct which requirements governed each reporting period.

Study instruction:
- "Select a grant and trace every reporting obligation to the executed agreement, effective amendments, and approved instructions."
Key point:
- Each reporting obligation needs an identifiable and applicable source.
- Unresolved contractual conflicts must be escalated rather than assumed away.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 2 — Umoja Overview and Grant Management Concepts
4. Q: How should a donor reporting calendar be designed and maintained?
A: Show answer
Build the calendar around each grant's reporting obligations rather than using one generic deadline for every donor. Record the reporting period, report type, contractual deadline, applicable time zone, template, submission channel, internal preparation deadline, reviewer deadline, certification date, approval date, and named owner. Include interim, cumulative, forecast, final, and closeout submissions where required. Allow sufficient time for partner reports, reconciliations, query resolution, and authorized approvals. Automated reminders should escalate overdue inputs and approaching deadlines to designated managers. Changes to reporting frequency or due dates require an identifiable source and an authorized update. Keep historical calendar versions so reviewers can explain what deadline applied when a report was prepared and whether the submission was timely. An invented date in a demonstration must never be copied into a live calendar without verification.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a calendar for one grant and test whether partner reporting, review, authorization, and submission milestones fit within the verified donor deadline."
Key point:
- Donor deadlines and internal target dates are different controls.
- Calendar changes must be evidence-based and traceable.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting Presentation
5. Q: Why must agreement, amendment, and budget versions be controlled separately?
A: Show answer
A report can be calculated incorrectly even when its formulas work perfectly if it uses a superseded agreement, an unapproved budget revision, or an outdated category mapping. Maintain unique identifiers, version numbers, effective dates, approval status, source files, and change descriptions for agreements, amendments, budgets, donor instructions, and mapping tables. At reporting time, determine which approved version applies to the relevant transaction and reporting period. A later document must not automatically be applied retrospectively without an authorized basis. When a version changes, identify affected transactions, reports, calculations, and prior approvals, then perform the necessary recalculation and review. Archive the earlier versions rather than overwriting them, preserving the ability to reconstruct historical submissions. The source example's fictional agreement identifiers demonstrate record structure, not real contractual authority.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate a budget amendment and trace its effective date, approval, affected mappings, recalculation, and reporting consequences."
Key point:
- Only an authorized and applicable version may govern a report.
- Material changes require impact assessment and potentially renewed approval.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 2 — Grant Management
6. Q: How should responsibilities and approval authority be assigned across the reporting lifecycle?
A: Show answer
Assign distinct responsibilities for source-data preparation, grant administration, transaction classification, eligibility assessment, calculation, reconciliation, independent review, programme certification, financial approval, submission, donor correspondence, and closure authorization. Use a responsibility matrix to identify who performs, approves, advises, and receives information at each step. Approval limits, legal entity, grant scope, currency, validity dates, and delegation documents must be recorded and verified. The person preparing a report should not be its sole independent reviewer or final approver. Where staffing constraints prevent complete segregation, document compensating controls, such as independent recalculation, supervisory review of portal receipts, and post-submission reconciliation. Consultants may perform delegated work but do not automatically replace the organization's own authorization and oversight obligations.

Study instruction:
- "Build a role matrix for each workflow and identify conflicts of duties, missing owners, expired delegations, and compensating controls."
Key point:
- Preparation, independent review, approval, and submission require controlled accountability.
- Delegated authority must be valid, appropriately scoped, and evidenced.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Financial Controls
7. Q: What source data should be collected before donor report preparation begins?
A: Show answer
Identify the complete population of potentially relevant transactions and records, including general ledger entries, accounts payable, payroll allocations, procurement records, partner expenditure reports, advances, payment records, asset registers, and other schedules specified by the agreement. Record the source system, extraction date, reporting period, query parameters, filters, record count, control totals, currency, version, preparer, and file identity. Obtain evidence that all required sources and periods are included. Data should be linked to supporting documents and the applicable grant, project, account, and budget line. A file's arrival does not establish that its population is complete or its values are accurate. Missing systems, failed extracts, omitted periods, and unexplained differences must be investigated before eligibility decisions and report totals are finalized.

Study instruction:
- "Create a source inventory and prove completeness using record counts, monetary totals, extraction parameters, and independent reconciliations."
Key point:
- Completeness must be demonstrated, not assumed from receiving a spreadsheet.
- Every reportable amount needs traceability to its source population.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Reconciliation Processes
8. Q: How should completeness of an accounting extract be demonstrated before calculating a donor claim?
A: Show answer
Reconcile the extracted population to an authoritative source using a defined query, reporting-period cutoff, inclusion criteria, number of records, debit and credit totals where appropriate, and monetary control totals by relevant dimension. Where extraction filters are used, document their rationale and test that they do not omit relevant transactions. Compare the extract with source-system totals and investigate unexplained discrepancies, missing documents, unexpected blank fields, and duplicate records. Retain a stable extract identity or hash where the available tools support it, together with the extraction details and review evidence. A zero difference is not sufficient if the source population itself is incomplete. Any unresolved completeness failure should prevent the affected report from receiving final approval until the failure is corrected or an authorized, evidence-based resolution is recorded.

Study instruction:
- "Test an extract containing an omitted period and a duplicate record, then verify that the controls detect both population errors."
Key point:
- Extraction accuracy and source-population completeness are separate assertions.
- Unexplained population differences block reliable reporting.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Reconciliation
9. Q: What is donor-category mapping, and how should it link transactions to approved budgets and general ledger accounts?
A: Show answer
Donor-category mapping translates accounting and operational classifications into the categories, budget lines, and formats required by a particular agreement or reporting template. Maintain a controlled mapping from source account and project codes to donor categories and approved budget lines, including effective dates, approval status, applicable grant, and any permitted split-allocation rule. Validate that each reportable transaction maps to an authorized destination. Missing, ambiguous, expired, or conflicting mappings should be flagged for review rather than assigned automatically to a generic category. A mapping change can affect multiple transactions and historical periods, so its effective date and impact must be recorded. The mapping supports presentation and calculation but does not, by itself, prove that an expenditure is eligible under the agreement.

Study instruction:
- "Test the mapping for every required budget line and identify unresolved, duplicate, expired, or unauthorized destinations."
Key point:
- Mapping supports consistent reporting but is not evidence of donor eligibility.
- Effective dates and approval records are essential to reproducible totals.
