- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 2 — Grant Management and Reporting Structures
10. Q: How should expenditure eligibility be distinguished from accounting recognition and available budget?
A: Show answer
Accounting recognition, budget availability, payment, and donor eligibility answer different questions. A transaction may be correctly recorded in the ledger yet be ineligible for reimbursement under a particular grant. Likewise, an unused budget does not establish that an expense meets the donor's conditions. Assess eligibility against the executed agreement, authorized budget, applicable period, permitted activity, supporting evidence, approval requirements, procurement conditions, allocation rules, and any required prior consent. The accounting treatment must still follow the organization's applicable accounting framework. Record the eligibility decision, evidence, reviewer, rationale, and exceptions separately from the accounting classification. Questioned or unsupported costs should not enter the clean eligible total until resolved under the documented procedure; their accounting treatment must be assessed rather than automatically reversed merely because the donor has questioned them.

Study instruction:
- "Compare an eligible expense, an unsupported expense, and an expense recorded to an available budget line; determine the evidence required for each decision."
Key point:
- Correct accounting does not automatically establish donor eligibility.
- Budget availability is not a substitute for contractual compliance and evidence.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Revenue from Non-Exchange Transactions
11. Q: What evidence should support a transaction included in a donor financial report?
A: Show answer
Evidence should establish the nature of the transaction, its occurrence, amount, period, coding, relationship to the funded activity, and compliance with applicable conditions. Depending on the cost, this may include an invoice, contract, purchase order, goods-received record, attendance record, timesheet, payroll allocation, delivery confirmation, payment record, partner expenditure report, or asset documentation. Evidence should be linked to a unique transaction reference and retained in a controlled location. Reviewers must evaluate the substance and reliability of the evidence rather than merely checking whether a file exists. Missing, conflicting, altered, or insufficient documents should be recorded as exceptions with an owner and resolution deadline. The absence of an invoice does not always determine accounting recognition, but it may prevent a conclusion that the donor-reporting evidence requirements have been satisfied.

Study instruction:
- "Select transactions from several cost categories and build an evidence checklist demonstrating the relationship between each item, its accounting entry, and its funded activity."
Key point:
- Supporting documents must substantiate the relevant assertion, not simply fill an attachment folder.
- Unsupported costs require documented review and resolution.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Processes
12. Q: How should duplicate transactions and expenses charged to multiple grants be controlled?
A: Show answer
Use a combination of transaction reference, supplier, invoice number, date, amount, currency, account, project, and supporting document identity to identify possible duplicates. Do not automatically reject transactions merely because their invoice numbers or amounts match, as different suppliers or legitimate instalments may create similar records. Separately test whether a single expense has been charged to more than one grant beyond the permitted allocation. An authorized cost allocation may distribute shared expenditure across grants, but the allocation method and total charged must be supported and must avoid double recovery. Investigate candidates, document the decision, and preserve rejected matches and corrections in the review trail. Compare source totals with the final report and ensure automated deduplication has not removed legitimate transactions or retained duplicate funding claims.

Study instruction:
- "Run a duplicate test using similar invoice numbers, repeated imports, and a cost allocated across two grants; verify each result against evidence."
Key point:
- Duplicate-record detection and duplicate-funding detection are different controls.
- Legitimate allocation is permitted only when supported and not counted twice.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Financial Records and Reconciliation
13. Q: How should foreign-currency expenditure be converted for donor reporting?
A: Show answer
Determine the conversion basis from the executed agreement, donor instructions, applicable accounting policy, and relevant requirements. Record the original currency and amount, exchange-rate source, applicable date, currency pair, conversion direction, resulting amount, rounding method, and approval of any exception. Distinguish the rate used for donor reimbursement calculations from the rate used to record transactions or retranslate balances under the accounting framework. These methods may differ. Independently recalculate a sample of conversions and investigate an inverted currency pair, an unsupported rate, inconsistent dates, or unexpected rounding. Do not invent an organizational rate or copy an illustrative rate from the supplied SOP. A material unresolved foreign-exchange issue should remain visible and be resolved before affected report figures are approved.

Study instruction:
- "Recalculate a foreign-currency transaction from its source amount to the reported amount and verify the exchange-rate source, date, direction, and rounding."
Key point:
- The correct conversion method depends on the applicable agreement and accounting requirements.
- A reproducible calculation needs complete rate and conversion evidence.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Statement Presentation and Reconciliation
14. Q: How should shared costs, indirect costs, and co-financing be calculated and supported?
A: Show answer
Identify the agreement-authorized cost categories, approved rates or allocation methods, eligible allocation pool, denominator, allocation driver, co-financing commitments, and applicable reporting period. Recalculate the allocation independently and confirm that the underlying pool is complete, the driver is supported, and amounts allocated across grants reconcile to the original cost. For indirect costs, verify the applicable rate, base, exclusions, and effective approval rather than assuming a standard percentage. Co-financing should be reported in accordance with the donor's definition and supporting evidence, without counting the same financing twice. If the agreement requires specific approval for a rate, budget movement, or allocation method, document that approval. Unapproved rates, unsupported denominators, and inconsistently applied methods should block the affected calculation until resolved.

Study instruction:
- "Recompute one shared-cost allocation and one indirect-cost calculation from the approved method, then reconcile allocated amounts to their source pools."
Key point:
- Allocation formulas must be authorized, evidence-based, and independently reproducible.
- Co-financing and eligible expenditure must follow the relevant agreement's definitions.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting and Reconciliation
15. Q: How should cumulative reporting be reconciled with previously accepted reports and current-period expenditure?
A: Show answer
Maintain a roll-forward that identifies prior accepted cumulative amounts, current-period movements, corrections to earlier periods, and the resulting cumulative position. Distinguish accounting-period expenditure from donor-claim amounts and from amounts the donor has formally accepted. Apply the agreement's rules for cumulative reporting, adjustments, and prior claims. When a previously submitted amount changes, preserve the earlier report and identify whether the change requires a revised report, donor notification, or other authorized action. Compare the cumulative total with the current period's increment and investigate any unexplained difference or duplicated prior-period amount. A later report must not silently overwrite the historical submission or imply that the donor accepted a revised amount without evidence of the donor's decision.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile two prior cumulative reports to the current report and trace one historical correction from its original submission through its authorized treatment."
Key point:
- Cumulative totals must preserve a transparent link to earlier periods and corrections.
- Submitted, accepted, and accounted amounts are distinct states.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting
16. Q: What is the difference between budget-to-actual reconciliation and report-to-GL reconciliation?
A: Show answer
Budget-to-actual reconciliation compares expenditure or other financial measures with the approved budget using the relevant reporting categories and approved budget version. Report-to-GL reconciliation compares reported amounts with the underlying accounting records and supporting schedules. The first helps explain available budget, variance, and potential budget-line pressure; the second establishes whether the report agrees with accounting records and identifies differences in population, coding, timing, allocation, or calculation. Neither reconciliation alone proves donor eligibility. Both must use identified source versions, clear reporting periods, and documented treatment of reconciling items. Material or unexplained differences must be investigated rather than forced to zero through unsupported adjustments. Corrections to the ledger must follow the authorized accounting process, not merely be entered into a reporting workbook.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare both reconciliations for the same period and classify each difference by cause, evidence, owner, and required resolution."
Key point:
- Budget compliance and accounting accuracy are related but different assertions.
- A balancing spreadsheet does not validate an incomplete population or unsupported expenditure.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Reconciliation
17. Q: How should a report discrepancy be investigated and resolved?
A: Show answer
Record the amount, account or report line, source population, period, identified cause, potential impact, responsible owner, due date, and supporting evidence. Investigate common causes such as missing transactions, duplicates, incorrect mappings, cut-off differences, allocation errors, foreign-exchange conversion, stale source data, unposted journals, or incorrect spreadsheet formulas. Decide whether the issue requires an accounting correction, a report adjustment permitted by the governing rules, further evidence, specialist review, or donor clarification. The preparer must not independently approve their own correction. Once the issue is resolved, update the relevant records, rerun affected calculations and reconciliations, and have an independent reviewer verify the result. If the discrepancy remains unresolved, keep it open and prevent final approval where it breaches a mandatory control or makes the reported figure unreliable.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a sample discrepancy from detection through root-cause analysis, authorized correction, independent retesting, and closure evidence."
Key point:
- Every material discrepancy needs a supported cause and controlled resolution.
- Corrections require rechecking all affected calculations and reports.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Adjustments
18. Q: What should an independent reviewer verify before a donor financial report is approved?
A: Show answer
The reviewer should examine completeness, eligibility decisions, mapping versions, agreement compliance, calculation methods, currency conversions, allocations, cumulative figures, budget variances, report-to-GL reconciliation, supporting schedules, questioned costs, and open exceptions. Verify that the correct reporting period, template, grant identifier, currency, and agreement version are used. Recalculate material or high-risk amounts independently and challenge unusual fluctuations, unsupported manual entries, and differences from prior reports. Confirm that required programme or budget-holder certifications are attached and that unresolved specialist issues have not bypassed mandatory gates. Evidence the reviewer, date, version reviewed, procedures performed, exceptions raised, and final disposition. An informal verbal confirmation or a signature on a different file version is not a sufficient record of review.

Study instruction:
- "Apply a review checklist to a sample report, including independent recalculation and verification of each material exception's resolution."
Key point:
- Independent review must address substance, calculations, completeness, and evidence.
- Approval applies only to the version actually reviewed.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation and Reconciliation
19. Q: How should authorization be linked to the exact report submitted to a donor?
A: Show answer
Assign every report package a unique identifier and version. Record its preparation date, reporting period, source versions, calculations, supporting schedules, reviewer, approver, delegated authority, and approval date. Where appropriate technical controls exist, generate a hash of the approved package and retain the immutable or controlled copy. Before submission, verify that the package selected for upload is identical to the authorized version. A changed file, recalculated figure, or replaced schedule can invalidate the evidence supporting earlier approval. Material changes should trigger renewed review and approval. The submission register should identify the exact package sent, authorized sender, channel, timestamp, and receipt reference. Keep drafts separate from approved and submitted copies so that staff cannot mistake the most recent editable workbook for the authorized submission.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate a report amendment after approval and demonstrate how version comparison prevents submission of an unauthorized package."
Key point:
- Approval must be linked to the exact version and authorized delegation.
- Any material post-approval change requires impact assessment and renewed authorization.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Workflows and Controls
20. Q: How should submission, donor receipt, donor acceptance, claim approval, and cash settlement be distinguished?
A: Show answer
These states represent different events. Submission means the package was sent through the required channel. Receipt means there is evidence that the donor's system or authorized recipient received it. Acceptance means the donor has confirmed an applicable acceptance status, where the process requires that status. Claim approval is a separate decision on the amount or entitlement, and settlement concerns the resulting financial obligation, such as payment or refund. Do not infer acceptance from an upload confirmation or assume a submitted claim is an account receivable or cash receipt without applying the relevant recognition criteria. Record the evidence and date for each transition, together with the report version concerned. Use distinct controlled statuses and retain the actual donor communication or system receipt to support each assertion.

Study instruction:
- "Inspect a donor portal history and identify separate evidence for submission, receipt, acceptance, claim approval, and settlement."
Key point:
- A submission receipt does not prove donor acceptance or settlement.
- Each status transition requires evidence appropriate to the event.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Revenue from Non-Exchange Transactions
21. Q: What should happen when a donor rejects a report or requests clarification?
A: Show answer
Register the query, rejection, or clarification request with its receipt date, original correspondence, deadline, report version, affected claim lines, assigned owner, and required response. Assess whether the request indicates a missing document, eligibility concern, calculation error, mapping problem, agreement interpretation, or other issue. Determine the impact on the report, accounting records, claims, prior submissions, and related donor communications. Prepare a response with evidence and any necessary correction, subject to independent review and authorization. Preserve the original submission and query history rather than overwriting them. Submit the response or revised report through the authorized channel, link it to the original case, and record actual receipt evidence. Keep the query open until the required resolution has been achieved or an authorized residual action has been formally recorded.

Study instruction:
- "Process a fictional donor query from receipt to impact assessment, correction, response approval, resubmission, and evidenced closure."
Key point:
- Donor queries need an owner, deadline, impact assessment, and retained correspondence.
- Resubmission does not automatically establish acceptance of the revised claim.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting and Disclosures
22. Q: How should accounting corrections related to donor reporting be processed?
A: Show answer
First establish whether the issue is an accounting error, a donor-reporting classification difference, an unsupported claim, or a change in information. Determine the affected accounts, periods, grant codes, financial statements, reports, payments, and prior approvals. Accounting corrections must follow the organization's applicable accounting policy and authorized journal process. A reporting adjustment that does not change the ledger must be clearly distinguished from an actual journal correction. Preserve the original report, journal, supporting evidence, rationale, and any donor correspondence. Reperform affected calculations and reconciliations, assess whether previously submitted reports require correction, and obtain the necessary approvals. Do not change historical ledgers or replace archived evidence merely to make a current report agree with an expected amount.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a ledger error with a report-mapping error and document the correct resolution path for each."
Key point:
- Ledger corrections and reporting adjustments are not interchangeable.
- Historical changes require authorized procedures and a traceable audit trail.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Adjustments
23. Q: How should partner expenditure reports and partner advances be verified before grant closure?
A: Show answer
Obtain the partner's complete report, transaction listing, supporting documents required by the agreement, and a reconciliation to amounts transferred and previously reported. Verify expenditure against the applicable grant period, approved budget, permitted activities, and partner-reporting requirements. Use risk-based sampling where permitted, and define the population, selection method, evidence obtained, exceptions found, and limitations of the work. Separate accepted costs from questioned or unsupported items, and do not automatically include all partner-reported expenditure in the donor claim. Reconcile each advance from opening balance through disbursements, supported liquidation, and evidenced refunds. Unresolved differences must remain open with a named partner owner, deadline, escalation route, and documented impact on the final claim and closeout decision.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile one partner's reported costs and advance balance, including a questioned amount and an unverified refund."
Key point:
- Partner-reported totals require verification appropriate to the risk and agreement.
- An advance remains open until its balance is properly supported and resolved.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Financial Reconciliation and Controls
24. Q: How should staff, field, and partner advances be reconciled at final closeout?
A: Show answer
Maintain separate records for each advance, identifying the recipient, authorization, purpose, grant, disbursement date, amount, currency, expected liquidation date, supported expenditure, questioned expenditure, refunds, and closing balance. A basic roll-forward is opening balance plus advances issued, less accepted liquidation and verified refunds, equals the remaining balance, subject to any separately documented corrections or foreign-exchange effects. Verify that partner reports have been reviewed and that liquidation amounts are not counted both as expenses and as outstanding advances. Age overdue balances, assign recovery owners, and escalate unresolved items according to approved thresholds. Do not clear a balance simply because the grant has ended or a partner has submitted a statement. Closure requires appropriate evidence, accounting treatment, authorization, and documentation of any legally approved residual arrangement.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare an advance roll-forward and trace the ending balance to accepted costs, refund evidence, and the ledger."
Key point:
- An issued advance is not automatically eligible expenditure.
- Unliquidated advances require a documented resolution and authorized closure treatment.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
25. Q: How should the final donor claim and any related receivable or payable be determined?
A: Show answer
Recalculate the final claim from the complete, verified eligible expenditure population and the relevant contractual calculation rules. Reconcile prior accepted claims, current eligible costs, authorized adjustments, other reportable income, partner balances, and any other agreement-specified components. Determine whether the donor agreement permits recovery of the resulting amount and whether the applicable accounting framework's recognition criteria for a receivable are met. A submitted claim does not, by itself, prove that a receivable exists in the required amount. Similarly, a rejected cost does not automatically determine the accounting treatment without assessing the underlying facts and requirements. Review material uncertainties, disputed amounts, and possible repayment obligations with the appropriate finance or specialist authority, and document assumptions, decisions, and evidence before approving the final claim.

Study instruction:
- "Recalculate a final claim and distinguish its accounting impact from submission status and the donor's subsequent claim decision."
Key point:
- Final claims must follow verified expenditure and the executed agreement.
- Receivable and payable recognition must follow the applicable accounting framework.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
26. Q: How should unspent grant funds and donor refunds be calculated and settled?
A: Show answer
Determine the meaning of unspent funds and the treatment of remaining cash, eligible expenditure, outstanding obligations, interest or other income, advances, and refunds from the executed agreement and applicable requirements. Reconcile the final calculation to the accounting records and verified donor receipts. Do not assume that the entire cash balance equals the refundable amount or that all unspent amounts can be retained. Verify any authorized retention, carry-forward, offset, or refund arrangement and document the decision. Match refunds to actual payment evidence and the relevant grant liability or settlement record. If the amount, authority, or destination is unresolved, keep the obligation visible, identify the responsible owner and deadline, and prevent financial closure while a mandatory settlement condition remains outstanding.

Study instruction:
- "Recalculate an unspent balance using the agreement's actual rule and distinguish the calculated refund obligation from the proof of payment."
Key point:
- Refunds and retention depend on the applicable contractual and legal conditions.
- A calculated refund is not settled until the required settlement evidence exists.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Revenue from Non-Exchange Transactions
27. Q: How should grant-funded assets be identified, accounted for, and disposed of at closure?
A: Show answer
Identify assets acquired or received through the grant by linking the original transaction to the asset register, physical identification, location, custodian, condition, ownership information, and applicable donor conditions. Determine the accounting treatment using the organization's applicable framework and capitalization policy; donor funding alone does not determine whether an item qualifies for recognition as an asset. Assess control, useful life, and other relevant recognition criteria. Separately examine agreement conditions for transfer, continued use, sale, retention, or return of equipment. Required donor consent and legal approvals must be obtained before disposition. Retain handover forms, recipient acknowledgments, sale proceeds, or other evidence appropriate to the authorized action. An asset must not be marked as disposed of merely because the programme ended or the purchase cost was included in a final report.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one grant-funded equipment purchase from its source transaction through asset recognition, donor-consent assessment, and evidenced disposition."
Key point:
- Accounting recognition and donor-authorized disposition are separate decisions.
- Asset closure requires evidence of ownership, required consent, and completion of the authorized action.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
28. Q: How should expenditure on an asset be distinguished from an ordinary operating expense in a donor report?
A: Show answer
Begin with the applicable accounting framework and organizational policy to determine whether the item meets the criteria for asset recognition or should be expensed. Consider the nature of the item, control, expected service potential or economic benefits, useful life, materiality where applicable, and the organization's approved capitalization policy. Separately determine how the donor agreement requires the acquisition to be budgeted and reported. A purchase may be accounted for as an asset but treated under a particular donor budget category, subject to contractual rules. Conversely, a donor budget label does not override the accounting conclusion. Ensure the asset register, general ledger, donor report, depreciation or other subsequent accounting, and any disposition requirements are connected and reconciled.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the accounting classification and donor-reporting category for a capital item and a routine consumable, citing the policies that govern each."
Key point:
- Accounting classification and donor budget classification serve different purposes.
- Capitalization thresholds and treatment must be verified under the applicable policy.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
29. Q: What conditions must be satisfied before a grant can be financially closed?
A: Show answer
The closure review should establish that the final source population is complete; applicable agreement, budget, and mapping versions are confirmed; eligibility decisions are supported; calculations and reconciliations are independently reviewed; prior claims and report versions are linked; partner expenditure and advances are resolved; assets have the required ownership and disposition evidence; unspent funds and refunds are dealt with; donor queries and rejections are closed or routed through an authorized residual arrangement; and final claims and settlements are correctly distinguished. Required accounting records and supporting schedules must also be complete. Archive retrievability and any legally applicable retention requirements should be verified. Every mandatory gate needs explicit evidence and an authorized result. If a gate fails, the grant remains in a controlled open or blocked state with an owner and resolution deadline instead of being declared closed for administrative convenience.

Study instruction:
- "Apply a final closeout checklist and identify which unresolved conditions must block closure under the approved SOP."
Key point:
- A grant is not financially closed solely because its activity period has ended.
- Mandatory closure conditions require evidence-backed completion or an expressly authorized residual route.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting and Disclosures
30. Q: How should residual obligations be handled when a grant cannot yet be fully closed?
A: Show answer
Identify the unresolved matter, including its financial value where known, contractual or accounting implications, evidence still required, responsible owner, next action, due date, escalation route, and impact on closure. Residual matters may include disputed claims, unsettled advances, unconfirmed refunds, incomplete asset actions, donor queries, missing records, or unresolved reconciliations. Determine whether the applicable agreement and policies permit a controlled residual status and what authority is required. A residual route must not be used to bypass a mandatory requirement or falsely state that a payment or donor decision has occurred. Keep monitoring the item until the required evidence is obtained or an authorized disposition is documented. Closure of an individual task must be distinguished from closure of the overall grant.

Study instruction:
- "Create a residual-obligation register and distinguish mandatory open gates from actions that may be carried under an authorized arrangement."
Key point:
- Every unresolved matter needs an owner, deadline, and controlled status.
- A residual route must be authorized and must not conceal outstanding obligations.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Reconciliation Controls
31. Q: How should exceptions, incidents, and corrective actions be managed throughout the grant lifecycle?
A: Show answer
Record each exception with a unique identifier, detection date, affected grant and report, issue category, financial value or exposure, severity, root cause, immediate containment, accountable owner, due date, required approvals, and closure evidence. Distinguish a one-time processing mistake from a systemic defect, inadequate training, unclear agreement terms, weak segregation of duties, or an unreliable data interface. The corrective action should address the cause, not merely repair the current spreadsheet. Escalate overdue and high-risk items according to approved rules. Following remediation, independently retest the control and retain the test result. A failed retest keeps the matter open. Trend analysis can identify recurring risks across grants and inform changes to workflows, templates, training, or system controls.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one material exception through containment, root-cause analysis, corrective action, independent retesting, and documented closure."
Key point:
- Exceptions need owners, deadlines, evidence, and verified resolution.
- Corrective action should prevent recurrence rather than only repair a symptom.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Financial Control and Adjustment Processes
32. Q: How should financial-reporting records be archived, protected, and retained?
A: Show answer
Use a stable structure that links each grant and reporting period to the agreement, amendments, approved budget, source extracts, calculation worksheets, reconciliations, review evidence, approvals, submitted report, receipt, donor decisions, queries, partner schedules, asset records, settlement evidence, and closure authorization. Preserve original submissions and earlier versions rather than overwriting them. Restrict access according to role and legitimate business need, especially where records contain personal, payroll, banking, or confidential donor information. Establish retention periods from the applicable law, agreement, organizational schedule, and other binding requirements; do not invent a universal retention period. Verify backups and test retrieval of a sample archive. Record the storage location, access permissions, retention basis, disposition authority, and any legal or donor hold applicable to the records.

Study instruction:
- "Retrieve a closed reporting period from the archive and verify access control, version integrity, completeness, and the authorized retention basis."
Key point:
- An archive must be complete, secure, and practically retrievable.
- Retention and deletion must follow verified requirements and authorization.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
33. Q: How should the organization monitor whether donor reporting and grant closeout controls are effective?
A: Show answer
Define measurable indicators linked to control objectives, including on-time submissions, completeness of required evidence, number and age of unresolved discrepancies, questioned-cost exposure, outstanding partner advances, open donor queries, overdue refunds, incomplete asset actions, repeat corrections, and time between final activity and financial closure. Specify each metric's source population, formula, owner, frequency, threshold, limitations, and escalation rule. For example, an on-time submission rate should include all required submissions in the denominator, not only those submitted successfully. Verify dashboard results against the obligation register and actual evidence. Review trends across periods and grants, investigate deterioration, and assign corrective actions. A high completion percentage should not hide a single unresolved mandatory control or material exposure.

Study instruction:
- "Define five control KPIs and independently recompute their results from the underlying grant and reporting registers."
Key point:
- Reliable KPIs require clear definitions and complete populations.
- Management must investigate risk, not rely only on aggregate completion rates.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting and Disclosures
34. Q: How can Excel and Power Query improve donor reporting without replacing financial controls?
A: Show answer
Excel tables and Power Query can combine approved source extracts, standardize field names, normalize dates and currencies, join transaction records to controlled master data, flag duplicates, and prepare reconciliation schedules. The resulting model should preserve source references, extract parameters, mapping versions, record counts, control totals, and exception details. Data transformations should be tested for omitted rows, incorrect joins, duplicate records, unexpected nulls, and changed data types. Workbook calculations must remain aligned with the applicable accounting framework and actual agreement requirements. Automated outputs must not silently decide complex eligibility issues or create authoritative approvals. Retain a clear separation between raw data, transformation and calculation layers, review workpapers, and final reports. Independently test a sample before relying on a refreshed model for reporting or closure decisions.

Study instruction:
- "Build a controlled query that combines transaction and budget data, then test duplicate detection, missing mappings, control totals, and source traceability."
Key point:
- Automation improves consistency only when source completeness and business rules are controlled.
- Software cannot establish contractual eligibility or accounting validity by itself.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Reconciliation Processes
35. Q: How can VBA help control report approvals, submission records, and audit trails?
A: Show answer
VBA may automate repetitive administrative tasks such as generating report packs, recording workflow status changes, exporting controlled PDFs, writing output filenames and timestamps to a log, and creating exception summaries. The macro should preserve source references, identify the relevant report version, validate required fields, and stop safely when mandatory inputs are missing. Approval must still come from an authorized person through the organization's approved process. A macro must not mark a donor report as accepted merely because an export or upload attempt succeeded. Log material actions, errors, user-provided identifiers, and affected output versions, while avoiding inappropriate exposure of personal or confidential information. Protect the code and configuration under controlled change management and independently test outputs before production use.

Study instruction:
- "Test a reporting macro using missing inputs, a changed report version, an export failure, and an unauthorized approval state."
Key point:
- VBA can support workflow discipline but cannot replace authorization or donor evidence.
- Error handling, traceability, and independent testing are essential controls.

36. Q: What negative tests should be performed before approving a grant-reporting workflow for operational use?
A: Show answer
Test scenarios in which the signed agreement is missing, an unsigned amendment is treated as current, an obsolete budget is selected, a source extract is incomplete, a transaction is duplicated, one cost is charged to two grants, a required donor consent is absent, an exchange rate is unsupported, an allocation denominator is incorrect, an advance is counted as expenditure, the report does not reconcile to the ledger, or an approval relates to a different version. Also test an attempted closeout with unresolved refunds, donor queries, partner balances, or asset actions. Define the expected control response for each case: block, hold, route for specialist review, or require a specific correction. Record the test input, expected result, actual result, evidence, severity, and owner. A failed mandatory test must be corrected and retested independently before release.

Study instruction:
- "Design at least ten negative test cases and document the expected failure route, evidence, correction, and independent retest."
Key point:
- Testing only successful scenarios leaves important control failures undiscovered.
- A failed mandatory test must not be recorded as passed without a successful retest.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Financial Controls and Reconciliations
37. Q: How should workflow statuses and transitions be controlled across reporting and closure?
A: Show answer
Define permitted states such as draft, prepared, under review, returned for correction, approved, submitted, received, accepted, queried, resubmitted, settled, and closed, adapting them to the actual process. Specify which role can move an item between states, what evidence is required, and what conditions block the transition. Distinguish the status of an individual report from the status of the grant as a whole. A record must not move directly from draft to accepted or from open to closed without satisfying the corresponding evidence requirements. Maintain the previous state, event date, user or authorized actor, related version, and transition evidence. Where a transition fails, retain a controlled blocked or returned status and a linked corrective action rather than forcing the workflow to appear complete.

Study instruction:
- "Create a status-transition matrix and test whether an unauthorized user can move a report to approved, accepted, or closed."
Key point:
- Every state transition requires defined authority and evidence.
- Report status and overall grant status must remain distinct.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Workflows and Controls
38. Q: How should the organization respond to a failed upload, interrupted submission, or uncertain portal status?
A: Show answer
Preserve the attempted package, its exact version, timestamp, channel, error message, and any receipt or transaction identifier. Check the authoritative portal state before resubmitting, because an interrupted response does not necessarily mean the submission failed. Confirm whether the original package was received, rejected, or partially processed, and follow the channel's documented recovery procedure. If the system remains unavailable, record the incident, contact the authorized support channel, and escalate risk to the responsible manager. Do not assume an extension or alternate submission route exists without an official basis. Once the state is resolved, verify the actual receipt, link it to the approved package, and reconcile the register with the portal. Keep unresolved submission status open until supported evidence establishes the outcome.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate an interrupted upload and document the checks required before deciding whether to retry or escalate."
Key point:
- An error message or missing confirmation does not establish the final submission outcome.
- Retrying without checking status can create duplicate or conflicting submissions.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting
39. Q: How should changes in accounting policies, donor instructions, or regulations be incorporated into the SOP?
A: Show answer
Assign a responsible owner to monitor authoritative sources and relevant organizational decisions. For each change, document the source, publication and effective dates, affected grants and reporting periods, impacted calculations and templates, necessary training, and approvals. Assess whether historical reports, outstanding claims, ledger entries, or current submissions are affected. Do not change a formula simply because a new template or informal message appears to require it. Changes should be tested against representative transactions, reviewed by appropriate specialists, approved by authorized personnel, and released with a controlled version and effective date. Communicate the change to affected users and preserve earlier procedures where needed to reconstruct prior-period decisions. If the effect or applicability of a rule is uncertain, keep the impacted treatment on hold pending an authorized determination.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one policy or donor-instruction change from authoritative source through impact assessment, testing, approval, release, and user communication."
Key point:
- Rule changes require verified authority and an assessment of their actual impact.
- Version control preserves the ability to explain historical reporting decisions.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual
40. Q: How should an auditor or reviewer trace a reported figure from the donor report back to its source?
A: Show answer
Start with a selected report line and identify the calculation, donor mapping, budget line, relevant ledger accounts, transaction population, and individual entries supporting the amount. Trace selected transactions in the opposite direction from source documents through the ledger and reporting transformation to confirm that relevant items are not omitted. Verify agreement applicability, eligibility evidence, foreign-exchange or allocation calculations, review decisions, and the report version submitted. Record the identifiers and evidence inspected, the population and sampling method, discrepancies found, limitations, and resulting conclusions. Reconciliation alone is not sufficient if the underlying population is incomplete or eligibility is unsupported. Preserve the chain of evidence so another reviewer can reproduce the result without relying solely on the original preparer's explanation.

Study instruction:
- "Perform bidirectional tracing on a material report line and document every system, schedule, approval, and source record encountered."
Key point:
- Effective traceability connects each reported amount to underlying evidence and authorized decisions.
- A reproducible audit trail requires more than matching final totals.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Reconciliation
41. Q: What should happen when a donor-reporting procedure conflicts with another organizational process?
A: Show answer
Identify the boundary between donor reporting and the process that owns the underlying action. Donor reporting does not independently authorize procurement, payroll, partner disbursements, journal entries, bank transactions, tax filings, asset transfers, refunds, or incident responses. Refer the matter to the relevant accounts payable, procurement, treasury, tax, partner-management, asset-management, legal, or security process. Create a linked referral record containing the issue, responsible receiving role, due date, requested action, financial impact, and closure evidence. Ensure the reporting workflow retains oversight of the effect on the grant claim and closure decision. Conflicts between procedures should be resolved through authorized governance, not by allowing a donor-reporting spreadsheet to override the control requirements of the process responsible for the transaction.

Study instruction:
- "Choose three cross-functional issues and identify the owning process, referral evidence, reporting impact, and escalation path."
Key point:
- Donor reporting coordinates financial evidence but does not replace other control processes.
- Every referral must remain traceable until the required outcome is evidenced.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Workflows
42. Q: How should a fictional SOP example be converted into a live, approved organizational procedure?
A: Show answer
Begin by replacing fictional entities, agreements, amounts, exchange rates, source records, role names, approvals, donor decisions, and settlement evidence with verified organizational information. Confirm the legal entity, accounting framework, jurisdiction, signed agreement, approved budget, effective amendments, applicable donor requirements, internal policies, and authorized roles. Validate every workflow, control, form, status transition, formula, source link, and test against the real operating environment. Run realistic positive and negative tests, reconcile outputs independently, and record any limitations or unresolved issues. Obtain review from the responsible finance, grants, legal, tax, asset, or other specialists as relevant. Secure formal adoption by authorized management and record the effective date, owner, review schedule, and approval evidence. Until these steps are complete, clearly label the procedure as a controlled draft, not an approved SOP.

Study instruction:
- "Create an adoption checklist separating verified organizational facts, fictional assumptions, mandatory tests, outstanding approvals, and release evidence."
Key point:
- A detailed fictional procedure is a design reference, not evidence of actual compliance.
- Formal adoption requires verified applicability, testing, documented approvals, and an effective version.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual
