Table 9
Q&A In-Kind Donation and Donated Asset Controls
1. Q: What is the purpose of an in-kind donation and donated asset standard operating procedure (SOP)?
A: Show answer
An in-kind donation SOP establishes a controlled process for managing non-cash contributions from initial offer through due diligence, acceptance, physical receipt, ownership or control assessment, accounting analysis, valuation, custody, permitted use, reporting, and eventual return or disposal. It connects Finance, Programs, Procurement, Warehousing, Asset Management, Grants, Legal, Tax, Customs, Safety, and Internal Audit without transferring their specialist responsibilities to a single department. Its purpose is to ensure that donations are suitable for the organization, lawful to receive, properly documented, accurately recorded, protected against loss or misuse, and used according to applicable restrictions. An SOP does not itself establish ownership, authorize acceptance, prove fair value, or determine accounting recognition. Each decision must be supported by evidence and approved under the organization's actual delegation of authority. The supplied SOP is explicitly a fictional demonstration, so its organization, transaction records, approvals, and example amounts must not be treated as facts or adopted policy. The real organization must complete the required legal, accounting, donor, safety, and operational assessments before implementation.

Study instruction:
- "Map the complete donation lifecycle and identify the evidence, responsible role, decision gate, and approval required at each stage."

Key point:
- An SOP governs the donation lifecycle but does not replace law, accounting standards, donor agreements, or approved organizational policy.
- The fictional example must remain separate from actual organizational decisions.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Revenue from Non-Exchange Transactions
2. Q: How should an organization distinguish an offer, a pledge, a dispatch, a receipt, an acceptance, and an accounting recognition event?
A: Show answer
These are separate events that require different evidence. An offer records the donor's proposal but does not establish that the organization has accepted it. A pledge or promise records a proposed future transfer and must be assessed under the applicable accounting framework and binding arrangement terms. Dispatch establishes that goods have been sent, not that the organization has received or controlled them. Physical receipt establishes possession of the quantity actually delivered, but it does not necessarily establish legal title, enforceable rights, or accounting recognition. Acceptance is the authorized decision to proceed after the required due diligence and approval gates have been completed. Accounting recognition is a separate conclusion reached under the applicable reporting framework after examining the rights, control, recognition criteria, measurement requirements, and any associated obligations. Each event should have its own date, status, evidence reference, and authorized owner. The sequence may differ for enforceable arrangements or specific contractual terms, so the SOP must not impose one assumed accounting date on every donation.

Study instruction:
- "Compare an offer letter, dispatch note, goods-received record, acceptance approval, and accounting decision for one donation, then explain what each document proves."

Key point:
- Receipt, acceptance, control, and accounting recognition are not interchangeable.
- Record the actual event and its supporting evidence rather than inferring completion from a document label.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3
- IPSASB — Revenue, including IPSAS 47
3. Q: Which accounting framework should determine the recognition and measurement of donated goods and assets?
A: Show answer
The organization must first establish its legal form, jurisdiction, reporting obligations, applicable Indonesian Financial Accounting Standards (SAK) framework or other reporting basis, and relevant organizational policies. A nonprofit or foundation status does not automatically establish that the organization must use IPSAS. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual is a valuable reference for understanding public-sector accounting processes and the United Nations' treatment of in-kind contributions. However, its recognition thresholds, operational procedures, system instructions, and accounting policies are specific to the relevant UN reporting environment and must not automatically be applied to an Indonesian NGO. IPSAS 47, Revenue, became effective for reporting periods beginning on or after 1 January 2026, replacing IPSAS 23, IPSAS 9, and IPSAS 11 within the IPSAS framework, subject to applicable transition and early-adoption provisions. Consequently, an organization actually applying IPSAS must consult the standards effective for its reporting period rather than relying exclusively on an older finance-manual chapter. The Chief Accountant and an appropriately qualified independent reviewer should document the framework decision, applicable standard, relevant paragraphs, effective date, and approval before the SOP is finalized.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a framework applicability memorandum identifying the organization's reporting basis, the current standard, the effective period, and the paragraphs governing donated goods."

Key point:
- Select the accounting framework before designing recognition and measurement rules.
- The UN Finance Manual is not automatically the governing accounting authority for every NGO.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Accounting Manual
- IPSASB — Revenue
- Institute of Indonesia Chartered Accountants (IAI)
4. Q: How should accounting standards, legislation, donor agreements, and internal procedures be applied when their requirements appear to conflict?
A: Show answer
Build a documented hierarchy of applicable requirements rather than blending conflicting instructions informally. Mandatory legislation, valid permits, and regulatory requirements apply within their legal scope. The approved financial reporting framework determines accounting recognition, measurement, presentation, and disclosure. Executed donor agreements and transfer instruments establish the contractual rights, restrictions, conditions, consent provisions, and return obligations they validly impose. Approved internal policies and work instructions operationalize these requirements but cannot override mandatory law or authorize a transaction beyond delegated authority. If two requirements appear inconsistent, the responsible specialist should identify the exact clauses, determine whether both apply, assess the consequences, and refer the unresolved issue to the authorized decision-maker or legal adviser. Preserve the original sources, amendments, applicability assessment, interpretation, and final approval. A donor's preference for a particular accounting entry cannot replace the applicable accounting standard, just as a favorable accounting conclusion cannot override an import prohibition or safety requirement.

Study instruction:
- "Resolve a hypothetical conflict between a donor's requested treatment, an internal policy, and the applicable accounting framework using documented sources and authorized escalation."

Key point:
- Every requirement needs an applicability assessment and an identified decision authority.
- Unresolved conflicts must remain on hold rather than being silently combined or ignored.

Source links:
- IPSASB — Revenue
- Indonesian Financial Accounting Standards Institute (IAI)
5. Q: How should in-kind donations be classified when they first enter the organization’s process?
A: Show answer
Register every offer or discovered item under a unique identifier and classify it according to its actual nature and arrangement. Relevant categories include an inquiry or pledge, a proposed outright gift, a conditional transfer, tangible goods, equipment, consumable supplies, inventory for distribution, property, a donated intangible asset, a donated service, a right to use property, a loan, a consignment, custody-only goods, a partial shipment, a regulated item, or a refused donation. Classification affects the evidence required, the specialist reviews triggered, the accounting questions to resolve, and the controls governing custody and subsequent use. Record the donor, description, proposed quantity and unit, condition, serial or lot number where relevant, intended purpose, destination, proposed transfer date, claimed value, available terms, and responsible coordinator. Do not infer ownership or accounting treatment from the category name. If essential facts are unknown, use an explicit unresolved classification and refer the item for review before an irreversible decision is made.

Study instruction:
- "Classify sample offers into tangible goods, consumables, donated services, right-to-use arrangements, loans, custody-only items, and regulated goods, explaining the evidence needed for each."

Key point:
- Classification determines the applicable assessment and control path.
- An intake category is a starting point for investigation, not a final accounting conclusion.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory
6. Q: What due diligence should be completed before an in-kind donation is accepted?
A: Show answer
The organization should verify the donor's identity and authority to transfer the item, its provenance and authenticity, any competing ownership claim, the proposed transfer terms, intended use, program need, recipient suitability, and known legal or ethical concerns. Evaluate whether the item is appropriate for the program, compatible with existing equipment or systems, and usable within the relevant period. Assess transport, insurance, storage, maintenance, training, consumables, disposal costs, and other lifecycle costs. Trigger additional reviews for regulated goods, imported equipment, hazardous materials, medicine, personal data, security-sensitive products, or items with safeguarding implications. The donation coordinator manages the evidence and deadlines, while Legal, Grants, Tax, Customs, Safety, Finance, and program specialists make decisions within their responsibilities. The authorized approver must confirm that mandatory requirements are complete and that accepting the donation is in the organization's interest. A donor's reputation, generosity, or urgency is not a substitute for due diligence.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a due-diligence checklist for a proposed equipment donation and identify the documents required from the donor and from internal specialists."

Key point:
- Acceptance requires evidence of donor authority, suitability, and compliance with applicable requirements.
- Lifecycle costs and risks must be considered before committing the organization to a donation.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Accounting Manual
7. Q: When should a donation be rejected, placed on hold, or quarantined?
A: Show answer
Reject a donation when there is a substantiated reason that it cannot lawfully, safely, ethically, or operationally be accepted, or when the authorized decision-maker concludes that its terms or costs are unacceptable. Place it on hold when a mandatory decision is unresolved, such as uncertain donor authority, missing title or transfer evidence, unclear contractual restrictions, an unverified accounting framework, incomplete permits, or an unsupported safety assessment. Quarantine physical goods when inspection identifies damage, contamination, suspected counterfeiting, expiration, a quantity discrepancy, or another condition requiring the item to be isolated from usable stock. The record must identify the hold or refusal reason, responsible owner, risk, required evidence, deadline, and escalation route. Access and movement restrictions must prevent accidental use while the issue is unresolved. A hold may be released only after the required conditions are met and independently verified. A refused item must not appear as an accepted donation in operational or accounting totals merely because it remains temporarily in the organization's possession.

Study instruction:
- "Design separate decision paths for acceptance, conditional hold, quarantine, and refusal, including the required evidence to release each hold."

Key point:
- Unresolved mandatory gates must prevent acceptance, use, or recognition as applicable.
- Quarantine controls physical risk while the accounting and legal assessments continue.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory
8. Q: What records should support the physical transfer and receipt of donated goods?
A: Show answer
The receipt file should link the approved offer, donor and transferor information, authorized transfer terms, dispatch record, transport documentation, delivery record, and inspection results. Record the actual receiving date and time, receiving location, person delivering the goods, recipient, shipment reference, item identifiers, quantity and unit, serial or lot numbers, expiration dates where relevant, physical condition, and discrepancies. Identify quantities that were offered, dispatched, physically received, inspected, accepted, quarantined, and refused separately. If goods are received in stages, record each delivery rather than reporting the entire offer as completed. An independent checker should verify significant or higher-risk receipts where practicable. If the donor document states a quantity of 100 but only 92 units arrive, the receipt record should reflect 92 units and the remaining eight as outstanding or disputed. Any subsequent adjustment should retain the original information and an explanation of the correction.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile a sample dispatch note to the actual receiving report, including a partial shipment, damaged goods, and a quantity discrepancy."

Key point:
- Physical records must reflect the goods actually received and inspected.
- Partial receipts and discrepancies require explicit status and follow-up.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory
9. Q: How should the organization determine whether it has legal title, accounting control, another enforceable right, or merely physical custody?
A: Show answer
Review the actual transfer instrument, applicable law, contractual terms, practical rights, and relevant circumstances. Legal title and accounting control can be related but must not be treated as identical without analysis. A transfer may convey ownership, provide a limited right to use an asset, create a loan or consignment arrangement, or permit temporary custody while another party retains control. Examine who can direct the asset's use, who receives its service potential or benefits, whether access can be restricted, who bears relevant obligations, whether the transfer can be revoked, and whether there are return or onward-transfer clauses. A goods-received note proves physical receipt, not necessarily title or control. If the evidence does not support a conclusion, record the status as unresolved and refer it to Legal and the accounting policy owner. Maintain custody records while the accounting assessment remains open, so the item is safeguarded without being prematurely represented as an owned asset.

Study instruction:
- "Compare an outright gift, a loan, a consignment, and a custody-only arrangement using their agreements and identify the rights and obligations in each case."

Key point:
- Physical possession does not automatically establish ownership or accounting recognition.
- Unresolved rights must be documented and escalated before the relevant accounting decision is finalized.

Source links:
- IPSASB — Revenue
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 7 — Leases
10. Q: How should an organization decide whether donated goods meet the criteria for accounting recognition?
A: Show answer
First identify the applicable accounting framework and reporting period. Then assess the actual rights acquired, the existence of an asset or other resource under the applicable standard, the probability or other recognition conditions required by that framework, and whether the monetary measurement requirements can be satisfied. Determine whether the item is property, plant and equipment, inventory, an intangible asset, a right-to-use arrangement, a service, or an arrangement that does not qualify for recognition as an asset. The conditions for recognizing an asset and those for recognizing the related contribution or revenue must be assessed together but need not always produce the same conclusion or timing. A physical receipt record is important evidence but does not settle the full accounting question. The decision should cite the applicable standard, effective date, relevant contractual facts, measurement basis, and reviewer approval. If recognition is unresolved, retain the relevant custody and disclosure analysis without inventing a journal entry.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare an accounting recognition memorandum for one donated equipment item, citing the current applicable framework and identifying the rights, recognition criteria, measurement evidence, and reviewer approval."

Key point:
- Recognition depends on the applicable standard and actual facts, not on the label used by the donor.
- Record the recognition decision and supporting evidence before posting an accounting entry.

Source links:
- IPSASB — Revenue
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
11. Q: How should donated property, plant and equipment be distinguished from inventory and consumable supplies?
A: Show answer
Classification should reflect the nature of the item, the way the organization controls and uses it, the relevant recognition criteria, the applicable capitalization or measurement policy, and any specific standard requirements. Property, plant and equipment generally concerns tangible items held for use over more than one reporting period, subject to the applicable framework. Inventory can include goods held for distribution, consumption, or other purposes within the relevant inventory definition. Consumables may form part of inventory before issue or consumption, depending on the organization's systems and policies. An item being expensive or durable does not alone guarantee capitalization; conversely, donor financing does not itself require an otherwise recognizable asset to be excluded from the register. Use an approved item-classification matrix and maintain links between the physical record, inventory or fixed-asset register, accounting decision, and supporting documents. Verify capitalization thresholds and treatment under the actual entity's current policy rather than importing UN-specific thresholds into an Indonesian NGO's accounts.

Study instruction:
- "Classify donated laptops, stationery, medicines for distribution, furniture, and a temporary equipment loan under the organization's approved accounting policy, explaining the evidence for each classification."

Key point:
- Donated status does not replace the normal classification analysis.
- Item registers and accounting classifications must agree without treating custody-only items as owned assets.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory
- IAI — Indonesian Financial Accounting Standards
12. Q: How should the organization measure donated goods when accounting recognition requires a monetary value?
A: Show answer
Determine the measurement basis required by the applicable accounting framework before choosing a valuation method. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual describes fair-value measurement at the acquisition date for donated goods and property, plant and equipment under the UN's relevant policies. If those requirements are applicable, use evidence appropriate to the asset and the date of acquisition, such as observable market prices for comparable items or an independent appraisal where necessary. Record the item description, accepted quantity, unit of measure, condition, location, currency, measurement date, methodology, comparable evidence, assumptions, uncertainties, and reviewer approval. Adjust the analysis for relevant differences in condition, model, age, functionality, market, location, and restrictions where the selected methodology requires it. A valuation must reflect the measurement objective of the applicable standard; it should not simply copy an amount from a donor letter. If reliable measurement is not possible, document the uncertainty and apply the applicable standard's recognition, measurement, or disclosure requirements rather than inventing a value.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a valuation worksheet using comparable market evidence, documented item condition, measurement date, and an independent review for a donated equipment item."

Key point:
- Measurement follows the accounting framework's requirements and the relevant acquisition or recognition date.
- Valuation methodology, assumptions, evidence, and review must be reproducible.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10
- UN IPSAS Corporate Guidance — Inventory Measurement
13. Q: Why should a donor's declared value not automatically become the accounting value of a donation?
A: Show answer
A donor's declared amount may represent historical purchase cost, replacement cost, an insurance figure, a customs value, a suggested donation value, or an estimate made for a different purpose. These measures are not necessarily equivalent to the monetary measurement required for financial reporting. The organization should retain the donor's statement as evidence but independently assess whether it supports the required measurement basis. For example, a donor may state that an old laptop originally cost IDR 15 million. That historical cost does not establish its current value when the laptop is transferred, especially if it has been used, damaged, or superseded. Obtain comparable evidence, consider condition and relevant valuation factors, document uncertainty, and secure independent review where materiality or risk warrants it. Tax, customs, insurance, replacement, and accounting values may need to be stored separately and linked to their respective purposes. The organization should never overwrite one measurement with another without preserving the basis and history.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a donor's original purchase invoice with current comparable prices and explain why the figures may serve different measurement purposes."

Key point:
- Donor-stated value is evidence, not automatic accounting measurement.
- Distinct legal, tax, insurance, and accounting valuations require distinct purposes and supporting records.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8
- UN IPSAS Corporate Guidance — Inventory Measurement
- IPSASB — Measurement
14. Q: How should donated services and donated rights to use property be treated differently from donated tangible goods?
A: Show answer
A donated service involves work performed for the organization, whereas a donated right-to-use arrangement provides a contractual or other enforceable right to use an asset. Neither should automatically be treated as an outright transfer of a physical asset. For donated services, identify the service actually received, the period, the nature of the contribution, and the requirements of the applicable reporting framework. The UN Finance Manual has historically described a UN policy under which in-kind services are not recognized as revenue but material services are disclosed; that specific treatment must not be generalized to every organization without checking the current applicable standard and policy. For a donated right to use property, examine the arrangement's term, enforceable rights, obligations, and applicable lease or revenue guidance. The accounting conclusion should address whether the arrangement creates a recognizable right or asset, a liability, revenue, expense, or disclosure requirement as applicable. Keep service records, use-right documentation, and physical asset records distinct.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a donated consultant service, a gift of equipment, and free use of office space under the applicable framework and identify the evidence and accounting assessment needed for each."

Key point:
- Services, goods, and rights to use assets may require different accounting and disclosure analysis.
- Historical UN-specific policies must be checked against current standards and the entity's own reporting basis.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 7 — Leases
- IPSASB — Revenue
15. Q: How should an organization distinguish donor restrictions from conditions attached to a donation?
A: Show answer
Read the transfer agreement and applicable standard carefully. A restriction may limit the purpose, location, recipient, period, publicity, or other permitted use of the donation without necessarily creating a liability under the governing accounting framework. A condition may have different accounting consequences when the applicable requirements for a binding performance obligation and enforceable return obligation are satisfied. The precise distinction depends on the applicable standard and the actual agreement; the word "restricted" in a donor document does not settle the accounting result by itself. Translate relevant clauses into specific operational requirements, including eligible activity, quantity, location, recipient, use period, consent requirements, reporting duties, and return or disposal rules. The Grants or donor-relationship owner should interpret contractual obligations with Legal where needed, while Finance determines accounting consequences under the applicable framework. Any unresolved interpretation must be documented and referred to an authorized reviewer before the organization approves a potentially inconsistent use.

Study instruction:
- "Review two hypothetical donor agreements and identify whether each clause creates a use restriction, an accounting-relevant condition, a return obligation, or an unresolved legal question."

Key point:
- Contractual restrictions and accounting conditions are related but are not automatically equivalent.
- Accounting conclusions must be grounded in the current applicable standard and the actual enforceable terms.

Source links:
- IPSASB — Revenue
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3
16. Q: What should a donation agreement and restriction register record to support compliant use?
A: Show answer
The register should identify the donation, donor, executed instrument and amendments, effective dates, controlling clauses, permitted purposes, eligible recipients, geographical or location limits, permitted quantities, use period, reporting requirements, publicity obligations, consent requirements, and return or disposal provisions. Link each requirement to the item or batch identifiers, operational owner, evidence required, approval route, and review date. The system should block or flag a proposed movement or distribution when it conflicts with a current restriction or lacks required prior consent. If the agreement is unclear, the responsible owner must seek an authorized interpretation instead of creating an informal rule. Amendments must be recorded as new versions, preserving the terms that applied to past transactions. At each reporting period, reconcile compliance with the restrictions against actual use, recipient acknowledgments, distributions, and other required evidence. A clean accounting record does not by itself demonstrate that donor conditions have been met operationally.

Study instruction:
- "Convert selected clauses from a sample donor agreement into structured register fields and test whether an unauthorized destination or recipient triggers a hold."

Key point:
- Restriction registers convert contractual obligations into operational controls.
- Prior approvals, amendments, movements, and actual use must remain traceable.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Accounting Manual
17. Q: What Indonesian tax and customs checks are necessary before an NGO accepts a donation?
A: Show answer
The organization should distinguish accounting recognition from tax and customs treatment. For Indonesian income tax, assess the current applicable rules for assistance, donations, gifts, donor identity, recipient status, transaction facts, and required supporting documents. PMK No. 114 of 2025 is an important current official source governing specified income-tax treatment of assistance, contributions, and gifts; it took effect on 31 December 2025 and revoked certain earlier provisions. Eligibility must be assessed against the operative text rather than assumed from the recipient's nonprofit status or the donor's charitable purpose. For imported goods, assess the importer, commodity, shipment route, end use, documentation, and any applicable permits or facilities. PMK No. 99 of 2025 governs specified customs and excise relief for certain gift or donation shipments and took effect on 27 February 2026. It is not a blanket exemption for all NGO imports. The responsible tax or customs specialist should document the applicable provisions, eligibility, submission evidence, clearance status, and unresolved obligations before the goods are released for use.

Study instruction:
- "Assess a hypothetical imported equipment donation by separating income-tax questions from customs eligibility, import permits, accounting measurement, and donor restrictions."

Key point:
- Donation status does not automatically establish tax exemption or customs relief.
- Apply current Indonesian rules to the actual donor, item, shipment, recipient, and intended use.

Source links:
- PMK No. 114 of 2025 — Official Indonesian Legal Database
- PMK No. 99 of 2025 — Ministry of Finance Legal Information System
18. Q: How should product safety, health, environmental, safeguarding, and other specialist risks be controlled for donated goods?
A: Show answer
Specialist review must be proportional to the item's nature and the consequences of failure. Medical products may require review of authorization, storage temperature, batch, expiration, and traceability. Electrical equipment may require technical testing and compatible power specifications. Chemicals, batteries, laboratory equipment, and other regulated goods may require specialist handling, storage, environmental controls, or permits. Personal data contained on donated computers must be protected and securely assessed before access or reuse. Safety-sensitive equipment may require qualified inspection, user training, and written clearance. Customs release does not automatically prove technical safety, and accounting recognition does not authorize use. The responsible specialist should document the requirement, evidence reviewed, restrictions, clearance decision, effective date, and any conditions for release. Goods must remain quarantined or otherwise restricted while a mandatory clearance is outstanding, with an identified owner and escalation deadline. The SOP should prevent acceptance pressure or operational urgency from overriding non-waivable safety and legal gates.

Study instruction:
- "Create specialist review checklists for donated medicines, electrical equipment, and used computers, identifying which deficiencies require quarantine or refusal."

Key point:
- Legal, safety, privacy, and environmental controls remain independent of accounting approval.
- Release for use requires the relevant competent clearance and supporting evidence.

Source links:
- PMK No. 99 of 2025 — Customs and Excise Relief
19. Q: How should responsibilities and segregation of duties be designed for the donation process?
A: Show answer
Define the responsibilities of the offer originator, donation coordinator, program owner, donor or grant specialist, Legal, Tax, Customs, Safety, receiving custodian, valuation preparer, accounting decision-maker, journal preparer, independent reviewer, approver, system implementer, reporting owner, and independent tester. The originator should not be the sole acceptance approver. A valuation preparer should not be the sole approver of their own material valuation, and a custodian should not solely approve their own inventory adjustments, loss, or disposal. The person implementing an approved system configuration must not independently authorize accounting substance or privileged access. Internal Audit may advise or test controls but should not assume management responsibility for the same control it later audits. Each delegation must identify the authorized individual or role, organizational scope, value limits, effective dates, and revocation status. Where staffing constraints prevent full segregation, establish documented compensating controls, such as independent approval, after-the-event review, and periodic reconciliation by a different person.

Study instruction:
- "Create a responsibility matrix for intake, acceptance, valuation, receipt, accounting, journal approval, distribution, and disposal, then identify incompatible duties."

Key point:
- No single person should control every critical stage of a material donation.
- Compensating controls must be documented and tested when full separation is impracticable.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
20. Q: What workflow stages and decision gates should be used to manage a donation from intake to closure?
A: Show answer
Use controlled statuses such as INTAKE_REGISTERED, DUE_DILIGENCE_IN_PROGRESS, ACCEPTANCE_PENDING, ACCEPTED, TRANSFER_PENDING, PARTIAL_RECEIPT, RECEIPT_INSPECTED, QUARANTINED, TITLE_CONTROL_UNRESOLVED, VALUATION_PENDING, RECOGNITION_HOLD, APPROVED_FOR_USE, DISTRIBUTED, INCIDENT_OPEN, DISPOSAL_PENDING, RETURN_PENDING, and CLOSED. These are illustrative status names and must be adapted to the organization's actual system. Define valid transitions, preconditions, authorized roles, required evidence, failure routes, and terminal-state criteria. For example, a status should not move from receipt inspection to approved-for-use while a required safety clearance or donor consent remains outstanding. Closing a record must require evidence of the actual terminal event, completion of required accounting and reporting work, resolution of mandatory exceptions, and independent verification where needed. Retain failed transitions and rejected requests in the audit trail rather than deleting them. Workflow controls should help staff act consistently, not allow a status label to become a substitute for proof of the underlying event.

Study instruction:
- "Draw a status transition table for an equipment donation and test whether missing acceptance, valuation, permit, and disposal approvals correctly prevent progression."

Key point:
- Every workflow transition requires defined evidence and authorized responsibility.
- A terminal status requires proof that the real event and closure conditions are complete.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Accounting Manual
21. Q: What should a donated asset or inventory register contain?
A: Show answer
Maintain a stable, uniquely identifiable record linking the donation intake, donor, transfer document, actual receipt, item or batch, title and control assessment, valuation, accounting decision, restrictions, location, custodian, asset or inventory classification, movements, incidents, and disposal history. Include an effective status, quantity, unit of measure, condition, serial number or lot where relevant, expiration date where relevant, location, and links to supporting documents. The register should distinguish physical custody from accounting ownership and recognize that some offers, quarantined goods, loans, consignments, or rejected items may require operational records without being recognized assets. Asset tags and serial numbers assist identification but do not prove title, control, or accounting recognition. Use consistent identifiers across warehouse, inventory, fixed assets, project coding, donor reporting, and the general ledger. A change of location or custodian should create a traceable event rather than erase the earlier record.

Study instruction:
- "Design a register row for a donated asset showing unique ID, source evidence, control status, valuation basis, restriction, location, custodian, and accounting reference."

Key point:
- A controlled register connects physical items, accounting decisions, and supporting evidence.
- Tags and register entries are control tools, not substitutes for legal or accounting evidence.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory
22. Q: How should the organization control storage, movements, distribution, and custody changes for donated items?
A: Show answer
Set written procedures for authorized receipt locations, secure storage, custody assignment, inventory issues, transfers between locations, beneficiary distributions, and handovers. Each movement should identify the item or batch, quantity and unit, source and destination, date, purpose, authorized initiator and approver, person handing over, recipient, and acknowledgment evidence. Validate each proposed issue or movement against current donor restrictions, approved quantities, intended recipient, period, and any applicable permits or consent requirements. Maintain independent count checks where the item's value or risk warrants them. For goods held in multiple locations, reconcile transfer-out and transfer-in records so that an item cannot appear simultaneously available at two sites. Do not change the custodian, location, or quantity merely to resolve a system mismatch without investigation and approval. When discrepancies are found, preserve the prior state, secure the goods, investigate the cause, and document any approved adjustment separately.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a sample distribution from the inventory record through the authorization, stock issue, transport record, and signed recipient acknowledgment."

Key point:
- Movement records protect traceability from receipt to final recipient.
- Donor restrictions and independent checks must continue after accounting recognition.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory
23. Q: How should accounting entries for donated goods be prepared and approved?
A: Show answer
The accounting entry should be based on the approved recognition and measurement decision and the actual transaction facts. If the applicable framework requires recognition of the donated asset and a corresponding contribution or revenue, determine the correct accounts and timing under that framework, including any liability or other treatment required by the transfer terms. Determine whether the donation is inventory, property, plant and equipment, an intangible asset, or another type of resource. Verify the measurement basis, recognition date, debit and credit amounts, currency, accounting period, project and donor dimensions, and supporting-document references. The journal preparer should use the approved accounting memorandum and not select treatment solely from an Excel formula or a donor's requested account. An independent reviewer must check material entries and ensure that the approved journal agrees with the supporting evidence. Distinguish a draft journal from a journal that is actually posted; only the official accounting-system reference and status prove posting. Reconcile the resulting balance and any associated contribution or liability to the relevant registers and reporting schedules.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a sample journal workflow for donated equipment, showing the approved accounting basis, supporting evidence, reviewer sign-off, posting reference, and reconciliation."

Key point:
- Journal entries must follow the approved accounting decision and current applicable standard.
- A prepared journal is not proof of posting or proof that the physical and legal controls are complete.

Source links:
- IPSASB — Revenue
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
24. Q: How should donor-funded assets be handled when the organization receives them for a particular project or grant?
A: Show answer
Identify whether the organization or another party controls the asset, what the grant or transfer agreement requires, and whether the applicable framework's asset and contribution recognition criteria are met. Link the donation to its project, donor, authorized purpose, restriction, receipt event, valuation record, and asset or inventory identifier. The project code should explain where the resource is used or reported without overriding the accounting classification. Confirm whether the donor agreement restricts the period of use, recipient, location, transfer, or final disposal and whether prior approval is required for onward transfer. Asset ownership and donor funding are different dimensions: an item may be donor-funded while still requiring independent analysis of title, control, recognition, and reporting. At grant closure, determine whether the asset remains controlled by the organization, must be handed over to a beneficiary, must be returned to the donor, or may be retained under the agreement. Record the approved decision and the evidence of the actual transfer or retention.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one donor-funded item from the grant agreement through receipt, project coding, asset-register linkage, donor reporting, and the grant-closure decision."

Key point:
- Grant coding and donor restrictions complement but do not replace accounting recognition analysis.
- End-of-project asset transfers require evidence of authority, consent where applicable, and actual handover.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3
25. Q: How should maintenance, depreciation, impairment, expiry, damage, and other lifecycle events be managed after a donation is accepted?
A: Show answer
Acceptance is the beginning of the controlled lifecycle, not the end of the process. For recognized depreciable assets, apply the useful-life, depreciation, residual-value, component, and timing requirements of the applicable accounting framework and approved policy. For inventory and consumables, manage storage conditions, expiry, quality, physical verification, and any required write-down or consumption recognition. Monitor maintenance, insurance, obsolescence, damage, safety, and donor-specific usage requirements according to risk. If an item is damaged, lost, stolen, contaminated, expired, or otherwise impaired, protect people and remaining assets, restrict movement or use where needed, preserve evidence, notify the appropriate responsible teams, assess the accounting and legal consequences, and obtain required approvals. Do not change a valuation, depreciation record, or asset status without retaining the original basis and the approved change. The asset register, general ledger, incident log, and relevant donor report must reflect approved events consistently.

Study instruction:
- "Follow a donated equipment item through a periodic condition check, depreciation review, damage incident, corrective action, and independent verification."

Key point:
- Donated assets require ongoing operational and accounting control throughout their useful lifecycle.
- Damage, loss, impairment, and expiry must be supported by evidence and approved treatment.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10
26. Q: What controls should apply before a donated asset is returned, sold, transferred to a beneficiary, written off, or disposed of?
A: Show answer
Before the proposed action, establish the organization's rights, donor restrictions, contractual return obligations, and any required consents, permits, or approvals. Assess whether the proposed recipient or buyer is eligible and legitimate, whether the transaction creates conflicts of interest, and whether safety or environmental rules govern the method of transfer or destruction. The approval should identify the asset, quantity, method, destination, expected proceeds or recovery where relevant, timing, responsible individuals, and applicable conditions. For a sale, retain the sale documentation, proceeds evidence, and accounting assessment. For a return, retain the donor's authorization and acknowledgment of receipt. For a transfer to beneficiaries, retain the authority to transfer and evidence of handover. For a write-off or disposal, document the reason, physical status, required permissions, and the approved accounting treatment. Update the asset register, inventory, custody records, general ledger, and donor reporting only after the actual event is evidenced and reconciled. An asset tag should not be removed or a record closed merely because a disposal request has been approved.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a disposal authorization pack for an obsolete donor-funded laptop and identify the evidence needed before approval and after the physical disposal."

Key point:
- Disposal and transfer require authority, required consent, event evidence, and accounting updates.
- Approval to act and proof that the action occurred are separate control requirements.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10
27. Q: How should the organization reconcile donation records, physical inventory, the asset register, the general ledger, and donor reports?
A: Show answer
Reconciliation must compare the relevant populations and totals across the full donation lifecycle. Begin with offers and approved transfers, then reconcile quantities dispatched, actually received, accepted, quarantined, distributed, returned, and held in custody. Reconcile recognized items to the appropriate inventory or fixed-asset register and to the related accounting balances or contribution records required by the applicable framework. Verify that donor-reporting quantities and values are consistent with the agreement's reporting requirements and the underlying evidence. Differences can arise from partial shipments, timing, unposted journals, duplicate records, classification differences, valuation revisions, returns, or unapproved movements. Each difference should have an identified cause, value or quantity, responsible owner, deadline, corrective action, and closure evidence. Independently recalculate totals and inspect material exceptions instead of relying solely on system-generated balances. Unexplained material differences should be resolved or escalated before final reports are approved.

Study instruction:
- "Perform a three-way reconciliation among the physical count, item register, and accounting records, then document each difference and approved correction."

Key point:
- Reconciliation links the physical, contractual, accounting, and reporting records.
- Differences must be explained with evidence, not eliminated by unsupported adjustments.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
28. Q: What information should be disclosed or reported about in-kind contributions at month-end and year-end?
A: Show answer
The required reporting depends on the applicable framework, materiality requirements, donor agreement, and organizational policy. Relevant information may include the nature of material contributions, quantities received and distributed, amounts recognized, measurement methods and uncertainties, movements in asset or inventory balances, unused or returned items, restrictions, outstanding conditions, significant incidents, and unresolved control issues. Distinguish amounts recognized in financial statements from memorandum tracking amounts and donor-reporting values used for a different purpose. The UN Finance Manual documents UN-specific accounting and disclosure policies for in-kind goods and services; its rules and thresholds must not automatically be adopted by an organization using another framework or a later version of IPSAS. The reporting owner should reconcile reported amounts and quantities to approved workpapers, the general ledger, item registers, and supporting documents. Reports should clearly identify the reporting period, valuation basis, status date, reviewer, and material unresolved matters. Do not describe a draft or unsupported figure as a final recognized amount.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a month-end reporting schedule showing received, recognized, distributed, returned, and outstanding donation quantities, with amounts reconciled to the applicable financial records."

Key point:
- Presentation and disclosure depend on the governing framework and materiality.
- Management and donor reports must distinguish accounting amounts from other reporting measures.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3

29. Q: How should the organization investigate a donation with missing documents, quantity discrepancies, or uncertain ownership?
A: Show answer
Open a formal exception record that identifies the donation and item, the rule not satisfied, missing or conflicting evidence, known quantity or value impact, severity, immediate containment, responsible owner, and deadline. Preserve original documents, photographs, inspection results, correspondence, and system records. Contact the donor or other relevant parties through an authorized channel to obtain verifiable evidence. Obtain independent physical counts or specialist inspections where necessary. The accounting owner should determine whether recognition, valuation, reporting, or an existing journal is affected. Legal should assess disputed title or transfer authority, while the relevant safety or customs specialist addresses any related legal clearance. Do not resolve a discrepancy solely by changing the recorded quantity or by relying on a verbal assurance. If the evidence remains insufficient, maintain the appropriate hold, custody classification, or unresolved status and escalate according to risk and materiality. Close the exception only when the decision, corrective action, review, and supporting evidence are complete.

Study instruction:
- "Investigate a hypothetical receipt with an incomplete transfer agreement and an unexplained shortage, recording containment, evidence requests, accounting impact, escalation, and closure conditions."

Key point:
- An exception requires an owner, deadline, evidence, and controlled resolution.
- Unsupported accounting or inventory adjustments cannot replace investigation.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
30. Q: How should the donation process maintain an audit trail, record retention, privacy, and controlled access?
A: Show answer
Assign immutable identifiers to intake records, items, receipt events, valuation decisions, approvals, movements, journal references, exceptions, and lifecycle changes. Record timestamps, responsible users, source documents, previous and new values, reasons for changes, approval references, and test or verification results where relevant. Retain original transfer documents and superseded approvals rather than replacing them with the newest version. Establish a retention schedule based on applicable law, donor requirements, contracts, audit obligations, and approved organizational records policy. Limit access to donor information, beneficiary data, personal identifiers, security information, and financial documents according to role and legitimate need. Use approved storage and backup arrangements, and periodically test recovery of archived evidence. Do not store passwords, authentication tokens, or other confidential credentials in shared workbooks. A record should remain traceable even after the associated item has been distributed, transferred, returned, written off, or disposed of.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a donation record from intake to final disposal and verify that original evidence, changes, approvals, access controls, and retention requirements remain demonstrable."

Key point:
- A reliable audit trail preserves both the current status and the history behind it.
- Retention, privacy, access, and recovery are ongoing control responsibilities.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
31. Q: How can Excel, Power Query, and VBA support donation accounting and control without replacing professional judgment?
A: Show answer
Excel Tables can provide structured item, donor, valuation, custody, and exception registers. Power Query can combine approved source files, standardize incoming columns, normalize identifiers, detect candidate duplicates, and prepare reconciliation populations. Formulas can calculate quantities, extensions, and approved measurement calculations when the underlying methodology has been authorized. VBA can assist with report preparation, controlled PDF export, validation checks, workflow notifications, and audit-log entries if those functions are designed and tested appropriately. However, automation cannot establish legal title, determine whether a donor's terms are enforceable, select an unapproved accounting framework, or grant safety or customs clearance. Every output should identify its source data, refresh time, calculation or rule version, unresolved exceptions, and review status. Material formula or code changes require authorization and appropriate regression testing. Validate automated results against independently prepared samples, and retain evidence of actual system posting rather than treating a generated journal as a completed accounting event.

Study instruction:
- "Design a structured donation register and Power Query reconciliation, then test duplicate detection, incomplete source data, incorrect units, and unresolved approval gates."

Key point:
- Automation improves consistency and traceability but does not determine legal or accounting conclusions.
- Rules, calculations, outputs, and changes must be controlled and independently testable.

- Microsoft Learn — What Is Power Query?
- Microsoft Learn — Excel VBA Reference
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory
32. Q: How should document versions and changes to donation procedures be approved and tested?
A: Show answer
Maintain a controlled register showing the document or configuration identifier, version, owner, reviewer, approver, approval date, effective date, reason for change, affected processes, and required testing. Changes may be triggered by new legislation, a revised accounting standard, donor-agreement amendments, new item categories, incidents, audit findings, or changes to the organization's operating model. The change owner must determine which policies, forms, registers, formulas, training materials, and reporting outputs are affected. Test relevant positive and negative scenarios before implementation, especially where a change can permit acceptance, recognition, movement, release, or disposal that was previously blocked. Ensure the production version can be distinguished from a draft or test version. If a change affects the evidence supporting an earlier approval, re-evaluate whether that approval remains valid. Archive superseded versions so decisions under earlier requirements can be explained without applying the newest procedure retrospectively.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate a change to the valuation process and document the impact assessment, revised procedure, approval, test evidence, communication, and effective date."

Key point:
- Procedural and system changes require controlled approval and appropriate retesting.
- Historical decisions and prior versions must remain traceable.

- UN IPSAS Accounting Manual
33. Q: What key performance indicators should management use to monitor the effectiveness of donated asset controls?
A: Show answer
Select indicators that measure both timeliness and control quality. Useful measures may include the proportion of donations with complete due diligence before acceptance, the proportion of receipts independently checked where required, the number and age of unresolved title or control questions, valuation approvals outstanding, items without current location or custodian information, expired permits or restrictions, inventory discrepancies, unacknowledged distributions, unresolved accounting reconciliations, overdue incidents, and disposal records awaiting closure. Each indicator needs a defined population, calculation, source, reporting frequency, owner, target, and escalation threshold. For example, the percentage of complete records should use all relevant records as its denominator, not just those already cleared. Separate the number of open exceptions from the age and severity of those exceptions, because a small number of critical issues can carry greater risk than numerous minor administrative delays. Validate dashboard statuses against actual source evidence and management-approved definitions before reporting results.

Study instruction:
- "Build a control dashboard with defined denominators for due diligence, record completeness, valuation approval, reconciliation, and overdue exceptions, then validate it against a sample of source records."

Key point:
- KPIs should expose control failures and support specific corrective actions.
- Dashboard results must be tied to reliable populations and evidence.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16
34. Q: How should management test whether the donation SOP operates effectively rather than merely appearing complete on paper?
A: Show answer
Perform an end-to-end walkthrough of a completed donation and test the underlying evidence at each important stage. Verify the donor and transfer authority, due diligence, program need, acceptance approval, shipping and receipt records, quantity and condition inspection, title and control analysis, valuation, accounting decision, journal posting, restriction compliance, custody and movements, reporting, and final archive. Select both routine and higher-risk cases and test whether actual practice follows the documented procedure. Check whether the recorded quantities and amounts reconcile across systems. Where practical, include negative tests that deliberately omit mandatory evidence, use an invalid approval, introduce a duplicate record, exceed an authorization limit, or propose a restricted movement. The expected result should be a hold, refusal, or escalation, as appropriate. Document failures, root causes, corrective actions, owners, deadlines, and retest evidence. Do not claim that an automated test, a parsed document, or a sample walkthrough proves all controls are effective across all transactions.

Study instruction:
- "Select a completed donation and carry out a documented walkthrough, then test at least three negative scenarios that should block acceptance, accounting recognition, or disposal."

Key point:
- A written procedure must be tested against actual records and real workflow behavior.
- Negative testing is essential for confirming that mandatory gates cannot be bypassed.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
35. Q: What should happen when a donated asset must be transferred to an implementing partner or end beneficiary at project closure?
A: Show answer
First establish the current title and control position, the applicable donor or transfer agreement, the conditions governing retention or onward transfer, and any required consent or legal approval. Assess whether the asset remains controlled by the organization under the applicable accounting framework or whether control is to pass to another party. Identify the exact recipient, items, quantities, condition, transfer date, purpose, location, and documentation required. Secure the competent approval before acting and confirm any safety, customs, or other compliance requirements. Record the handover using a signed acknowledgment or other appropriate evidence, update custody and asset records, and assess the required accounting derecognition, disposal, or transfer treatment. Reconcile the change to the general ledger and donor reporting where relevant. The asset must not be treated as transferred merely because a project has ended or the asset was physically moved; the actual rights, terms, approvals, and handover evidence must support closure.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a project-closure handover file for donor-funded equipment, showing the transfer authority, recipient acknowledgment, accounting decision, and register updates."

Key point:
- Project closure does not itself authorize an asset transfer or establish that accounting control has ended.
- Actual handover, consent, and accounting updates must be linked and evidenced.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8
36. Q: How should an organization handle a donation consisting of multiple items with different conditions, values, restrictions, or accounting classifications?
A: Show answer
Do not assume that a single donation agreement requires every component to receive the same accounting classification, valuation, or operational treatment. Identify each materially distinct item, batch, or component and assess whether it needs its own record or an appropriate grouped record under the applicable policy. For example, a shipment containing reusable computers, consumable accessories, and damaged units may require different custody, inspection, classification, valuation, and disposition decisions. Allocate a total donor-stated amount only when the allocation methodology is justified for the relevant purpose. Preserve the link between individual items and the overall transfer instrument, and identify any components that are accepted, quarantined, refused, returned, or left unresolved. Apply restrictions and safety clearances to the correct item population rather than to the shipment as a whole when they differ. Reconcile item-level records to the received quantity and any aggregate reporting totals so exclusions and partial acceptance remain visible.

Study instruction:
- "Break down a mixed equipment shipment into distinct items and demonstrate separate inspection, valuation, restriction, and accounting decisions while reconciling to the shipment total."

Key point:
- A single donation may contain several distinct operational and accounting populations.
- Item-level decisions must reconcile to the overall transfer record without concealing exceptions.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10
37. Q: How should the organization respond when it receives donated equipment that is usable but has incomplete ownership or valuation evidence?
A: Show answer
Separate the immediate safeguarding decision from the unresolved accounting and legal decisions. Inspect the item and secure it in an authorized location, documenting its condition, serial number, quantity, receipt date, and source. If required ownership, transfer authority, or safety evidence remains incomplete, do not represent the item as fully cleared merely because it is physically usable. Assign an owner and deadline for obtaining a valid transfer document or independent clarification. Finance should assess whether the current facts satisfy the applicable framework's recognition and measurement requirements. Where the value cannot be measured reliably under the applicable standard, document the limitations and determine the appropriate hold, measurement, or disclosure treatment. Do not create an unsupported accounting amount to keep the month-end process moving. Any authorized release or subsequent recognition must follow the completed assessment, review, and approval. Maintain the original intake and receipt records so the delay and eventual resolution remain auditable.

Study instruction:
- "Work through a scenario with physically received equipment, incomplete title evidence, and no reliable market comparable, identifying what may proceed and what must remain on hold."

Key point:
- Operational safeguarding can continue while unresolved accounting and legal questions are escalated.
- Usability alone does not establish ownership, recognition, or a supportable value.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3
38. Q: How should an organization correct a donation record after discovering an error in quantity, classification, valuation, or accounting treatment?
A: Show answer
Identify the affected donation and item, preserve the original record, and establish the cause and impact of the error. Determine whether it affects physical inventory, title or control, valuation, asset classification, accounting recognition, depreciation or consumption, donor restrictions, tax or customs records, financial statements, or reports already distributed. Prepare a documented correction proposal with the original value, corrected value, reason, evidence, effective date, linked transaction, and relevant approvals. The appropriate specialist should review any change affecting legal rights, safety, tax, customs, or accounting treatment. Correct the underlying source or register through a controlled amendment rather than deleting the historical record. Assess whether a journal reversal, replacement entry, corrected report, revised donor submission, or formal disclosure is necessary under the applicable requirements. Independently verify the updated records and close the exception only after all affected registers, reports, and reconciliations agree.

Study instruction:
- "Investigate a hypothetical donation entered at the wrong quantity and classification, then document the correction from original evidence through approved journal, updated register, and reconciled reporting."

Key point:
- Correct errors through an evidenced, approved, and traceable adjustment process.
- Preserve the original transaction history and assess every affected record and report.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
39. Q: What documentation is required before an in-kind donation SOP can be formally adopted by an actual organization?
A: Show answer
Replace all fictional entities, records, amounts, dates, approvals, and assumptions with verified organizational facts and controlled evidence. Confirm the organization's legal form and jurisdiction, applicable accounting framework, current legal and tax requirements, import and customs rules, relevant donor agreements, and approved internal policies. Assign the policy owner, independent reviewer, authorized approver, effective date, review date, and document version. Validate every cited source, including its applicability, effective period, and relevant section. Check that responsibilities, workflow steps, forms, register fields, exception procedures, test cases, and reporting requirements agree with the actual operating model and current systems. Test the procedures using representative real-world scenarios and suitable negative tests before release. If independent legal, technical, or accounting validation remains incomplete, mark the affected decision and the adoption itself as unverified or on hold. Approval should only occur through the organization's valid governance process; a fictional approval reference or technically complete document is not evidence of actual authorization.

Study instruction:
- "Create an adoption checklist covering verified entity facts, current sources, actual delegations, controlled forms, end-to-end tests, independent review, and authorization."

Key point:
- A demonstration SOP must not be represented as approved operational policy until the required adoption gates have been completed.
- Source verification, real-entity applicability, independent testing, and authorized approval are separate requirements.
